Just as for non-relative uniform K-homology we shall find it useful to be able to represent relative uniform K-homology classes by finite-propagation operators. Recall from the discussion surrounding (
4
) that if
X
has jointly bounded geometry
25
25

25

Again, coarsely bounded geometry, i.e. the existence of a quasi-lattice in
X
would actually suffice here. However, jointly bounded geometry, is essentially a standing assumption for the more non-trivial applications of (relative) uniform K-homology, and it certainly holds for manifolds of bounded geometry, so we will not worry too much about only making this weaker assumption here.
, then it admits a uniformly locally finite partition of unity
\{\psi_{i}\}
with supports of uniformly bounded diameters such that
\sqrt{\psi_{i}}
is uniformly Lipschitz. Just as in the non-relative case truncation using the
\psi_{i}
produces finite-propagation representatives for relative uniform K-homology classes.

Lemma 2.25
.

Let
X
be a metric space of jointly bounded geometry, and
Z
a closed subspace. If
(H,\rho,F)
is a relative uniform Fredholm module over
(X,Z)
, then so is

\left(H,\rho,\mathrm{trunc}(F)=\sum_{i}\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})F\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})\right)\;,

and the two differ only by a uniformly compact perturbation. In particular, any class in
K^{u}_{*}(X,Z)
has a finite-propagation representative.

Proof.

The argument is completely analogous to that in the non-relative case, see Proposition 7.4 of
[
Špa09
]
. We first show that
\mathrm{trunc}(F)
is uniformly pseudo-local. Fix
L,R\geq 0
, and let
f\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(X)
. Then,

[\rho(f),\mathrm{trunc}(F)]=\sum_{i}\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})[\rho(f),F]\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})\;.

Due to the jointly bounded geometry of
X
, the support of
f
-which is bounded in diameter by
R
- intersects the supports of a uniformly finite number of
\sqrt{\psi_{i}}
. Hence, the right-hand side is a uniformly finite sum, and each summand is uniformly approximable, since
[\rho,F]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
.

Now we show that
(F-\mathrm{trunc}(F))\rho\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
over
X\setminus Z
. To that end let
g\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(X\setminus Z)
, with
L,R\geq 0
again fixed. Using that
F
is uniformly pseudo-local, calculate that

\mathrm{trunc}(F)\rho(g)=\sum_{i}\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})F\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}}g)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\sum_{i}\rho(\psi_{i}g)F=\rho(g)F\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}F\rho(g)\;.

Here we again used that the support of
g
intersects the supports of a uniformly finite number of
\psi_{i}
. An appeal to Lemma
2.23
concludes the proof.
∎

A graded relative uniform Fredholm module
(H,\rho,F)
is called
balanced
if
H
is the direct sum
H^{\prime}\oplus H^{\prime}
of an ungraded Hilbert space
H^{\prime}
,
H
is graded by this direct sum decomposition, and
\rho=\rho^{\prime}\oplus\rho^{\prime}
for a representation
\rho^{\prime}
on
H^{\prime}
. A graded unitary between balanced Fredholm modules is called
balanced
if it is a direct sum of a single unitary.

We define
K^{u,b}_{0}(X,Z)
as the free group on balanced unitary equivalence classes of balanced relative uniform Fredholm modules over
(X,Z)
, modulo direct sums and operator-homotopies through balanced Fredholm modules. There is a natural map
K^{u,b}_{0}(X,Z)\to K^{u}_{0}(X,Z)
induced by the identity on the level of Fredholm modules. The following proposition states that this map is an isomorphism. We say that the zeroth relative uniform K-homology can be
normalized
by the requirement that all Fredholm modules be balanced. For Paschke duality it will be more convenient to work with this balanced normalization.

Proposition 2.26
.

The identity on Fredholm modules induces a natural isomorphism

K^{u,b}_{0}(X,Z)\xlongrightarrow{\sim}K^{u}_{0}(X,Z)\;.

The statement of the next lemma sounds very natural, but is actually not without subtlety. Indeed, item (ii) can fail for non-balanced graded Fredholm modules. Issues arise from a potential lack of degenerate Fredholm modules with a given representation. We refer to the discussion around Proposition 8.3.14, as well as Exercise 8.8.17, in
[
HR00
]
.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
\rho,\rho^{\prime}:\,C_{0}(X)\to\mathfrak{B}(H)
be representations such that
\rho\sim_{ua}\rho^{\prime}
.

(i)

Let
(H,\rho,F)
be an ungraded relative uniform Fredholm module. Then,
(H,\rho^{\prime},F)
is also a relative uniform Fredholm module, and

\left[(H,\rho^{\prime},F)\right]\;=\;\left[(H,\rho,F)\right]\quad\in\;K^{u}_{1}(X,Z)\;.

(ii)

Let
(H\oplus H,\rho\oplus\rho,F)
be a balanced graded relative uniform Fredholm module. Then,
(H\oplus H,\rho^{\prime}\oplus\rho^{\prime},F)
is also a relative uniform Fredholm module, and

\left[H\oplus H,\rho^{\prime}\oplus\rho^{\prime},F\right]\;=\;\left[H\oplus H,\rho\oplus\rho,F\right]\quad\in\;K^{u,b}_{0}(X,Z)\;.

Lastly the formal twofold periodicity of multigraded spaces and operators provides a formal twofold periodicity on relative uniform K-homology.

Proposition 2.28
.

Let
p\geq\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
. There is a natural formal periodicity isomorphism

K^{u}_{-(p+2)}(X,Z)\cong K^{u}_{-p}(X,Z)\;.

3
Paschke duality

In this section we prove Paschke duality for relative uniform K-homology. Paschke duality relates the K-homology groups
K^{u}_{*}(X,Z)
to the operator K-theory of quotients of the so-called uniform dual algebra
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)
. We refer to Appendix
12
for some basics on K-theory for
C^{*}
-algebras.

Consider for the moment an absolute cycle
(H,\rho,F)
over
X
and assume that
F^{2}=I
and
F^{*}=F
. First consider the case that the cycle is ungraded. The operator
F
has spectrum contained in
\{\pm 1\}
, and we may consider the projection
P
onto the
(+1)
-eigenspace, it is given by
P=\frac{1}{2}(F+1)
. Defining
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
as the algebra of those operators
T
satisfying
[T,\rho]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
, we get that
P
is a projection in the algebra
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
and thus defines an element in its zeroth K-theory.

If the cycle
(H,\rho,F)
is graded and balanced instead, and we write
F=\begin{pmatrix}0&U^{*}\\
U&0\end{pmatrix}
, then
U
is a unitary, which also lies in the algebra
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
. Thus
U
defines an element of the first K-theory of
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
.

Now consider
(H,\rho,F)
to be a relative cycle. In the ungraded case
P=\frac{1}{2}(F+I)
need not be a projection, but it satisfies
(P^{2}-P)\rho\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
and
(P-P^{*})\rho\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
over
X\setminus Z
. Thus
P
still defines a projection in the quotient
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
, where
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
is the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
consisting of those operators
T
for which
T\rho\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
over
X\setminus Z
. Thus
P
defines an element of the zeroth K-theory of that quotient. Analogously in the graded case
U
is a unitary in the quotient
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
and hence defines an element in its first K-theory. It is this assignment that will give us Paschke duality.

There is one technical obstacle to overcome: Cycles with different representations generally give rise to projections/unitaries in different dual algebras. Two different approaches exist to handle this issue in non-uniform K-homology. There exists an (essentially unique) representation of
C_{0}(X)
which contain every other representation as sub-representations up to unitary equivalence and compact perturbation. This is a consequence of a theorem of Voiculescu
26
26

26

In more detail: A representation
\rho:\,A\to\mathfrak{B}(H^{\rho})
of a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is called
ample
if the image of
\rho
contains no compact operator besides
0
. Voiculescu’s theorem states that if
\rho
is ample and
\sigma:\,A\to\mathfrak{B}(H^{\sigma})
is any other representation, then there exists an isometry
V:\,H^{\sigma}\to H^{\rho}
such that
V^{*}\rho V-\sigma
is compact. It then follows easily that any two ample representations are unitarily equivalent up to a compact perturbation.
. Then any Fredholm module over
X
is stably isomorphic to one over this universal representation. A uniform version of Voiculescu’s theorem exists, but is valid only under the assumption of jointly bounded geometry.

The other approach taking the colimit of the K-theories of the dual algebra quotients over all representations instead. While a bit more involved this works in full generality also in the uniform setting, making this path preferable for our purposes. The colimit-approach to Paschke duality can be found for example in
[
Hig95
]
, and we will loosely follow this source.

The proof of Paschke duality falls into what we have called the first category of proofs in K-homology, in that it is entirely algebraic, relying only on matrix algebra and the formal property of equality up to (uniformly) compact error. Paschke duality for uniform K-homology is therefore proved in exactly the same way as it is for analytic K-homology. Again, we thus present a few proofs as demonstrations that things in uniform K-homology indeed work exactly as they do in analytic K-homology, and skip the rest.

3.1
Uniform covering isometries

We begin by introducing a notion for comparing different representations in the uniform setting. This will allow us to take colimits over all representations later on. This is a modification of the usual concept of covering isometry, and was introduced in
[
Špa09
]
. First, let us quickly introduce a small bit of notation: For a representation
\rho:\,C_{0}(X)\to\mathfrak{B}(H)
we shall also write
(H,\rho)
,
27
27

27

Recall our convention that all Hilbert spaces are assumed to be separable.
and for the sake of brevity we shall also say that
(H,\rho)
is a representation of
X
(instead of
C_{0}(X)
).

Definition 2.29
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces, and
\phi:\,C_{0}(Y)\to C_{0}(X)
a
∗
-homomorphism. Let
(H^{\rho},\rho)
and
(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
be representations of
X
and
Y
respectively. We say that an isometry
V:\,H^{\rho}\to H^{\sigma}

uniformly covers

\phi
if

V^{*}\sigma(f)V\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\rho(\phi(f))\;.

In this case we shall also write
V:\,(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
to stress the representations involved.

The first lemma states that if
V:\,(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
is a uniform covering isometry, then up to a uniformly compact error
\sigma
splits as the direct sum of
\rho
and an action on the complement of
V(H^{\rho})
.

Lemma 2.30

(Compare
[
HR00
, Lemma 3.1.6]
)
.

Let
X
and
Y
be metric spaces,
\phi:\,C_{0}(Y)\to C_{0}(X)
a
∗
-homomorphism, and
(H^{\rho},\rho)
and
(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
representations of
X
and
Y
respectively. Let
V:\,(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
be an isometry uniformly covering
\phi
. Let
P:=VV^{*}
denote the orthogonal projection onto the image of
V
. Then,

P\sigma(I-P),\;(I-P)\sigma P\;\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\;0\;.

Proof.

Decompose
H^{\sigma}=PH^{\sigma}\oplus(I-P)H^{\sigma}
, and write

\sigma\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{11}&\sigma_{12}\\
\sigma_{21}&\sigma_{22}\end{pmatrix}

with respect to that decomposition. This means that
\sigma_{11}=P\sigma P
,
\sigma_{12}=P\sigma(I-P)
and so on. Let
f\in C_{0}(X)
. Because
\sigma
is a
∗
-homomorphism, it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{11}(\bar{f}f)&\sigma_{12}(\bar{f}f)\\
\sigma_{21}(\bar{f}f)&\sigma_{22}(\bar{f}f)\end{pmatrix}\;=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{11}(f)^{*}\sigma_{11}(f)+\sigma_{12}(f)^{*}\sigma_{12}(f)&\sigma_{11}(f)^{*}\sigma_{21}(f)+\sigma_{12}(f)^{*}\sigma_{22}(f)\\
\sigma_{21}(f)^{*}\sigma_{11}(f)+\sigma_{22}(f)^{*}\sigma_{12}(f)&\sigma_{22}(f)^{*}\sigma_{22}(f)+\sigma_{21}(f)^{*}\sigma_{21}(f)\end{pmatrix}\;.

(5)

Observe that
\sigma_{11}=P\sigma P\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}V(\rho\circ\phi)V^{*}
. It follows that
28
28

28

If
f\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(X)
, then
\bar{f}f\in(2L)\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R}(X)
. The first relation therefore follows from
\sigma_{11}\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}V(\rho\circ\phi)V^{*}
. The last relation holds because if
\varphi_{1}\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\psi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\psi_{2}
, then
\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}
, as can be shown with the help of Lemma
10.4
(iv).

\sigma_{11}(\bar{f}f)\;\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\;V\rho(\phi(\bar{f})\phi(f))V^{*}\;=\;(V\rho(\phi(f))V^{*})^{*}(V\rho(\phi(f))V^{*})\;\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\;\sigma_{11}(f)^{*}\sigma_{11}(f)\;.

The upper-left corner of (
5
) therefore reduces to
\sigma_{12}(f)^{*}\sigma_{12}(f)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
. It follows that
\sigma_{12}\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
as well
29
29

29

We note in Appendix
10
that the set of
\sigma
with
\sigma\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
forms a closed two-sided
∗
-ideal in the
C^{*}
-algebra of bounded maps from the unit ball in
C_{0}(X)
to
\mathfrak{B}(H)
. It is a general fact that if
J
is such an ideal in a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, and
a\in A
is such that
a^{*}a\in J
, then
a\in J
.
, hence also
\sigma_{21}(f)=\sigma_{12}(\bar{f})^{*}\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
.
∎

We can use the uniform covering isometry
V
to view
H^{\rho}
as a subspace of
H^{\sigma}
. The previous lemma states that
\sigma\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}(\rho\circ\phi)\oplus\sigma_{22}
, where
\sigma_{22}
acts on the orthogonal complement of
H^{\rho}
. Assume that
\phi=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, and let
(H^{\rho},\rho,F)
be a uniform Fredholm module. Then add a degenerate Fredholm module on the orthogonal complement of
H^{\rho}
, for example
\pm I
. Since
\sigma
agrees with
\rho\oplus\sigma_{22}
up to a uniformly compact perturbation, this yields a uniform Fredholm module on
(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
, and since it differs from
(H^{\rho},\rho,F)
only by a degenerate module, it will have the same uniform K-homology class. The following lemma makes this argument precise.

Lemma 2.31
.

Let
(H^{\rho},\rho)
and
(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
be representations of
X
, and suppose that
V:\,(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
is an isometry uniformly covering the identity of
X
. Let
\kappa\in\{\pm 1\}
.

(i)

Let
x=(H^{\rho},\rho,F)
be an ungraded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(X,Z)
. Then,

\mathrm{Ad}_{\kappa}(V)(x):=(H^{\sigma},\sigma,VFV^{*}+\kappa(I-VV^{*}))

is also an ungraded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(X,Z)
, and

[\mathrm{Ad}_{\kappa}(V)(x)]=[x]\in K^{u}_{1}(X,Z)\;.

(ii)

Let
x=(H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\rho},\rho\oplus\rho,\begin{pmatrix}0&F_{-}\\
F_{+}&0\end{pmatrix})
be a balanced graded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(X,Z)
. Then,

\mathrm{Ad}_{\kappa}(V)(x):=\left(H^{\sigma}\oplus H^{\sigma},\sigma\oplus\sigma,\begin{pmatrix}0&VF_{-}V^{*}+\kappa(I-VV^{*})\\
VF_{+}V^{*}+\kappa(I-VV^{*})&0\end{pmatrix}\right)

is also a balanced graded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(X,Z)
, and

[\mathrm{Ad}_{\kappa}(V)(x)]=[x]\in K^{u}_{0}(X,Z)\;.

Proof.

We start with (i). Set
P:=VV^{*}
, the orthogonal projection in
H^{\sigma}
onto the image of
V
. Lemma
2.30
and the fact that
V
is a uniform covering isometry imply that

\sigma\;\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\;P\sigma P+(I-P)\sigma(I-P)\;\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\;V\rho V^{*}+(I-P)\sigma(I-P)\;.

Because
(I-P)V=0
and
V^{*}(I-P)=0
, it follows for
f\in C_{0}(X)
and
g\in C_{0}(X\setminus Z)
that

\displaystyle[\sigma(f),VFV^{*}+\kappa(I-P)]\;

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\;V[\rho(f),F]V^{*}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\;0

\displaystyle\left((VFV^{*}+\kappa(I-P))^{2}-I\right)\sigma(g)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left(VF^{2}V^{*}-P\right)\sigma(g)

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}VF^{2}\rho(g)V^{*}-V\rho(g)V^{*}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0

\displaystyle\left((VFV^{*}+\kappa(I-P))^{*}-VFV^{*}-\kappa(I-P)\right)\sigma(g)\;

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}V(F^{*}-F)\rho(g)V^{*}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0\;.

Hence,
(H^{\sigma},\sigma,VFV^{*}+\kappa(I-P))
is a relative uniform Fredholm module. To see that it defines the same uniform K-homology class as
(H^{\rho},\rho,F)
, consider the self-adjoint unitary

U\;:=\;\begin{pmatrix}0&V^{*}\\
V&I-P\end{pmatrix}

acting on
H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\sigma}
. It holds that

U\begin{pmatrix}F&0\\
0&\kappa I\end{pmatrix}U\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\kappa I&0\\
0&VFV^{*}+\kappa(I-P)\end{pmatrix}\;.

Thus, in
K^{u}_{1}(X,Z)
we have the equalities

\displaystyle\left[H^{\rho},\rho,F\right]\;

\displaystyle=\;\left[H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\sigma},\rho\oplus\sigma,F\oplus\kappa I\right]

\displaystyle=\;\left[H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\sigma},U(\rho\oplus\sigma)U,\kappa I\oplus\left(VFV^{*}+(I-P)\right)\right]\;.

Now, compute that

U^{*}(\rho\oplus\sigma)U\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}V^{*}\sigma V&V^{*}\sigma(I-P)\\
(I-P)\sigma V&V\rho V^{*}+(I-P)\sigma(I-P)\end{pmatrix}\;.

It thus follows from Lemma
2.30
and the fact that
V
is a uniform covering isometry that
U^{*}(\rho\oplus\sigma)U\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\rho\oplus\sigma
. With the help of Lemma
2.27
we conclude that

\displaystyle\left[H^{\rho},\rho,F\right]\;

\displaystyle=\;\left[H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\sigma},U^{*}(\rho\oplus\sigma)U,\kappa I\oplus(VFV^{*}+(I-P))\right]

\displaystyle=\;\left[H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\sigma},\rho\oplus\sigma,\kappa I\oplus(VFV^{*}+(I-P))\right]

\displaystyle=\;\left[H^{\sigma},\sigma,VFV^{*}+(I-P)\right]

in
K^{u}_{1}(X,Z)
.

The proof of (ii) is analogous. The balanced unitary
U\oplus U
on
(H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\sigma})\oplus(H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\sigma})
, with
U
as above, provides a unitary equivalence between

\begin{pmatrix}&&F_{-}&0\\
&&0&\kappa I\\
F_{+}&0&&\\
0&\kappa I&&\end{pmatrix}\;\mathrm{and}\;\begin{pmatrix}&&\kappa I&0\\
&&0&VF_{-}V^{*}+\kappa(I-P)\\
\kappa I&0&&\\
0&VF_{+}V^{*}+\kappa(I-P)&&\end{pmatrix}\;,

and
(U\oplus U)((\rho\oplus\sigma)\oplus(\rho\oplus\sigma))(U\oplus U)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}(\rho\oplus\sigma)\oplus(\rho\oplus\sigma)
. Equality of the uniform K-homology classes again follows from Lemma
2.27
.
∎

3.2
Uniform dual algebras

Next we recall the definition of (relative) uniform dual algebras, which were introduced in
[
Špa09
]
. Their properties and compatibility with uniform covering isometries were partially covered there already, and the parts that were not are straight-forward adaptations of the non-uniform counterparts. Throughout, let
X
be a metric space, and
Z
a closed subspace.

Definition 2.32
.

Let
(H,\rho)
be a representation of
X
. Define
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
as the subset of
\mathfrak{B}(H)
consisting of all operators
T
such that
[T,\rho]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
over
X
. Furthermore, let
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
denote the subset of
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
of all operators
T
such that
T\rho,\,\rho T\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
over
X\setminus Z
.

We call
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
the
uniform dual algebra
and
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
the
relative uniform dual algebra
(associated to
\rho
). If
Z=\emptyset
, we will write
\mathfrak{C}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
for
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,\emptyset)
, the algebra of uniformly locally compact operators. Both the uniform dual algebra and its relative version are
C^{*}
-algebra, with the latter being an ideal in the former.

Proposition 2.33

(
[
Špa09
, Lemma 4.2]
)
.

\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathfrak{B}(H)
, and
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
is an ideal
30
30

30

By an ideal of a
C^{*}
-algebra we shall always mean a closed two-sided
∗
-ideal
of
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
.

We can therefore form the quotient
\mathfrak{Q}_{\rho}^{u}(X,Z):=\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
, this is a well-defined
C^{*}
-algebra. Paschke duality states that the K-theory of this quotient algebra represents relative uniform K-homology, at least after taking the colimit over
\rho
. Again, we refer to Appendix
12
for the basics of operator K-theory. This colimit can be implemented using uniform covering isometries, as is stated by the following proposition.

Proposition 2.34

(
[
Špa09
, Lemmas 4.8, 5.4]
)
.

Let
(X,Z)\xrightarrow{\phi}(Y,W)
be a filtered map of pairs, let
(H^{\rho},\rho)
and
(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
be representations of
X
and
Y
respectively, and let
V:\,(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
be an isometry uniformly covering
\phi
. Then, there is a well-defined
∗
-homomorphism

\mathrm{Ad}(V):\;\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\sigma}(Y)\;,\;T\longmapsto VTV^{*}

such that
\mathrm{Ad}(V)\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\subseteq\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\sigma}(Y,W)
.

The induced homomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}(V)_{*}:\;

\displaystyle K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)\right)\longrightarrow K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\sigma}(Y)\right)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}(V)_{*}:\;

\displaystyle K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\right)\longrightarrow K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\sigma}(Y,W)\right)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}(V)_{*}:\;

\displaystyle K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\right)\longrightarrow K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\sigma}(Y,W)\right)

are independent of
V
, in that any other isometry
(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
uniformly covering
\phi
induces the same homomorphisms.

Define
\mathfrak{P}(X)
to be the category whose objects are representations
(H^{\rho},\rho)
of
X
,
31
31

31

All Hilbert spaces are assumed separable.
and whose morphisms are isometries
(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
uniformly covering the identity. This category is directed, because for any two representations
(H^{\rho},\rho)
and
(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
, the inclusion into
(H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\sigma},\rho\oplus\sigma)
as the first and second direct summand respectively is a morphism in
\mathfrak{P}(X)
.

By Proposition
2.34
the assignments mapping
(H^{\rho},\rho)
to either of the algebras
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
,
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
or
\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
are well-defined functors from
\mathfrak{P}(X)
to the category of
C^{*}
-algebras and
∗
-homomorphisms. Arbitrary colimits exist in that category, so that we can define
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)
,
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z)
and
\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)
as the colimits of the respective functors.

We will be interested in the K-theory of these colimits. Operator K-theory commutes with colimits, so that

\displaystyle K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{colim}\;K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}_{\rho}^{u}(X))\;,

\displaystyle K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{colim}\;K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}_{\rho}^{u}(X,Z))\;,

\displaystyle K_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{colim}\;K_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}_{\rho}^{u}(X,Z))\;,

where all colimits are taken over
\mathfrak{P}(X)
. If one wishes to avoid colimits of
C^{*}
-algebras, one could also take the respective right-hand sides as definitions for
K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X))
,
K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))
and
K_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z))
. These colimits of K-theory groups have a somewhat simpler structure, because all uniform covering isometries between fixed representations induce the same homomorphism on K-theory. The colimit over
\mathfrak{P}(X)
of the K-theory groups therefore factors as the colimit over the poset whose elements are representations
(H^{\rho},\rho)
, and where
\rho\leq\sigma
holds if there is a uniform covering isometry
(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
.

Suppose that
X
has jointly bounded geometry, and that
(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
is an ample representation.
32
32

32

Recall that
\sigma
is called ample if
\sigma(C_{0}(X))H^{\sigma}=H^{\sigma}
, i.e.
\sigma
is non-degenerate, and
\sigma(f)
is compact only if
f=0
.
Then, the uniform version of Voiculescu’s theorem proved by Špakula implies that for any other representation
(H^{\rho},\rho)
there exists a uniform covering isometry
(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
(
[
Špa10
, Corollary 3.3]
).
33
33

33

The uniform Voiculescu theorem stated in
[
Špa10
]
requires slightly different properties of the space
X
. See the discussion in
[
Eng14
, Section 4.5]
for the observation that jointly bounded geometry is required instead of locally and coarsely bounded geometry separately, and that properness of the underlying space, which was required in
[
Špa10
]
, is not essential.
While this does not quite make
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\sigma}(X)
a cofinal object of the diagram
(H,\rho)\mapsto\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
, since there may be many such uniform covering isometries, this is no issue on the level of K-theory. Thus the K-theory of the uniform dual algebra associated to an ample representation is cofinal for the K-theories of uniform dual algebras associated to any representation, same for the relative uniform dual algebras and their quotients. Thus we conclude:

Proposition 2.35
.

Let
X
have jointly bounded geometry, and let
(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
be an ample representation of
X
. Then,

K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X))\cong K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}^{u}(X))\;,\;K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))\cong K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}^{u}(X,Z))\;,\;K_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z))\cong K_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}_{\sigma}^{u}(X,Z))\;.

The K-theory of the dual algebra of a space relative to the empty set vanishes, and the same holds in the uniform setting. Viewed through the lense of Paschke duality this expresses that the uniform K-homology of a space
X
and that of
X
relative to
\emptyset
agree. Recall that we denote
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,\emptyset)
by
\mathfrak{C}^{u}(X)
.

Proposition 2.36

(Compare
[
Hig95
, Lemma 1.6]
)
.

It holds that
K_{*}(\mathfrak{C}^{u}(X))=0
. Therefore the quotient map induces a natural isomorphism

K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)\right)\cong K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,\emptyset)\right)\;.

Lastly we turn to the functoriality in the underlying pair. This is essentially covered by Proposition
2.34
. In fact, if
\phi:\,(X,Z)\to(Y,W)
is a filtered map of pairs and
(H^{\rho},\rho)
a representation of
X
, then
(H^{\rho},\rho\circ\phi)
is a representation of
Y
, and
\mathrm{id}_{H^{\rho}}:\,(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\rho},\rho\circ\phi)
uniformly covers
\phi
. Thus we have inclusions
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)\subseteq\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho\circ\phi}(Y)
and
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\subseteq\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho\circ\phi}(Y,W)
, and therefore a canonical map
\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\to\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho\circ\phi}(Y,W)
. Moreover, if
V:\,(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
uniformly covers
\mathrm{id}_{X}
, then
V:\,(H^{\rho},\rho\circ\phi)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma\circ\phi)
uniformly covers
\mathrm{id}_{Y}
. These data therefore assemble into well-defined maps
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)\to\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Y)
,
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z)\to\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Y,W)
and
\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\to\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(Y,W)
in a functorial way. Passing to K-theory lets us conclude:

Proposition 2.37
.

The assignments

X\mapsto K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)\right)\;,\;(X,Z)\mapsto K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\;,\;(X,Z)\mapsto K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)

are functors from the category of metric spaces
34
34

34

recall that we really mean the category of second-countable locally compact metric spaces.
and filtered maps to abelian groups in the first case, and from the category of pairs and filtered maps of pairs to abelian groups in the latter cases.

3.3
Paschke duality for relative uniform K-homology

The proof of Paschke duality for relative uniform K-homology is the same calculation as for analytic K-homology. We will therefore only indicate the steps involved instead of reproducing the entire proof.

We begin with the definition of the map
K_{*+1}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z))\to K_{*}^{u}(X,Z)
. To that end fix a representation
(H^{\rho},\rho)
of
X
. Let
p\in\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)=\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
be a projection, and choose a representative
P\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
of
p
. Then,
P
being a projection modulo
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
translates into
(H^{\rho},\rho,2P-I)
being an ungraded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(X,Z)
. Denote its relative uniform K-homology class by
\varphi_{1}^{\rho}(p)
. The class
\varphi_{1}^{\rho}
can be defined equally well for projections in a matrix algebra over
\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
.

Had we chosen a different representative
P^{\prime}
of
p
, then
P-P^{\prime}
would lie in
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
, meaning that the Fredholm modules
(H^{\rho},\rho,2P-I)
and
(H^{\rho},\rho,2P^{\prime}-I)
differ only by a uniformly compact perturbation. By Lemma
2.23
the class
\varphi_{1}^{\rho}(p)
is therefore independent of the choice of representative of
p
. Moreover, homotopies of
p
can be lifted to homotopies of representatives, yielding operator-homotopies of the corresponding Fredholm modules. Moreover, the assignment
\varphi_{1}^{\rho}
is compatible with direct sums, and sends the zero-projection to the class of a degenerate module, i.e. to zero. We conclude that
\varphi_{1}^{\rho}
descends to a well-defined homomorphism

\varphi_{1}^{\rho}:\,K_{0}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\right)\longrightarrow K_{1}^{u}\left(X,Z\right)\;.

We can do the same with degree-1 K-theory: Let
u\in\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
be a unitary, and
U\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
a representative. Then,
\left(H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\rho},\rho\oplus\rho,\begin{pmatrix}0&U^{*}\\
U&0\end{pmatrix}\right)
is a balanced graded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(X,Z)
. Denote its class by
\varphi_{0}^{\rho}(u)\in K_{1}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z))
. It can be defined analogously for unitaries in a matrix algebra over
\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
. Different choices of representative again yield uniformly compact perturbations, homotopies yield operator-homotopies, the identity is mapped to zero, and the mapping respects direct sums. We therefore conclude that
\varphi_{0}^{\rho}
descends to a well-defined homomorphism

\varphi_{0}^{\rho}:\,K_{1}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\right)\longrightarrow K_{0}^{u}\left(X,Z\right)\;.

We wish to argue that the maps
\varphi_{*}^{\rho}
give rise to maps on
K_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z))
. To that end suppose that
V:\,(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
is a uniform covering isometry between representations of
X
. Using the definitions it is straight-forwardly calculated that

\mathrm{Ad}_{(-1)^{p}}(V)\circ\varphi^{\rho}_{p}=\varphi_{p}^{\sigma}\circ\mathrm{Ad}(V)

holds on the level of Fredholm modules. We recall that
\mathrm{Ad}_{(-1)^{p}}(V)
is a suitable conjugation by
V
on Fredholm modules, as defined in Lemma
2.31
. There it is also shown that
[\mathrm{Ad}_{(-1)^{p}}(V)x]=[x]
for any
[x]\in K_{*}^{u}(X,Z)
. We conclude that

\varphi_{*}^{\sigma}\left(\mathrm{Ad}(V)a\right)=\varphi^{\rho}_{*}(a)\;\in K_{*}^{u}(X,Z)\;,

where
a\in\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
is either a projection or a unitary, depending on the degree. This lets us conclude that the maps
\varphi_{p}^{\rho}
assemble into a map
\varphi_{p}:\,K_{p+1}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z))\to K^{u}_{p}(X,Z)
on the colimit. We also note that this map is natural with respect to filtered maps of pairs, given that functoriality for both (relative) uniform dual algebras and uniform K-homology is obtained by pre-composition. Thus we have the following:

Lemma 2.38
.

The maps
\varphi_{p}
,
p=0,1
, constructed above give rise to natural homomorphisms

K_{1}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\xlongrightarrow{\varphi_{0}}K_{0}^{u}\left(X,Z\right)\quad,\quad K_{0}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\xlongrightarrow{\varphi_{1}}K_{1}^{u}\left(X,Z\right)\;.

The homomorphisms
\varphi_{p}
implement Paschke duality. We describe the construction of their inverses. Let
x=(H^{\rho},\rho,F)
be an ungraded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(X,Z)
. Then,
P:=\frac{1}{2}(F+I)
defines a projection in
\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
. Denote its K-theory class in the colimit by
\beta_{1}(x)\in K_{0}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z))
. The mapping
\beta_{1}
takes operator-homotopies to homotopies of projections, and it respects unitary equivalences and direct sums. Therefore it gives rise to a well-defined homomorphism

\beta_{1}:\;K_{1}^{u}(X,Z)\longrightarrow K_{0}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\;.

Analogously, if
x=(H^{\rho}\oplus H^{\rho},\rho\oplus\rho,\begin{pmatrix}0&F_{-}\\
F_{+}&0\end{pmatrix})
is a balanced graded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(X,Z)
, then
F_{+}
defines a unitary in
\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)
. Denote its K-theory class in the colimit by
\beta_{0}(x)\in K_{1}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z))
. We note that this construction really requires the Fredholm module to be balanced. Again, the mapping
\beta_{0}
descends to a well-defined homomorphism

\beta_{0}:\;K_{0}^{u,b}(X,Z)\longrightarrow K_{1}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\;.

The maps
\beta_{p}
and
\varphi_{p}
are mutual inverses. This is essentially by definition, though for
p=0
ever so slight attention has to be paid to the formal difference between
K_{0}^{u}(X,Z)
and
K^{u,b}_{0}(X,Z)
. This is covered by Lemma
2.26
though. Thus we have proved Paschke duality for relative uniform K-homology:

Theorem 2.39
.

The natural homomorphisms

K^{u}_{0}(X,Z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 7.3612pt\beta_{0}\hskip 7.3612pt}}}{{\xleftharpoondown[\hskip 8.19447pt\varphi_{0}\hskip 8.19447pt]{}}}K_{1}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\quad,\quad K^{u}_{1}(X,Z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 7.3612pt\beta_{1}\hskip 7.3612pt}}}{{\xleftharpoondown[\hskip 8.19447pt\varphi_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt]{}}}K_{0}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)

are mutually inverse isomorphisms. In particular,

K^{u}_{0}(X,Z)\cong K_{1}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\quad\;\quad K^{u}_{1}(X,Z)\cong K_{0}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\;.

From twofold periodicity we conclude the following.

Corollary 2.40
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
. There is a natural isomorphism

K^{u}_{-p}(X,Z)\cong K_{(p+1)\,\mathrm{mod}\,2}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\;.

Using the K-theory in degree
(p+1)\,\mathrm{mod}\,2
on the right-hand side of this isomorphism is technically correct, since that is what we have proved. It is however cumbersome from a notational standpoint. Using Bott periodicity for K-theory we also get an isomorhism

K^{u}_{-p}(X)\cong K_{-p+1}\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\;.

In the rest of this thesis we will use this degree convention for all things related to Paschke duality.

4
Excision and the long exact sequence

In this section we prove the core theorems relating relative and absolute uniform K-homology: excision and the long exact sequence. As eluded to in the introduction to this chapter these theorems fall into the second category of K-homology results, meaning that they require non-trivial geometric/analytic input. In order to make the proofs work in (relative) uniform K-homology we must make geometric assumptions on the underlying pair. We discuss these first.

Definition 2.41
.

Let
X
be a metric space, and
Z\subseteq X
a closed subspace. We say that
X
is
uniformly resolvable
near
Z
if there is a partition of unity
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i}
on
X\setminus Z
with the following properties:

(1)

For all
\delta>0
there exists
L(\delta)\geq 0
such that
\sqrt{\psi_{i}}
is
L(\delta)
-Lipschitz if
d(\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{i}),Z)\geq\delta
.

(2)

For all
R\geq 0
and
\delta>0
, there exists
M(R,\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all compact
K\subseteq X\setminus Z
with
\mathrm{diam}(K)\leq R
and
d(K,Z)\geq\delta
it holds that

\left|\left\{i\,|\,\psi_{i}|_{K}\neq 0\right\}\right|\leq M(R,\delta)\;.

(3)

For all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
i
it holds that

\mathrm{diam}(\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{i}))\geq\varepsilon\;\Rightarrow\;d(\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{i}),Z)\geq\delta\;.

Furthermore we say that
X
is
transversally controlled
near
Z
if the restriction
C_{0}^{+}(X)\to C_{0}^{+}(Z)
admits an approximately filtered completely positive section
35
35

35

Any positive map
\sigma:\,C_{0}^{+}(X)\to\mathfrak{B}(H)
is already completely positive. Thus we will also simply speak of positive section.
, meaning that there exists a completely positive map
\sigma:\,C_{0}^{+}(Z)\to C_{0}^{+}(X)
such that
\sigma(f)|_{Z}=f
for all
f\in C_{0}^{+}(Z)
, and for all
L,R\geq 0
and
\varepsilon>0
there exist
L^{\prime},R^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
\sigma\big(L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(Z)\big)
is contained in the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
L^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R^{\prime}}(X)
. Here
C^{+}_{0}(Z)
and
C^{+}_{0}(X)
denote the algebras of functions that are constant at infinity.

While the author was tempted to call a pair
(X,Z)
with these properties a pair of bounded geometry, he ultimately opted for a term that is less over-loaded and hopefully more descriptive. Indeed, one could think of a partition of unity as in the definition as providing a resolution of the geometry of
X
in terms of bounded pieces that shrink uniformly as one approaches
Z
, hence the name
uniformly resolvable
. Moreover, we may think of a completely positive section
C_{0}^{+}(Z)\to C_{0}^{+}(X)
as providing extensions of functions on
Z
to the direction „transversal to
Z
“  to obtain functions on
X
. This extension map being approximately filtered expresses that we have (approximate) control over Lipschitzness and the size of supports of the extensions, hence the name
transversally controlled
. Of course this is only a vague intuition, since we cannot speak of transversal directions in general metric spaces.

These definitions are technical wishlists, they exactly provide the tools needed to adapt the proofs of excision and the long exact sequence from analytic K-homology to the uniform setting. Fortunately all pairs relevant for the purposes of this thesis are covered by the definitions.

Lemma 2.42
.

Let
(X,Z)
be one of the following:

•

X
is a manifold of bounded geometry, and
Z
a bounded-geometry hypersurface (see Definition
4.7
). This includes the case that
Z
is the boundary of a manifold
X
with boundary and bounded geometry.

•

X
is a simplicial complex of bounded geometry, and
Z
is a subcomplex.

•

Z
has jointly bounded geometry, and
X
is the cone
CZ=[0,1)\times Z
, which contains
Z
as
Z\times\{0\}\subset CZ
.

Then
X
is uniformly resolvable and transversally controlled near
Z
.

Moreover, if
X
has jointly bounded geometry, then it is uniformly resolvable near any closed subspace
Z\subseteq X
.

The proof along with more general sufficient conditions can be found in Appendix
13
. There it is also shown that these really are conditions on the geometry near
Z
, in that if
U
is a uniformly thick neighborhood of
Z
in
X
such that
U
is uniformly resolvable/transversally controlled near
Z
, then so is
X
.

4.1
Excision

We begin with our proof of the excision isomorphism. We adapt the arguments given in Chapter 5 of
[
HR00
]
. The proof relies on a uniform version of the Kasparov lemma, which gives an equivalent condition for pseudo-locality. In the uniform setting it was already discussed by Engel for proper metric spaces
[
Eng14
, Lemma 4.45]
. However, the spaces we are interested in here, namely complements of closed subspaces, are rarely proper, requiring us to prove a version also applicable to our case.

Let
X
be a metric space. Denote by
C_{0}^{+}(X)
the
C^{*}
-algebra of continuous functions on
X
which are constant at infinity. It equals the unitization of
C_{0}(X)
, every function
f\in C_{0}^{+}(X)
can be written uniquely as
f=\lambda\cdot\mathbf{1}_{X}+g
, where
\lambda:=\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)
and
g\in C_{0}(X)
. Moreover, denote the
C^{*}
-algebra of bounded measurable functions on
X
by
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(X)
. Any representation
(H,\rho)
of
C_{0}(X)
extends to a representation of
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(X)
. We will denote this extension by
\rho
as well.

Proposition 2.43
.

Let
(H,\rho)
be a representation of
X
, and let
T\in\mathfrak{B}(H)
. Then, the following are equivalent:

(i)

[\rho(f),T]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(X)
.

(ii)

For all
f\in C_{0}^{+}(X)
it holds that
\rho(f)T\rho(g)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
and
\rho(g)T\rho(f)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
g\in C_{0}(X\setminus\mathrm{supp}(f))
.

(iii)

For all
f\in C_{b}(X)
it holds that
\rho(f)T\rho(g)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
and
\rho(g)T\rho(f)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
g\in C_{0}(X\setminus\mathrm{supp}(f))
.

(iv)

For all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(X)
it holds that
\rho(f)T\rho(g)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
and
\rho(g)T\rho(f)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
g\in C_{0}(X\setminus\mathrm{supp}(f))
.

Proof.

We start by proving (i)
\Rightarrow
(iv). Suppose
[T,\rho(g)]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
g\in C_{0}(X)
. Then for any
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(X)
, we have
\rho(f)T\rho(g)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}\rho(f)\rho(g)T=0
for
g\in C_{0}(X\setminus\mathrm{supp}(f))
, proving this implication. The implications (iv)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii) are obvious.

It remains to prove (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). Suppose that for all
f\in C_{0}^{+}(X)
it holds that
\rho(f)T\rho(g)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
g\in C_{0}(X\setminus\mathrm{supp}(f))
. It suffices to prove that
[T,\rho(g)]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for self-adjoint
g\in C_{0}(X)
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
,
L,R\geq 0
, and let
g\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(X)
be self-adjoint. Construct a partition of the image
g(X)\subseteq[-1,1]
into Borel sets
U_{1},\cdots,U_{n}
as follows: Let
\tilde{U}_{1}
be the half-open interval
(-\frac{\varepsilon}{4},\frac{\varepsilon}{4}]
. Let
\tilde{U}_{2},\cdots,\tilde{U}_{m}
be a partition of
[-1,1]\setminus\tilde{U}_{1}
into half-open intervals
\tilde{U}_{j}
with
\mathrm{diam}(\tilde{U}_{j})<\varepsilon
. The
\tilde{U}_{j}
can be chosen such that if the closures of
\tilde{U}_{j}
and
\tilde{U}_{i}
don’t intersect, then the distance between
\tilde{U}_{j}
and
\tilde{U}_{i}
is
\geq\varepsilon/2
. Set
U_{j}:=\tilde{U}_{j}\cap g(X)
. By discarding empty intersections and relabeling the index
j
in order of ascending supremum of
U_{j}
, we obtain a partition of
g(X)
into non-empty Borel sets
U_{1},\cdots,U_{n}
with the properties that
\mathrm{diam}(U_{j})<\varepsilon
, and
d(U_{j},U_{i})\geq\varepsilon/2
if
|j-i|>1
. Moreover, the cardinality
n
of the partition is bounded by
C/\varepsilon
for some universal constant
C>0
.

We denote by
g_{j}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(X)
the characteristic function of
g^{-1}(U_{j})
. Fix
x_{j}\in g^{-1}(U_{j})
, and set
\tilde{g}:=g(x_{1})\cdot g_{1}+\cdots+g(x_{n})\cdot g_{n}
,
\tilde{g}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(X)
. By construction, we have
||g-\tilde{g}||_{\infty}<\varepsilon
, from which it follows that
||[T,\rho(g)]-[T,\rho(\tilde{g})]||<2||T||\cdot\varepsilon
. Suppose
|i-j|>1
. From our assumptions on
g
and
U_{j},U_{i}
it can be deduced that the distance of
g^{-1}(U_{j})
and
g^{-1}(U_{i})
is at least
\varepsilon/2L
. Set
L^{\prime}:=8L/\varepsilon
. Then, for all
j
, define the function

\tilde{g}_{j}(x)\;:=\;\min\left\{1,\;L^{\prime}\cdot d\left(x,X\setminus U_{1/L^{\prime}}(g^{-1}(U_{j}))\right)\right\}\;.

Here
U_{c}(A)
denotes the open
c
-neighborhood of a subset
A\subseteq X
. These functions have the following properties:

(a)

\tilde{g}_{j}\equiv 1
on
\mathrm{supp}(g_{j})
, and
||\tilde{g}_{j}||_{\infty}\leq 1
,

(b)

\tilde{g}_{j}
is
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz,

(c)

If
0\notin U_{j}
, then
\tilde{g}_{j}
is compactly supported and
\mathrm{diam}(\mathrm{supp}(\tilde{g}_{j}))\leq R^{\prime}:=R+2/L^{\prime}
. If
0\in U_{j}
, then
\tilde{g}_{j}
is constant at infinity.

(d)

the supports of
\tilde{g}_{j}
and
\tilde{g}_{i}
are disjoint if
|j-i|>1
.

Properties (c) and (d) deserve justification. We start with the observation that if
x\in\mathrm{supp}(\tilde{g}_{j})
, then
d(x,g^{-1}(U_{j}))\leq 1/L^{\prime}
, whence it follows from the Lipschitz property of
g
that
d(g(x),U_{j})\leq L/L^{\prime}=\varepsilon/8
. Now, the function
g\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(X)
is compactly supported. For
\delta>0
, the set
\{x\,|\,|g(x)|\geq\delta\}\subseteq X
is therefore also compact, and, because of the above observation, contains the support of
\tilde{g}_{j}
for
\delta
sufficiently small and
0\notin U_{j}
. This proves the compactness of the support of these
\tilde{g}_{j}
, and the diameter bound is clear from the definition of
\tilde{g}_{j}
. On the other hand, if
0\in U_{j}
, then
\tilde{g}_{j}\equiv 1
outside the compact support of
g
, which immediately implies
\tilde{g}_{j}\in C_{0}^{+}(X)
. This proves (c). Property (d) follows from the observation above, together with
d(U_{j},U_{i})\geq\varepsilon/2
.

The properties (a)-(d) in particular imply that
\tilde{g}_{j}\in L^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R+2/L^{\prime}}(X)
for all except at most one
j
, and that for that exception, it still holds that
\tilde{g}_{j}\in C_{0}^{+}(X)
. Using the assumption on
T
, we deduce from (d) that there exists
N=N(L,R,\varepsilon)
such that for
|i-j|>1
the operator
36
36

36

Note that since
|i-j|>1
, at least one of the sets
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
does not contain
0
. Thus,
\tilde{g}_{i}
or
\tilde{g}_{i}
is in
L^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R+2/L^{\prime}}(X)
, and in particular
\rho(\tilde{g}_{j})=\rho(\tilde{g}_{j})
or
\rho(\tilde{g}_{i})=\rho(\tilde{g}_{i})
.

\rho(g_{j})T\rho(g_{i})\;=\;\rho(g_{j})\big(\rho(\tilde{g}_{j})T\rho(\tilde{g}_{i})\big)\rho(g_{i})

is within
\varepsilon^{3}
of a rank-
N
operator. Now, using the fact that the
g_{i}
sum to
\mathbf{1}_{X}
, we write

\displaystyle[T,\rho(\tilde{g})]\;

\displaystyle=\;\left(\sum_{i}\rho(g_{i})\cdot T\cdot\rho(\tilde{g})\right)-\left(\sum_{j}\rho(\tilde{g})\cdot T\cdot\rho(g_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=\;\sum_{i,j}\left(g(x_{j})-g(x_{i})\right)\rho(g_{i})\cdot T\cdot\rho(g_{j})

\displaystyle=\;\sum_{|i-j|=1}\left(g(x_{j})-g(x_{i})\right)\rho(g_{i})\cdot T\cdot\rho(g_{j})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{|i-j|>1}\left(g(x_{j})-g(x_{i})\right)\rho(g_{i})\cdot T\cdot\rho(g_{j})\;.

Every summand in the second sum is within
\varepsilon^{3}
of a rank-
N
operator, and the sum has less than
n^{2}
summands. Recall that
n
itself is bounded by
C/\varepsilon
, hence the second sum is within
C^{2}\varepsilon
of a rank-
N
operator. We split the first sum into a sum with
j=i+1
and a sum with
j=i-1
. Moreover, observe that
\rho(g_{i})
is a projection, and
\rho(g_{i})
and
\rho(g_{j})
are orthogonal for
i\neq j
. Thus the sum with
j=i\pm 1
is a direct sum of operators from
\rho(g_{i\pm 1})H
to
\rho(g_{i})H
. The norm of this direct sum is the supremum of the norms of the summands. It follows that the norm of the sum over
|i-j|=1
is bounded by

\big(\sup_{i}|g(x_{i+1})-g(x_{i})|+\sup_{i}|g(x_{i-1})-g(x_{i})|\big)\cdot||T||\;\leq\;2\varepsilon\cdot||T||\;.

Combining these estimates, we conclude that
[T,\rho(\tilde{g})]
is within
(2||T||+C^{2})\varepsilon
of a rank-
N
operator, which in turn implies that
[T,\rho(g)]
is within
(4||T||+C^{2})\varepsilon
of a rank-
N
operator. Since
C
is an independent constant, and
N
only depends on
L,R
and
\varepsilon
, this concludes the proof.
∎

With this uniform version of the Kasparov lemma in hand we can proceed to prove the excision isomorphism. The crucial technical calculation is an isomorphism between quotients of the (relative) uniform dual algebras. This isomorphism requires the assumption of
X
being uniformly resolvable near
Z
.

Proposition 2.44
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a pair such that
X
uniformly resolvable near
Z
. Then, for any representation
(H,\rho)
of
X
the inclusion
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z)
induces an isomorphism

\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\;\longrightarrow\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z,\emptyset)\;.

Proof.

It holds by definition that
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)=\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)\cap\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z,\emptyset)
. This shows that the inclusion
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z)
induces a well-defined map on the quotients, and that this map is injective. To show that it is also surjective, we need to prove that
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z)=\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)+\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z,\emptyset)
. By our assumption of uniform resolvability there exists a partition of unity
(\psi_{i})_{i\in I}
satisfying the properties (1), (2), and (3) of Definition
2.41
. Set
T^{\prime}:=\sum_{i\in I}\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})T\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})
. We will show that
T^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
and
T-T^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z;\emptyset)
.

To prove that
T^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
, by Proposition
2.43
it suffices to show that if
f\in C_{b}(X)
is a bounded continuous function, then
\rho(f)T^{\prime}\rho(g)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
g\in C_{0}(X\setminus\mathrm{supp}(f))
. Thus, fix
f\in C_{b}(X)
, let
L,R\geq 0
,
\varepsilon>0
, and let
g\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(X)
with support disjoint from
\mathrm{supp}(f)
. Fix
\delta>0
with
L\delta<\varepsilon
, and a function
\psi:\,X\to[0,1]
with satisfies
\psi=0
on the closed
\delta
-neighborhood of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
,
\psi=1
on the complement in
X
of the closed
2\delta
-neighborhood of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
, and such that
\psi
is
L(\delta)
-Lipschitz
37
37

37

For example, one could take
\psi(x)
to be the minimum of
1
and
d(x,W)/\delta
, where

denotes the closed
\delta
-neighborhood of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
. In this case
L(\delta)=\delta
.
for some
L(\delta)
. Set
\tilde{g}:=\psi g
, a function in
(L(\delta)L)\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R}(X)
. It holds that
||g-\tilde{g}||\leq\sup_{d(x,\mathrm{supp}(f))\leq 2\delta}|g(x)|\leq 2\delta\cdot L<2\varepsilon
. Consequently, if
\rho(f)T\rho(\tilde{g})
is within
C\varepsilon
of some rank-
N
operator, then
\rho(f)T\rho(g)
is within
(C+2||f||\cdot||T||)\varepsilon
of that rank-

operator. Thus, to establish

such that
\rho(f)T\rho(\tilde{g})
is within

of a rank-

operator for some

.

We have the equality

\rho(f)T^{\prime}\rho(\tilde{g})\;=\;\sum_{i}\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})\left(\rho(f|_{X\setminus Z})T\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}}\tilde{g})\right)\;.

(6)

Observe that the
i
-th summand in (

) can only be non-zero if
\sqrt{\psi_{i}}f\neq 0
and

. In particular the support of

, whence

. Therefore, the compactum

has distance

from

, and diameter

. Property (2) provides

such that at most

many
\psi_{i}
are non-zero on

. In conjunction with property (3), the above discussion yields that at most

many summands in (
6
) are non-zero, and
\sqrt{\psi_{i}}
is
L(\delta^{\prime})
-Lipschitz for those summands by property (1).

The operator
T
was assumed to lie in
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z)
, which by Proposition
2.43
is equivalent
38
38

38

Via extension by zero,
C_{b}(X\setminus Z)
becomes a subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(X)
, thus it makes sense to insert a function from

into the representation
\rho
.
to
\rho(f^{\prime})T\rho(h)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for all fixed

and

with support disjoint from

. Thus, there exists a natural number

such that

To prove that

we again fix

,

, and take

. Fix

, and a function
\varphi:\,X\setminus Z\to[0,1]
with satisfies

For example, one could take

, where

.
for some

. Set
\tilde{g}:=\varphi g
, a function in

. It holds that
||g-\tilde{g}||\leq\sup_{d(x,Z)\leq 2\delta}|g(x)|\leq 2\delta\cdot L<2\varepsilon
. Consequently, if
(T-T^{\prime})\rho(\tilde{g})
is within

of some rank-

(T-T^{\prime})\rho(g)
is within

of that rank-

operator. Thus, to establish

, it suffices to show that there exists

such that
(T-T^{\prime})\rho(\tilde{g})
is within
C\varepsilon
of a rank-
N
operator for some

.

To that end, note that because
\mathrm{supp}(\tilde{g})
has diameter
\leq R
, and distance
\geq\delta
from

, there exists
M(R,\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sqrt{\psi_{i}}\tilde{g}
is non-zero for at most

many

. Moreover, for those
\sqrt{\psi_{i}}g
is in
(L(\delta)L)\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R}(X\setminus Z)
. These facts follow from properties (1) and (3). Calculate that

\displaystyle T^{\prime}\rho(\tilde{g})\;

\displaystyle=\;\sum_{i}\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})T\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}}\tilde{g})

\displaystyle=\;\sum_{i}\left(\rho(\psi_{i}\tilde{g})T+\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})\cdot[T,\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}}\tilde{g})]\right)

\displaystyle=T\rho(\tilde{g})-[T,\rho(\tilde{g})]+\sum_{i}\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})\cdot[T,\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}}\tilde{g})]\;,

which gives

(T-T^{\prime})\rho(\tilde{g})\;=\;[T,\rho(\tilde{g})]-\sum_{i}\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}})\cdot[T,\rho(\sqrt{\psi_{i}}\tilde{g})]\;.

(7)

Note that the sum on the right-hand side has at most
M(R,\delta)
non-zero summands. Now, let

be such that

is within
\varepsilon
of a rank-

operator for all

, where

, and set

. Since
\delta
depends only on

and
\varepsilon
, the constant

depends only on
L
,
R
and
\varepsilon
. Since
\tilde{g}
and

are in
(L^{\prime})\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R}(X\setminus Z)
, equation (
7
) implies that
(T-T^{\prime})\rho(\tilde{g})
is within
(1+M(R,\delta))\varepsilon
of a rank-
N
operator. This concludes the proof.
∎

We combine this isomorphism at the level of uniform dual algebras with Paschke duality to prove the excision isomorphism. The excision map on relative uniform K-homology is easily defined: If
(H,\rho,F)
is a relative uniform Fredholm module over
(X,Z)
, then
(H,\rho|_{C_{0}(X\setminus Z)},F)
is a uniform Fredholm module over
X\setminus Z
. This follows immediately from the definition of relative uniform Fredhol modules. This restriction process preserves unitary equivalences, operator-homotopies and direct sums, and therefore induces a well-defined homomorphism

\mathrm{exc}:\;K_{*}^{u}(X,Z)\longrightarrow K_{*}^{u}(X\setminus Z)\;.

The proof that it is an isomorphism is a straight-forward reduction to the corresponding isomorphism statement for the dual algebras.

Theorem 2.45
.

Let
(X,Z)
be pair such that
X
is uniformly resolvable near
Z
. Then, the excision map
\mathrm{exc}:\,K_{*}^{u}(X,Z)\to K^{u}_{*}(X\setminus Z)
is a natural isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
(H,\rho)
be a representation of
X
. The previous proposition provides an isomorphism

\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\xlongrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z,\emptyset)\;.

If
V:\,(H^{\rho},\rho)\to(H^{\sigma},\sigma)
is an isometry uniformly covering the identitiy on
X
, then the diagram

\textstyle{\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(V)}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X\setminus Z,\emptyset)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(V)}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\sigma}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\sigma}(X,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\sigma}(X\setminus Z)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\sigma}(X\setminus Z,\emptyset)}

commutes. Thus, the isomorphism passes to the colimits, and upon taking the K-theory, we obtain an isomorphism
40
40

40

There is an ever so slight subtlety involved in the statement that the map on colimits is an isomorphism, because the left-hand side is defined via the colimit over representations of
X
, and that on the right-hand side via representations of
X\setminus Z
. However, any representation
(H,\rho)
of
X\setminus Z
extends to a representation
(H,\tilde{\rho})
of
X
via
\tilde{\rho}(f)u:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho(f\psi_{n})u
, where
\psi_{n}
is an approximate unit of
C_{0}(X\setminus Z)
, see
[
HR00
, before Proposition 2.6.3]
.

K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\longrightarrow K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X\setminus Z)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X\setminus Z,\emptyset)\right)\;.

This isomorphism may be composed with the inverse of the isomorphism
K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X\setminus Z))\to K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X\setminus Z)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X\setminus Z,\emptyset))
from Proposition
2.36
to obtain an isomorphism

K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\longrightarrow K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X\setminus Z)\right)\;.

Since on the level of representatives this isomorphism is little more than an inclusion map, it is not hard to see that the diagram

\textstyle{K_{*+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{*}}
\textstyle{K_{*+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X\setminus Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{*}}
\textstyle{K_{*}^{u}(X,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{exc}}
\textstyle{K_{*}^{u}(X\setminus Z)}

commutes. We conclude that the excision map on uniform K-homology is an isomorphism. Moreover, that it is natural is rather immediate from its definition. Thus the theorem is proved.
∎

4.2
The long exact sequence

We come to the long exact sequence. Again, the essential technical component is an isomorphism statement for the uniform dual algebras, this time at the level of K-theory. This isomorphism requires the assumption of transversal control. The statement is essentially proved by Špakula in Section 5 of
[
Špa09
]
, but since some of the detail differ we give the proof here.

Proposition 2.46
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a pair such that
X
is transversally controlled near
Z
. Then, there is a natural isomorphism

K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Z)\right)\;\cong\;K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z)\right)\;.

Proof.

Let
(H_{X},\rho_{X})
and
(H_{Z},\rho_{Z})
be representations of
X
and
Z
, and
V:\,(H_{Z},\rho_{Z})\to(H_{X},\rho_{X})
an isometry uniformly covering the restriction map
\pi:\,C_{0}(X)\to C_{0}(Z)
. Let
\sigma:\,C_{0}^{+}(Z)\to C_{0}^{*}(X)
be an approximately filtered completely positive section as in Definition
2.41
. Out of these data, in the course of the proof of
[
Špa09
, Lemma 5.3]
Špakula constructs the following half-infinite commutative diagram

\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(SW)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(W)}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho^{\prime}_{X}}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{Z}}(Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(WV)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(V)}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho^{\prime}_{Z}}(Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(S)}
\textstyle{\cdots}

The representation
(H^{\prime}_{Z},\rho^{\prime}_{Z})
of
Z
is given by a dilation of the completely positive map
\rho_{X}\circ\sigma
to a representation, meaning there is an isometry
W:\,H_{X}\to H_{Z}^{\prime}
such that
W^{*}\rho_{Z}^{\prime}W=\rho_{X}\circ\sigma
. This dilation is provided by Stinespring’s theorem. The assumption that
\sigma
is approximately filtered ensures that
\mathrm{Ad}(W)
maps
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}}(X,Z)
to
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho^{\prime}_{Z}}(Z)
, see Remark
2.12
. The representation
\rho_{X}^{\prime}
is the direct sum of
\rho_{X}
and
\rho_{Z}^{\prime}\circ\pi
, and
S
is the isometry including
H_{X}
into
H_{X}\oplus H^{\prime}_{Z}
. This isometry
S
again covers
\pi
uniformly, and transversal control is again needed to ensure that
\mathrm{Ad}(S)
maps
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho^{\prime}_{Z}}(Z)
to
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho^{\prime}_{X}}(X,Z)
. This first commutative parallelogram is extended to the right by repeating the same construction for
S
instead of
V
to obtain a second such parallelogram, and so on. All vertical arrows come from isometries that uniformly cover the respective identity, or are at least homotopic to conjugation by isometries uniformly covering the identity (see
[
Špa09
, Section 5]
).

We extract the desired natural isomorphism from the diagram (
4.2
). Let
(H,\rho_{Z})
be a representation. By setting
\rho_{X}:=\rho_{Z}\circ\pi
and
V:=\mathrm{id}_{H}
, we land in the situation of the diagram (
4.2
). We may compose the map
\mathrm{Ad}(V)
with the map
K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}}(X,Z))\to K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))
to obtain a map
K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{Z}}(Z))\to K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))
. Taking the colimit over
\rho_{Z}
, obtain the map
\Psi:\,K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Z))\to K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))
, which is uniquely characterized by the property of making the diagram

\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{Z}}(Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rho_{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(V)}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rho_{X}}}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))}

commute for all
\rho_{Z}
, and
\rho_{X}
and
V
as above. Here the maps
\iota_{\rho_{Z}}
and
\iota_{\rho_{X}}
are the canonical maps into the respective colimit. We will show that
\Psi
is a natural isomorphism. To that end, we note first that the diagram (
4.2
) still commutes for any representation
(H_{X},\rho_{X})
and any isometry
V:\,(H_{Z},\rho_{Z})\to(H_{X},\rho_{X})
uniformly covering
\pi
. This is because we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{Z}}(Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(\mathrm{id}_{H_{Z}})}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(V)}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{Z}\circ\pi}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(V)}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))}

where for the vertical arrow
\mathrm{Ad}(V)
is viewed as uniformly covering
\pi
, and for the diagonal arrow as uniformly covering
\mathrm{id}_{X}
. Then, the left commutative triangle comes from the factorization
V=V\circ\mathrm{id}_{H_{Z}}
of uniform covering isometries, and the right commutative triangle comes from the definition of the colimit
K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))
. The combination of this latter commutative diagram with the diagram (
4.2
) for
\rho_{X}=\rho_{Z}\circ\pi
and
V=\mathrm{id}_{H_{Z}}
then gives the diagram (
4.2
) for arbitrary
\rho_{X}
and
V
. Then, diagrams (
4.2
) and (
4.2
) combine to yield the commutative diagram

\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{Z}}(Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rho_{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(WV)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(V)}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{Z}^{\prime}}(Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rho_{Z}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(S)}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rho_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(W)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(SW)}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}^{\prime}}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rho_{X}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))}

This diagram, considered for suitable
\rho_{Z}
,
\rho_{X}
and
V
, will yield bijectivity of
\Psi
. Let us start with injectivity. Let
x\in K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Z))
be in the kernel of
\Psi
. The element
x
may written as
x=\iota_{\rho_{Z}}(u)
for some
\rho_{Z}:\,C_{0}(Z)\to\mathfrak{B}(H_{Z})
and
u\in K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{Z}}(Z))
. Let
\rho_{X}:=\rho_{Z}\circ\pi:\,C_{0}(X)\to\mathfrak{B}(H_{Z})
and
V=\mathrm{id}_{H_{Z}}
, and construct the corresponding diagram (
4.2
). Then,
\Psi(x)=0
implies
\iota_{\rho_{X}}(\mathrm{Ad}(V)u)=0
. It may be assumed that
\mathrm{Ad}(V)u=0
.
41
41

41

An element
v\in K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}}(X,Z))
satisfies
\iota_{\rho_{X}}v=0
if and only if there is a representation
\sigma_{X}:\,C_{0}(X)\to\mathfrak{B}(H^{\prime})
and an isometry
U:\,H_{X}\to H^{\prime}
uniformly covering
\mathrm{id}_{X}
, such that
\mathrm{Ad}(U)v=0
. This fact about colimits of abelian groups may be derived from the explicit construction of such colimits.We apply this to
v=\mathrm{Ad}(V)u
. The isometry
UV:\,H_{Z}\to H^{\prime}
uniformly covers
\pi
. Thus, me may construct the diagram (
4.2
) with
UV
instead of
V
, and
\sigma_{X}
instead of
\rho_{X}
. Then,
\iota_{\sigma_{X}}(\mathrm{Ad}(UV)u)=0
would hold, and
\mathrm{Ad}(UV)u=0
by construction. Then, we are in the situation of the text above.
From (
4.2
) it then follows that
x=\iota_{\rho_{Z}}(u)=(\iota_{\rho_{Z}^{\prime}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}(W))(\mathrm{Ad}(V)u)=0
, proving injectivity.

Let us turn to the surjectivity of
\Psi
. Any element
y\in K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))
is of the form
y=\iota_{\rho_{X}}(v)
for some
(H_{X},\rho_{X})
, and some
v\in K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}}(X,Z))
. The representation can be extended to a representation of the algebra
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(X)
of bounded measurable functions on
X
, which we still denote by
\rho_{X}
, and this extension may then be restricted to the subalgebra
C_{0}(Z)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(X)
. We consider the resulting representation
\rho_{Z}
of
C_{0}(Z)
as acting on the Hilbert space
H_{Z}:=PH_{X}
, where
P:=\rho_{X}(\mathbf{1}_{Z})
. Let
V
be the inclusion
H_{Z}\hookrightarrow H_{X}
. Then,

V^{*}\rho_{X}(f)V\;=\;\rho_{X}(\mathbf{1}_{Z})\cdot\rho_{X}(f)\;=\;\rho_{X}(\mathbf{1}_{Z}f)\;=\;\rho_{Z}(\pi(f))\;,

whence
V
uniformly covers
\pi
. Construct the diagram (
4.2
) corresponding to these choices for
\rho_{Z}
and
V
. According to this diagram, we may then write

y=\iota_{\rho_{X}}(v)=\Psi((\iota_{\rho_{Z}^{\prime}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}(W))(v))

(20)

This proves surjectivity and hence bijectivity of
\Psi
.

Naturality of
\Psi
, lastly, follows almost immediately from the definitions. Let
(Y,W)
be another pair, and
\phi:\,(X,Z)\to(Y,W)
a filtered map of pairs. By the defining properties of
\Psi
and the maps induced by
\phi
, the diagram

\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{Z}}(Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(V)}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rho_{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(\mathrm{id}_{H_{Z}})}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{Z}\circ\phi}(W))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rho_{Z}\circ\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(V)}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{*}}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(W))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{*}}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Y,W))}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rho_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}(\mathrm{id}_{H_{X}})}
\textstyle{K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho_{X}\circ\phi}(Y,W))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rho_{X}\circ\phi}}

commutes for
\rho_{Z}
,
\rho_{X}
, and
V
uniformly covering
\pi
. Since every element of
K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Z))
is in the image of some
\iota_{\rho_{Z}}
, it follows that the inner square commutes as well, which gives naturality of
\Psi
.
∎

Existence of the 6-term exact sequence in uniform K-homology now follows directly from the combination the 6-term exact sequence in operator K-theory and Proposition
2.46
.

Theorem 2.47
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a pair such that
X
is transversally controlled near
Z
. There exists a natural 6-term exact sequence

\textstyle{K_{0}^{u}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{0}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{0}(X,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K_{1}^{u}(X,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces K^{u}_{1}(Z)}

Proof.

For every representation
(H,\rho)
of
X
there is an associated short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\longrightarrow 0

of the uniform dual algebras. Given that the maps in this short exact sequence are simply inclusion and quotient map, the maps
\mathrm{Ad}(V)
for some uniform covering isometry
V
are clearly compatible with these exact sequences, whence we may take the colimit to obtain a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z)\longrightarrow 0

of
C^{*}
-algebras. We obtain the 6-term exact sequence

\textstyle{K_{1}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X)/\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))}

in K-theory. Now, Paschke duality and the isomorphism
K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Z))\cong K_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Z))
provided by Proposition
2.46
yield the 6-term exact sequence in uniform K-homology. As every part of this construction is natural in
(X,Z)
, the resulting exact sequence is also natural.
∎

We obtain the long exact corollary by extending the six-term exact sequence via twofold periodicity.

Corollary 2.48
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a pair such that
X
is transversally controlled near
Z
. There exists a natural long exact sequence

\cdots\longrightarrow K^{u}_{-p}(Z)\longrightarrow K^{u}_{-p}(X)\longrightarrow K^{u}_{-p}(X,Z)\xlongrightarrow{\partial}K^{u}_{-(p-1)}(Z)\longrightarrow\cdots\;.

Combining excision and the long exact sequence we may also draw the following corollary.

Corollary 2.49
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a pair such that
X
is uniformly resolvable and transversally controlled near
Z
. Then, there is a natural long exact sequence

\cdots\longrightarrow K^{u}_{-p}(Z)\longrightarrow K^{u}_{-p}(X)\longrightarrow K^{u}_{-p}(X\setminus Z)\xlongrightarrow{\partial}K^{u}_{-(p-1)}(Z)\longrightarrow\cdots\;.

We note that contrary to usual conventions for homology theories the boundary map increases the degree. This is a consequence of the degree convention that
p
-multigraded Fredholm modules define classes in the K-homology of degree
-p
. We have adopted this convention from
[
HR00
]
. Of course twofold periodicity in both K-homology and K-theory reduces this to little more than a formal matter. Thus, we aim to stay consistent with our degree conventions where it seems helpful, but overall prioritize readability over consistency. Also due to twofold periodicity in both (uniform) K-homology and K-theory we will not differentiate too carefully between long exact sequences and 6-term exact sequences, as one is turned into the other via periodicity.

4.3
The relative Mayer-Vietoris sequence

We also provide a Mayer-Vietoris sequence for relative uniform K-homology. The non-relative Mayer-Vietoris sequence was constructed by Špakula
[
Špa09
]
, and our construction is an elaboration of the non-relative case.

Definition 2.50
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a pair, and let
Y_{1},Y_{2}\subseteq X
be closed subspaces such that
Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}=X
. Set
W_{j}:=Y_{j}\cap Z
We say that
(X,Z)=(Y_{1}\cup Y_{2},W_{1}\cup W_{2})
is a
bounded Mayer-Vietoris decomposition
if:

•

d(Y_{1}\setminus Y_{2},Y_{2}\setminus Y_{1})>0
,

•

and the pairs
(X,Y_{j})
,
(Y_{j},Y_{1}\cap Y_{2})
,
(Y_{j},W_{j})
, and
(Y_{1}\cap Y_{2},W_{1}\cap W_{2})
are transversally controlled.

The Mayer-Vietoris sequence in relative uniform K-homology is constructed via an analogous Mayer-Vietoris sequence in the K-theory of the relative uniform dual algebras. We refer to Appendix
12
for a review of the Mayer-Vietoris sequence in operator K-theory, and Proposition
12.7
will be especially relevant to us.

Theorem 2.51
.

Let
(X,Z)=(Y_{1}\cup Y_{2},W_{1}\cup W_{2})
be a bounded Mayer-Vietoris decomposition. There exists a natural Mayer-Vietoris sequence

\textstyle{K_{0}^{u}(Y_{1}\cap Y_{2},W_{1}\cap W_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{0}(Y_{1},W_{1})\oplus K_{0}^{u}(Y_{2},W_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{0}(X,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{K_{1}^{u}(X,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{1}(Y_{1},W_{1})\oplus K_{1}^{u}(Y_{2},W_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces K_{1}^{u}(Y_{1}\cap Y_{2},W_{1}\cap W_{2})}

Proof.

Fix a representation
(H,\rho)
of
X
. We consider the ideals
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{j})\subseteq\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
. By our assumption that
d(Y_{1}\setminus Y_{2},Y_{2}\setminus Y_{1})>0
we can find Lipschitz functions
\psi_{j}:\,X\to[0,1]
,
\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j})\subseteq W_{j}
, with
\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}=1
. Then, any
T\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)
can be written as
T=T\psi_{1}+T\psi_{2}
. Since
T\psi_{j}\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{j})
we conclude that
\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X)=\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{1})+\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{2})
(compare
[
Špa09
, Section 5]
). Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{1}\cap Y_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{1})\cap\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{2})

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,W_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{j})\cap\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,W_{1}\cap W_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{1})\cap\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{2})\cap\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)\;.

Thus Proposition
12.7
provides a Mayer-Vietoris sequence for the decomposition

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Z)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{1})/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,W_{1})+\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{2})/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,W_{2})

(21)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{1}\cap Y_{2})/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,W_{1}\cap W_{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{1})/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,W_{1})\right)\cap\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{2})/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,W_{2})\right)\;.

Due to the naturality of the Mayer-Vietoris sequence we also get an exact sequence corresponding to the colimit of the decompositions (
21
).

Now, by Proposition
2.46
-applicable due to transerval control for the various pairs involved- the inclusions
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Y_{j})\to\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,Y_{j})
,
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(W_{j})\to\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Y,W_{j})
and
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(W_{j})\to\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,W_{j})
induce isomorphisms on K-theory. Combining the latter two implies that
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(Y_{j},W_{j})\to\mathfrak{D}^{u}(X,W_{j})
also induces an isomorphism on K-theory. The same applies to the intersections
Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}
and
W_{1}\cap W_{2}
as well. Naturality of the K-theory exact sequence and the 5-lemma provide us with isomorphisms

\displaystyle K_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(Y_{j},W_{j}))

\displaystyle\cong K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{j})/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,W_{j})\right)

\displaystyle K_{*}(\mathfrak{Q}^{u}(Y_{1}\cap Y_{2},W_{1}\cap W_{2}))

\displaystyle\cong K_{*}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,Y_{1}\cap Y_{2})/\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(X,W_{1}\cap W_{2})\right)

Inserting these isomorphisms into the above Mayer-Vietoris sequence and appealing to Paschke duality yields the relative Mayer-Vietoris sequence for uniform K-homology.
∎

Using twofold periodicity we again extend the 6-term Mayer-Vietoris sequence to a Mayer-Vietoris sequence involving all degrees.

Corollary 2.52
.

Let
(X,Z)=(Y_{1}\cup Y_{2},W_{1}\cup W_{2})
be a bounded Mayer-Vietoris decomposition. There exists a natural Mayer-Vietoris sequence

\cdots\to K_{-p}^{u}(Y_{1},W_{1})\oplus K_{-p}^{u}(Y_{2},W_{2})\to K^{u}_{-p}(X,Z)\xrightarrow{\delta}K^{u}_{-(p-1)}(Y_{1}\cap Y_{2},W_{1}\cap W_{2})\to\cdots\;.

5
Suspension and the Kasparov product

Let
X
be a metric space. The
suspension
of
X
is defined as
SX:=(0,1)\times X
, endowed with the product metric. It arises as the complement of
X\cong\{0\}\times X
in the cylinder
CX:=[0,1)\times X
. The algebra
C_{0}(CX)
is contractible, hence
CX
has vanishing K-homology. Consequently, the boundary map
\partial:\,K_{*-1}(SX)\to K_{*}(X)
is an isomorphism, the
suspension isomorphism
. We will convince ourselves that the same argument goes through in uniform K-homology.

Proposition 2.53
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
. Let
X
be a metric space of jointly bounded geometry, and
CX=[0,1)\times X
the cylinder equipped with the product metric. The boundary map

\partial:\,K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(SX)\cong K^{u}_{-(p+1)}\left(CX,X\right)\longrightarrow K^{u}_{-p}(X)

in the long exact sequence of the pair
(CX,X)
is a natural isomorphism.

Proof.

We show that
K^{u}_{*}(CX)=0
. The map

\phi:\,C_{0}(CX)\longrightarrow C_{0}([0,1]\times CX)=C_{0}([0,1]\times[0,1)\times X)\;,\;(\phi f)(t,s,x)=f(s(1-t)+t,x)

provides a homotopy from
\mathrm{eval}_{0}\circ\phi=\mathrm{id}_{CX}
to
\mathrm{eval}_{1}\circ\phi=0
, the usual contraction of
C_{0}(CX)
. Then
K_{*}^{u}(CX)=0
follows from homotopy invariance of uniform K-homology once we prove that
\phi
is filtered. To that end note that
\phi
is the restriction of the map

\tilde{\phi}=(m\times\mathrm{id}_{X})*:\,C_{0}\left([0,1]\times X\right)\to C_{0}\left([0,1]\times[0,1]\times X\right)

to the kernel of
\mathrm{id}_{[0,1]}\times\mathrm{eval}_{1}\times\mathrm{id}_{X}
, where
m:\,[0,1]\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
is given by
m(s,t)=s(1-t)+t
. The map
m
is Lipschitz, and trivially uniformly cobounded, so that
m\times\mathrm{id}_{X}
also has these properties. Therefore
\tilde{\phi}
is filtered. Since
\tilde{\phi}
maps
C_{0}(CX)
into
C_{0}([0,1]\times CX)
we conclude that
\phi
, the restriction of
\tilde{\phi}
to that subalgebra, is filtered as well.
∎

There is also a natural map going in the opposite direction. Indeed the Kasparov product provides a map

K^{u}_{*}(X)\cong K^{u}_{-1}((0,1))\otimes K^{u}_{*}(X)\longrightarrow K^{u}_{*-1}(SX)\;.

The first isomorphism follows from
K^{u}_{-1}((0,1))\cong K_{-1}((0,1))\cong\mathbb{Z}
. This map is thus given by
\alpha\mapsto d\times\alpha
for
\alpha\in K^{u}_{*}(X)
, where
d
is the generator of
K_{-1}((0,1))
corresponding to the canonical orientation of the interval
(0,1)
. For analytic K-homology this map is (almost) inverse to the suspension isomorphism. We will prove that the same holds for uniform K-homology as well.

Theorem 2.54
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
. Let
X
be a proper metric space of bounded geometry. Then, the map

K^{u}_{-p}(X)\longrightarrow K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(SX)\;,\;\alpha\longmapsto d\times\alpha

is inverse to the suspension isomorphism up to a sign. Indeed, it holds that
