, a set with diameter
\leq N\cdot r=:R^{\prime}
. Moreover, there exists
t=t(\delta)
such that
\mathrm{supp}(f_{t})\subseteq N_{\frac{\delta}{2}}(\mathrm{supp}(f))
. Lastly, the estimate (
29
) yields

\left|D^{\alpha}\left(f_{t}\circ\kappa_{i}^{-1}\right)\right|\leq\frac{||D^{\alpha}\varphi||}{t^{|\alpha|}}\;,

implying that
\|f_{t}\|_{C^{m}_{b}}
is bounded for any
m
by a constant dependent only on
t
and
m
but not
f
. It follows that for any
m
there is
L^{\prime}\geq 0
, depending on
L
,
m
and
t
but not the specific
f
, such that
f_{t}\in L^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R^{\prime},\frac{\delta}{2},m}(M)
for
t\leq t(\delta)
sufficiently small. Moreover, inspecting the estimate (
28
) we see that
||f-f_{t}||\lesssim L\cdot t
. Thus,
t
can be chosen so that
||f-f_{t}||\leq\varepsilon
for all
f\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(M)
. This finishes the proof of the implication (iv)
\Rightarrow
(ii).
∎

The second lemma provides uniformly bounded bump functions, whose existence will be exploited for example in the proof of Theorem
4.30
, and in Subsection
23.2
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Let
K\subset\Omega
be a compact subset, and set
R:=\mathrm{diam}(K)
and
\delta:=d(K,\partial\Omega)
. Then, for any
0<\delta^{\prime}<\delta
there exists a function
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
with
\psi=1
on
K
,
\psi=0
outside
N_{\delta^{\prime}}(K)
, and such that
\|\psi\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
is bounded by a constant dependent only on
R
and
\delta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

One can find a Lipschitz function
\psi^{\prime}\in C_{cc}(\Omega)
with
\psi^{\prime}=1
on
N_{\delta^{\prime}/3}(K)
,
\psi^{\prime}=0
outside
N_{2\delta^{\prime}/3}(K)
, such that the Lipschitz constant of
\psi^{\prime}
depends only on
\delta^{\prime}
. Define
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
by mollifying
\psi^{\prime}
. Taking the mollification parameter
t
sufficiently small it holds that
\psi=1
on
K
because
\psi^{\prime}=1
on
N_{\delta^{\prime}/3}(K)
, and that
\psi=0
outside
N_{\delta^{\prime}}(K)
because
\psi^{\prime}=0
outside
N_{2\delta^{\prime}/3}(K)
. The estimate (
29
) yields the desired norm estimate for
\psi
.
∎

15
Extensions and traces

This section deals with the problems of extending Sobolev functions from a domain to the entire space, and of restricting Sobolev functions on a domain to the boundary.

There are two ways to approach the extension problem: The first is abstract, making use of the fact that surjections of Hilbert spaces split. The second is to explicitly write down an extension formula on smooth functions, and verify that it extends to Sobolev spaces. The second approach is preferable in most ways; it is concrete, the same extension formula works for many Sobolev degrees, and it works for Sobolev spaces based on
L^{p}
for
p\neq 2
. The only advantage of the first approach is that it works in full generality, while the second requires uniform regularity up to some degree.

We treat the abstract approach first. Let
T:\,H\to H^{\prime}
be a continuous surjective map between Hilbert spaces. When restricted to
(\ker T)^{\perp}
the map
T
becomes bijective, and by the open mapping theorem its inverse is again continuous. Thus there exists a unique map
S:\,H^{\prime}\to H
such that
TS=\mathrm{id}_{H^{\prime}}
and
\mathrm{im}\,S=(\ker T)^{\perp}
.

Now, consider the restriction map
r:\,H^{s}(M;E)\to H^{s}(\overline{\Omega};E)
. An extension map
e:\,H^{s}(\overline{\Omega};E)\to H^{s}(M;E)
is the same as a right-inverse to
r
, meaning that
r(e(u))=e(u)|_{\Omega}=u
. The restriction map is surjective and continuous by definition. Thus, the abstract argument provides us with the following:

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
\Omega
be a domain in
M
, and let
s\geq 0
. Then, there is an extension map
e:\,H^{s}(\overline{\Omega};E)\to H^{s}(M;E)
uniquely characterized by the property that
\mathrm{im}\,e=(\ker r)^{\perp}
.

To reiterate: This construction heavily relies on the fact that we work with Hilbert spaces (surjections between Banach spaces need not split), and the resulting map is highly dependent on the degree
s
. Thus, whenever possible it is preferable to use the approach described now.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. We begin by considering the extension problem on half-space. Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
be a (potentially vector-valued) smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}=\{x_{n}\geq 0\}
. The function
u
can be extended across the plane
x_{n}=0
by reflection, and via superposition of multiple such reflections the first
k
derivatives can be made continuous. Indeed, let
e_{k}(u)
be defined by
e_{k}(u)=u
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
, and for
x_{n}<0
by

e_{k}(u)(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1},x_{n}):=\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}a_{j}\cdot u(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1},-jx_{n})\;,

where the coefficients
a_{1},\cdots,a_{k+1}
are determined uniquely by the requirement that
e(u)
be in
C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then, the assignment
e_{k}
extends to a continuous map
e_{k}:\,H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
0\leq s\leq k
. Note that the coefficients depend on the degree
k
, though a modification can be made that works for
k=\infty
as well
[
See64
]
. Note also that
e_{k}
can be modified via cut-off functions so that the values of
e_{k}(u)
on
\{x_{n}<0\}
depend only on the values of
u
in the strip
\{0\leq x_{n}\leq\delta\}
for a fixed
\delta>0
, and such that
e_{k}(u)
is supported on
\{-\delta^{\prime}\leq x_{n}\}
for some
\delta^{\prime}>0
.

Now, consider instead the space
\overline{\Omega}_{\xi}:=\{x_{n}\geq\xi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1})\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some bounded
C^{m,1}
-function
\xi:\,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
with finite
C^{m,1}
-norm. Then, the boundary of
\overline{\Omega}_{\xi}
may be straightened out by a
C^{m,1}
-diffeomorphism
\chi_{\xi}:\,\mathbb{R}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\chi_{\xi}(\overline{\Omega}_{\xi})=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
, with bounded
C^{m,1}
-norm. This
C^{m,1}
-diffeomorphism induces a bounded isomorphism

H^{s}(\overline{\Omega}_{\xi})\longrightarrow H^{s}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n})\;,;u\longmapsto u\circ\chi_{\xi}^{-1}\;,

for all
0\leq s\leq m+1
(see for example
[
McL00
, Theorem 3.23]
), and as such gives rise to extension maps

e_{k}^{\xi}:\;H^{s}(\overline{\Omega}_{\xi})\longrightarrow H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\;,\;e_{k}^{\xi}(u)=e_{k}(u\circ\chi_{\xi}^{-1})\circ\chi_{\xi}

for these
s
.

If
\Omega
is a uniform
C^{m,1}
-domain in the bounded-geometry manifold
M
, then these consideration in Euclidean space give rise to extension maps
H^{s}(\overline{\Omega};E)\to H^{s}(M)
,
0\leq s\leq m+1
. Indeed, locally the boundary of
\overline{\Omega}
looks like a domain
\overline{\Omega}_{\xi_{i}}
as considered before. Thus extension maps exist locally, and due to the various uniformness conditions the norms of the local extension maps are uniformly bounded. Using a partition of unity they may therefore be glued together to a global bounded extension map. Since the formula of the original extension map on half-space did not depend on the specific
s
, neither does the extension map for
\Omega
. Thus we obtain the following.

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
\Omega
be a uniform
C^{m,1}
-domain in the manifold
M
of bounded geometry. Then, for all
0\leq s\leq m+1
there exists a bounded extension map

e_{s}:\;H^{s}(\overline{\Omega};E)\longrightarrow H^{s}(M;E)

such that if
s\leq s^{\prime}
, then
e_{s^{\prime}}(u)=e_{s}(u)
for all
u\in H^{s^{\prime}}(\overline{\Omega};E)
. If
\Omega
is a bounded-geometry domain, then extension maps with this property exist for all
s\geq 0
.

The usefulness of the property that
e_{s^{\prime}}(u)=e_{s}(u)
for all
u\in H^{s^{\prime}}(\overline{\Omega};E)
will become immediately clear when we discuss interpolation for the spaces
H^{s}(\overline{\Omega};E)
below. To reiterate another point, though it is less relevant for our purposes: This construction works equally well for Sobolev spaces based on
L^{p}
,
p\neq 2
, and is therefore more general.

Let us briefly discuss trace maps on Sobolev spaces of domains. Apart from a few examples traces will play no role in this thesis, but a treatment of Sobolev spaces on domains would be incomplete without mentioning them. Again, half-space serves as the universal model case. There one shows (see for example
[
Tay96
, Proposition 4.1.6]
) that for all
s>\frac{1}{2}
there is a surjective map

H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\longrightarrow H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\;,\;u\longmapsto u|_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\;.

Then, if
\overline{\Omega}
is a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry, one constructs a trace to the boundary by locally straightening out via uniformly bounded diffeomorphisms, taking traces to half-space locally, and gluing together via a partition of unity. The details are carried out in greater generality in
[
GS13
]
. Thus, one obtains the following.

Theorem 4.24

(
[
GS13
, Theorem 47]
)
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry. Then, for all
s>\frac{1}{2}
there is a surjective trace map

H^{s}(\overline{\Omega};E)\longrightarrow H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega})\;,\;u\longmapsto u|_{\partial\Omega}\;.

In this discussion of traces we have focused on manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry, in particular to domains with smooth boundaries. This is because in this thesis we do not consider Sobolev spaces on non-smooth manifolds, so that we have no way to talk about Sobolev space on the boundary of a non-smooth domain. This is no substantial issue though. If
\Omega
is a uniform
C^{m,1}
-domain, then its boundary is a
C^{m,1}
-manifold that already comes with a canonical set of local
C^{m,1}
-trivializations. Thus we may define
H^{s}(\partial\Omega)
,
0\leq s\leq m+1
, in analogy to how we defined Sobolev spaces on manifolds of bounded geometry. Using the same procedure of local straightening via uniformly bounded
C^{m,1}
-diffeomorphisms, tracing to the straightened boundary, then gluing together via a partition of unity, one obtains surjective trace maps

H^{s}(\overline{\Omega})\longrightarrow H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega)

for
\frac{1}{2}<s\leq m+1
whenever
\Omega
is a uniform
C^{m,1}
-domain. See for example
[
McL00
]
, particularly Theorem 3.37 therein, where this is discussed for
C^{m,1}
-domains in Euclidean space.

16
Interpolation

At this point we will discuss interpolation of the Sobolev spaces on domains in manifolds of bounded geometry. The complex interpolation method was introduced by Calderón in
[
Cal64
]
, and has become a standard tool in the study of Sobolev spaces and related function spaces. Knowledge about interpolation for Sobolev spaces on domains will be necessary to make exact uniform summability statements later in the context of uniform Fredholm modules. The discussion is classical in the context of Euclidean space or compact manifolds. An exhaustive treatment of interpolation for domains in Euclidean space can be found in
[
Tri95
]
.

Let us recall the the basics. Let
E,F
be Banach spaces such that there is a bounded injection
F\hookrightarrow E
. Denote by
\mathcal{H}(E,F)
the space of bounded continuous functions
f
from the complex strip
\{0\leq\mathrm{Re}(z)\leq 1\}
to
E
such that
f
is analytic on the interior of
\{0<\mathrm{Re}(z)<1\}
, and
f(1+i\mathbb{R})\subseteq F
with
\sup_{t}||f(1+it)||_{F}<\infty
. Equipped with the norm
||f||_{\mathcal{H}(E,F)}:=\max\{\sup_{t}||f(it)||_{E},\,\sup_{t}||f(1+it)||_{F}\}
the set
\mathcal{H}(E,F)
becomes a Banach space. Then, for
\theta\in[0,1]
define the
(complex) interpolation space
as

[E,F]_{\theta}:=\{f(\theta)\,|\,f\in\mathcal{H}(E,F)\}\;,

equipped with the norm
||u||_{\theta}:=\inf_{f(\theta)=u}||f||_{\mathcal{H}(E,F)}
. The interpolation space
[E,F]_{\theta}
is a Banach space, and isomorphic to the quotient of
\mathcal{H}(E,F)
by the subspace
\{f(\theta)=0\}
. It is functorial in
E
and
F
in the following way. Let
E,F,E^{\prime},F^{\prime}
be Banach spaces such that there are bounded injections
F\hookrightarrow E
and
F^{\prime}\hookrightarrow E^{\prime}
. Let
T:\,E\to E^{\prime}
be a bounded map which restricts to a bounded map
T:\,F\to F^{\prime}
. Let
\theta\in[0,1]
. Then,
T
induces a bounded map

T_{\theta}:\,[E,F]_{\theta}\to[E^{\prime},F^{\prime}]_{\theta}

with norm bounded by
\|T\|_{E\to E^{\prime}}^{1-\theta}\|T\|_{F\to F^{\prime}}^{\theta}
. In particular, because composition with coordinate maps or vector bundle trivializations, and multiplication by smooth functions with bounded derivatives yield bounded maps on Sobolev spaces, they also yield maps on interpolation spaces between Sobolev spaces as well. We will make liberal use of this in the following.

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry, and
E\to M
a vector bundle of bounded geometry. Then, for any
s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\theta\in[0,1]
it holds that

\left[H^{s_{0}}(M;E),H^{s_{1}}(M;E)\right]=H^{(1-\theta)s_{0}+\theta s_{1}}(M;E)\;.

with equivalent norms.

The proof, as usual, is a localization argument combined with an appeal to knowledge that the result holds for Euclidean space. But since this is the first appearance of interpolation spaces in this thesis we provide the details.

Proof.

For the sake of simplicity we only treat the case where
E
is the trivial bundle, the case of general vector bundles is entirely analogous. Also, we restrict to
s_{0}=0
and
s_{1}=k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Choose an admissible trivialization
(U_{i}=B_{R}(x_{i}),\kappa_{i},\psi_{i})
of
M
, with
\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{i})\subset B_{r}(x_{i})
for
0<r<R<\mathrm{inj}_{M}
. Let
u\in L^{2}(M)
. Then
u\in H^{k\theta}(M)
if and only if for all
i
the function
u_{i}:=(\psi_{i}u)\circ\kappa_{i}^{-1}
is in
H^{k\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and it holds that
\sum_{i}\|u_{i}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
. Because
H^{k\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=[L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})]_{\theta}
(see for example
[
Tay96
, Section 4.2]
) with equivalent norms this is equivalent to the condition that
u_{i}\in[L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})]_{\theta}
for all
i
with
\sum_{i}\|u_{i}\|_{\theta}<\infty
. Thus it suffices to prove that this last condition is equivalent to
u\in[L^{2}(M),H^{k}(M)]_{\theta}
.

Thus suppose
u\in L^{2}(M)
is such that all
u_{i}
are in
[L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})]_{\theta}
with
\sum_{i}\|u_{i}\|_{\theta}<\infty
. Choose
g_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
with
g_{i}^{\prime}(\theta)=u_{i}
such that
\sum_{i}\|g_{i}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty
. Then, pick a smooth function
\rho\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\mathrm{supp}(\rho)\subseteq B_{R}(0)
and
\rho(x)=1
for
|x|\leq r
. Then
\rho u_{i}=u_{i}
, and it holds that
\mathrm{supp}(\rho)
is supported inside domain of the coordinate maps
\kappa_{i}
. Thus we may define
g_{i}:=(\rho g_{i}^{\prime})\circ\kappa_{i}
. These are elements of
\mathcal{H}(L^{2}(M),H^{k}(M))
, and it still holds that

\sum_{i}\|g_{i}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\lesssim\sum_{i}\|g_{i}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty\;.

Thus
\sum_{i}g_{i}
is an absolutely convergent series in
\mathcal{H}(L^{2}(M),H^{k}(M))
, hence converges to an element
g\in\mathcal{H}(L^{2}(M),H^{k}(M))
. This element then satisfies

g(\theta)=\sum_{i}\left((\rho g_{i}^{\prime})\circ\kappa_{i}\right)(\theta)=\sum_{i}(\rho u_{i})\circ\kappa_{i}=\sum_{i}u_{i}\circ\kappa_{i}=u\;.

Thus
u\in[L^{2}(M),H^{k}(M)]_{\theta}
.

Suppose conversely that
u\in[L^{2}(M),H^{k}(M)]_{\theta}
. Then
u_{i}\in[L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})]_{\theta}
as well, so that it suffices to check the finiteness condition. Due to the uniform local finiteness of the admissible trivialization there exists a decomposition
I=I_{1}\cup\cdots I_{N}
such that the supports of
\psi_{i}
,
i\in I_{j}
, have uniformly positive distance. Since

\sum_{i\in I}\|u_{i}\|_{\theta}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{j}}\|u_{i}\|_{\theta}\right)

it suffices to prove that each
\sum_{i\in I_{j}}\|u_{i}\|_{\theta}
is finite. To that end let
g\in\mathcal{H}(L^{2}(M),H^{k}(M))
have
g(\theta)=u
. Let
r\in\{0,1\}
, and let
H_{r}
be
L^{2}(M)
if
r=0
, and
H^{k}(M)
if
r=1
. In either case
H_{r}
is a Hilbert space, and we can consider the orthogonal projection
P_{i}^{r}
on
H_{r}
to the subspace of functions with support in
\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{i})
. Because the
P_{i}^{r}
have mutually orthogonal ranges within each
I_{j}
, we have

\sum_{i\in I_{j}}\|\psi_{i}g(r+it)\|_{H_{r}}=\sum_{i\in I_{j}}\|\psi_{i}(P_{i}^{r}g(r+it))\|_{H_{r}}\lesssim\sum_{i\in I_{j}}\|P_{i}^{r}g(r+it)\|_{H_{r}}\leq\|g(r+it)\|_{H_{q}}

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Taking the infimum over
g
we conclude that

\sum_{i\in I_{j}}\|u_{i}\|_{\theta}\lesssim\sum_{i\in I_{j}}\|\psi_{i}u\|_{\theta}\lesssim\|u\|_{\theta}\;,

which yields the reverse implication.
∎

An analogous argument could also be given for uniform
C^{m,1}
-domains. Instead we simply appeal to the extension map.

Proposition 4.26
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a uniform
C^{m,1}
-domain. Then

[L^{2}(\overline{\Omega};E),H^{m+1}(\overline{\Omega};E)]_{\theta}=H^{(m+1)\theta}(\overline{\Omega};E)

for all
\theta\in[0,1]
. In particular, if
\overline{\Omega}
is a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry, then

[L^{2}(\overline{\Omega};E),H^{k}(\overline{\Omega};E)]_{\theta}=H^{k\theta}(\overline{\Omega};E)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

Consider the extension map of Proposition
4.23
. It provides a map
e:\,L^{2}(\Omega;E)\to L^{2}(M;E)
that restricts to a bounded map
e:\,H^{m+1}(\overline{\Omega};E)\to H^{m+1}(M;E)
. Moreover, it is right-inverse to the restriction map
r:\,L^{2}(M;E)\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
, which also restricts to a bounded map
r:\,H^{m+1}(M;E)\to H^{m+1}(\overline{\Omega};E)
. Interpolation thus provides maps

\displaystyle e_{\theta}:

\displaystyle\;\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),H^{m+1}(\overline{\Omega};E)\right]_{\theta}\longrightarrow\left[L^{2}(M;E),H^{m+1}(M;E)\right]_{\theta}

\displaystyle r_{\theta}:

\displaystyle\;\left[L^{2}(M;E),H^{m+1}(M;E)\right]_{\theta}\longrightarrow\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),H^{m+1}(\overline{\Omega};E)\right]_{\theta}

such that
r_{\theta}\circ e_{\theta}=\mathrm{id}
. In particular,
r_{\theta}
is surjective. Now, we know that

\left[L^{2}(M;E),H^{m+1}(M;E)\right]_{\theta}=H^{(m+1)\theta}(M;E)\;.

Moreover, by definition the composition

H^{(m+1)\theta}(M;E)\xlongrightarrow{r_{\theta}}\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),H^{m+1}(\overline{\Omega};E)\right]_{\theta}\longrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)

is the restriction map. The latter map is an inclusion by definition, implying that

\ker(r_{\theta})=\left\{u\in H^{(m+1)\theta}(M;E)\,|\,u|_{\Omega}=0\right\}\;.

We conclude that

\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),H^{m+1}(\overline{\Omega};E)\right]_{\theta}=\mathrm{im}(r_{\theta})\cong H^{(m+1)\theta}(M;E)/\ker(r_{\theta})=H^{(m+1)\theta}(\overline{\Omega};E)\;,

proving the claim.
∎

In the above proof the fact that the extension map
e:\,L^{2}(\Omega;E)\to L^{2}(M;E)
restricts to a bounded map
e:\,H^{m+1}(\overline{\Omega};E)\to H^{m+1}(M;E)
-which is satisfied by the extension map of Proposition
4.23
but not that of Proposition
4.22
- is crucial to obtain the map
e_{\theta}
. Without it we could not conclude that
r_{\theta}
is surjective. Thus we would still get an inclusion of
H^{(m+1)\theta}(\overline{\Omega};E)
into
\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),H^{m+1}(\overline{\Omega};E)\right]_{\theta}
, but could not conclude the reverse inclusion. There is no reason, therefore, to expect the conclusion of Proposition
4.26
to hold without sufficient regularity assumptions.

We come to the interpolation properties of the various Sobolev spaces of sections supported inside a given domain. We begin with the general statement without any regularity assumptions.

Proposition 4.27
.

Let
\Omega
be an arbitrary domain. Let
s_{0},s_{1}\geq 0
and
\theta\in[0,1]
, and set
s_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)s_{0}+\theta s_{1}
. Then, there is a chain of inclusions

\tilde{H}^{s_{\theta}}(\Omega;E)\subseteq\left[\tilde{H}^{s_{0}}(\Omega;E),\tilde{H}^{s_{1}}(\Omega;E)\right]_{\theta}\subseteq\left[H^{s_{0}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E),H^{s_{1}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E)\right]_{\theta}\subseteq H^{s_{\theta}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E)\;.

Proof.

For the first inclusion note that
\left[\tilde{H}^{s_{0}}(\Omega;E),\tilde{H}^{s_{1}}(\Omega;E)\right]_{\theta}
is a closed subspace of
\left[H^{s_{0}}(M;E),H^{s_{1}}(M;E)\right]_{\theta}=H^{s_{\theta}}(M;E)
, so that it suffices to show that
\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\subseteq\left[\tilde{H}^{s_{0}}(\Omega;E),\tilde{H}^{s_{1}}(\Omega;E)\right]_{\theta}
. To that end let
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
have compact support
K\subset\Omega
. Let
g\in\mathcal{H}(H^{s_{0}}(M;E),H^{s_{1}}(M;E))
have
f(\theta)=u
. Pick a smooth function
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
with
\psi u=u
. Then,
(\psi\cdot g)(\theta^{\prime})
has compact support in
\Omega
for all
\theta^{\prime}\in[0,1]
, and
(\psi\cdot g)(\theta)=u
. Thus
\psi g\in\mathcal{H}\left(\tilde{H}^{s_{0}}(\Omega;E),\tilde{H}^{s_{1}}(\Omega;E)\right)
, so that
u\in\left[\tilde{H}^{s_{0}}(\Omega;E),\tilde{H}^{s_{1}}(\Omega;E)\right]_{\theta}
.

The second inclusion follows directly from the fact that
\tilde{H}^{s}(\Omega;E)
is a subspace of
H^{s}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E)
for any
s
.

For the third inclusion note simply that

\left[H^{s_{0}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E),H^{s_{1}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E)\right]_{\theta}\subseteq\left[H^{s_{0}}(M;E),H^{s_{1}}(M;E)\right]_{\theta}=H^{s_{\theta}}(M;E)\;,

and that any element of
\left[H^{s_{0}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E),H^{s_{1}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E)\right]_{\theta}
is in particular an element of
H^{s_{0}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E)
, so has support in
\overline{\Omega}
.
∎

In general one has no cause to expect any of these inclusions to be an equality. However, if
\Omega
is sufficiently regular for
\tilde{H}^{s}(\Omega;E)
and
H^{s}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E)
to agree (see Proposition
4.17
), one gets a much nicer result.

Corollary 4.28
.

If
\Omega
is a
C^{0}
-domain, then it holds that

\tilde{H}^{s_{\theta}}(\Omega;E)=\left[\tilde{H}^{s_{0}}(\Omega;E),\tilde{H}^{s_{1}}(\Omega;E)\right]_{\theta}=\left[H^{s_{0}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E),H^{s_{1}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E)\right]_{\theta}=H^{s_{\theta}}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E)

for all
s_{0},s_{1}\geq 0
and
\theta\in[0,1]
.

Recall that
U_{\delta}(\Omega)
and
N_{\delta}(\Omega)
denote the open and closed
\delta
-neighborhood of
\Omega
respectively. An analogous argument to Proposition
4.27
also provides the following statement, which may not be all that appealing but will turn out very useful nonetheless, see Lemma
6.5
below.

Proposition 4.29
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq M
be an arbitrary domain. Let
s_{0},s_{1}\geq 0
and
\theta\in[0,1]
, and set
s_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)s_{0}+\theta s_{1}
. Then, for any
\delta>0
one has inclusions

\tilde{H}^{s_{\theta}}(\Omega;E)\subseteq\left[\tilde{H}^{s_{0}}(U_{\delta}(\Omega);E),\tilde{H}^{s_{1}}(U_{\delta}(\Omega);E)\right]_{\theta}

and

\left[\tilde{H}^{s_{0}}(\Omega;E),\tilde{H}^{s_{1}}(\Omega;E)\right]\subseteq\tilde{H}^{s_{\theta}}(U_{\delta}(\Omega);E)\;.

The analogous statement for
H^{s}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M;E)
and
H^{s}_{N_{\delta}(\Omega)}(M;E)
also holds.

17
The uniformly summable Rellich-Kondrachov theorem

We conclude our discussion of Sobolev spaces on domains with the Rellich-Kondrachov theorem. The observation that the Sobolev embedding on compact domains is not only compact but finitely summable is classical, going back to e.g.
[
Wey12
]
. On manifolds of bounded geometry and domains in them this finite summability is even uniform. This observation will be the foundation of the uniform compactness statements required to extract uniform K-homology classes from uniformly elliptic operators.

Theorem 4.30
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional manifold of bounded geometry,
E\to\Omega
a rank-
m
vector bundle of bounded geometry, and
\Omega\subseteq M
a domain. For all
R\geq 0
,
s\geq 0

t>0
and all
p>\frac{n}{t}
there exist constants
\eta_{R,s,t,p}
and
\eta_{R,s,t,p}^{\Omega}
, dependent only on
M
,
E
and the indicated data, such that the following holds:

(i)

For all compact subsets
K\subseteq M
with
\mathrm{diam}(K)\leq R
the inclusion

H^{s+t}_{K}(M;E)\xlongrightarrow{j}H^{s}_{K}(M;E)

is
p
-summable with
||j||_{p}\leq\eta_{R,s,t,p}
.

(ii)

For all compact subsets
K\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
with
\mathrm{diam}(K)\leq R
the inclusion

H^{s+t}_{K}(\overline{\Omega};E)\xlongrightarrow{j}H^{s}_{K}(\overline{\Omega};E)

is
p
-summable with
||j||_{p}\leq\eta_{R,s,t,p}^{\Omega}
.

We adapt the argument given in
[
Rav12
]
. First, we show the statement for vector-valued functions on the torus. For manifolds of bounded geometry the statement can be reduced to that case via local trivializations. The case of domains then follows via extension.

Lemma 4.31
.

Let
\mathbb{T}^{n}_{r}
be the flat torus obtained by identifying the opposite faces of an
n
-dimensional cube with side length
r>0
. Let
s\geq 0
and
t>0
. Then, the embedding
j:\,H^{s+t}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{r};\mathbb{C}^{m})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{r};\mathbb{C}^{m})
is
p
-Schatten class for all
p>\frac{n}{t}
.

Proof.

The Sobolev space
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{r};\mathbb{C}^{m})
for
s\geq 0
consists of those
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{r};\mathbb{C}^{m})
for which
\langle u,u\rangle_{H^{s}}<\infty
, where the
H^{s}
-inner product is given by

\langle u,v\rangle_{H^{s}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\langle\hat{u}_{k},\hat{v}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\cdot\langle k\rangle^{2s}\quad\mathrm{where}\quad\langle k\rangle:=\left(1+\left(\frac{2\pi||k||}{r}\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}\;.

The elements
f^{s}_{k,i}(x):=r^{-1/2}\langle k\rangle^{-s}\cdot e^{2\pi i\langle k,x\rangle/r}\cdot e_{i}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
i=1,\cdots,m
, form a maximal orthonormal system. Here
e_{i}
is the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
standard basis vector of
\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

The
p
-norm of
j
is obtained from the spectral values of the operator
|j|=\sqrt{j^{*}j}
acting on
H^{s+t}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{r},\mathbb{C}^{m})
. We have
j(f^{s+t}_{k,i})=f^{s+t}_{k,i}=\langle k\rangle^{-t}f^{s}_{k,i}
, from which it follows that

\langle f^{s+t}_{k^{\prime},i^{\prime}},(j^{*}j)f^{s+t}_{k,i}\rangle_{H^{s+t}}=\langle jf^{s+t}_{k^{\prime},i^{\prime}},jf^{s+t}_{k,i}\rangle_{H^{s}}=\langle k\rangle^{-2t}\cdot\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}\;,

whence

(j^{*}j)f^{s+t}_{k,i}=\langle k\rangle^{-2t}\cdot f^{s+t}_{k,i}\;.

The
p
-norm of
j
is therefore equal to

||j||_{p}=\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}m\cdot\langle k\rangle^{-pt}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\;.

To see that this sum converges for all
p>\frac{t}{n}
, rearrange the estimate

\left(1+\left(\frac{2\pi k_{1}}{r}\right)^{2}\right)\cdots\left(1+\left(\frac{2\pi k_{n}}{r}\right)^{2}\right)\leq\left(1+\left(\frac{2\pi\|k\|}{r}\right)^{2}\right)^{n}=\langle k\rangle^{2n}

to obtain

\langle k\rangle^{-pt}\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}+\left(\frac{2\pi k_{i}}{r}\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{pt}{2n}}\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+|k_{i}|)^{-\frac{pt}{n}}\;.

The series
\sum_{k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+|k_{i}|)^{-pt/n}
converges if and only if
p>\frac{n}{t}
. For these
p
it then follows that

||j||_{p}^{p}\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+|k_{i}|)^{-\frac{pt}{n}}=\left(\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+a)^{-\frac{pt}{n}}\right)^{n}<\infty\;.

∎

We use this local computation to obtain the theorem globally on manifolds of bounded geometry.

Proof of Thm.
4.30
.

Ad (i): Let
(U_{i},\kappa_{i},\psi_{i},\phi_{i})
be an admissible trivialization of
M
and
E
. There is
N\in\mathbb{N}
-dependent on
R
but not the specific
K
- such that
K
intersects at most
N
of the
U_{i}
. Label those
U_{i}
as
U_{1},\cdots,U_{N}
. It holds that

||j||_{p}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}||j(\psi_{i}\cdot)||_{p}\;.

Multiplication by
\psi_{i}
maps
H^{s+t}_{K}(M;E)
to
H^{s+t}_{K_{i}}(U_{i};E)
, where
K_{i}:=K\cap\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{i})
is compact in
U_{i}
, with norm bounded independently of

by bounds on the first

\psi_{i}
for
\mathbb{N}\ni k\geq s+t
. Using the coordinate maps and trivializations, transfer to vector-valued functions on Euclidean space via

\textstyle{H^{s+t}_{K_{i}}(U_{i};E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{i}\circ(\cdot)\circ\kappa_{i}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{H^{s+t}_{\kappa_{i}(K_{i})}\left(\kappa_{i}(U_{i}),\mathbb{C}^{m}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{H^{s}_{K_{i}}(U_{i};E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{i}\circ(\cdot)\circ\kappa_{i}^{-1}}
\textstyle{H^{s}_{\kappa_{i}(K_{i})}\left(\kappa_{i}(U_{i}),\mathbb{C}^{m}\right)}

The horizontal arrows are isomorphism with norms bounded independently of

\phi_{i}
and
\kappa_{i}
. It therefore suffices to prove that the right vertical arrow is
p

of

as the restriction of the inclusion map

||j||_{p}\leq\eta_{R,s,t,p}:=C(n,k)\cdot N\cdot C_{k}(\psi)\cdot C_{k}(\phi)\cdot C_{k}(\kappa)\cdot J(r,m,t,p)<\infty\;.

Here
k\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies
k\geq t+s
,

is an

\psi_{i}
,
C_{k}(\phi)
and
C_{k}(\kappa)
are defined analogously,

is a universal constant

65

65

This universal constant arises from the specifics of how multiplication by

\phi_{i}
and

can be bounded in Sobolev norm. These norms are bounded by bounds on the derivatives of these maps, times constants coming from application of the chain rule, etc.
, and

is the

j:\,H^{s+t}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{r},\mathbb{C}^{m})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{r},\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

Ad (ii): For

pick a closed ball
\tilde{K}
of radius

\tilde{K}
. We get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{H^{s+t}(M;E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{H^{s+t}\left(\overline{\Omega},E\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

. Use Lemma

to produce a function

,

, and such that

and can be bounded by a constant dependent on

but not the specific

, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

. Then, the inclusion

can be factored as

H_{K}^{s+t}(\overline{\Omega};E)\xrightarrow{\sigma}H^{s+t}(M;E)\xrightarrow{\psi}H^{s+t}_{\tilde{K}}(M;E)\xrightarrow{\tilde{j}}H^{s}_{\tilde{K}}(M;E)\xrightarrow{r}H^{s}(\overline{\Omega};E)\;.

The inclusion

is

-summable by item (i), so we get that

is also

and by construction of

and item (i) the right-hand side can be bounded by a constant
\eta^{\Omega}_{R,s,t,p}
depending only on
