.
∎

Chapter 5
Uniformly elliptic operators

It was observed by Špakula that Dirac-type operators over manifolds of bounded geometry define classes in uniform K-homology
[
Špa09
]
. Such operators are examples of
uniformly elliptic operators
, meaning that their coefficients are uniformly bounded and their principal symbol is not only invertible but invertible in a uniform way. Engel then extended Špakula’s result to uniformly elliptic pseudo-differential operators
[
Eng18a
]
. In this chapter we will investigate uniformly elliptic operators over domains in manifolds of bounded geometry as well as their boundary conditions. We focus on differential operators as opposed to pseudo-differential ones, though see Subsection
23.5
. The analysis of uniformly elliptic operators and their boundary conditions developed in this chapter combined with that of the last chapter on Sobolev spaces will enable us to prove the existence of uniform K-homology classes defined by these operators in Chapter 6.

There are two reasonable approaches for treating differential operators with Hilbert space techniques. The first is operator-theoretic: A differential operator may be viewed as an unbounded operator on
L^{2}
-space with domain the space of smooth compactly supported sections. The second is via Sobolev spaces, between which differential operators map as bounded operators. The two approaches turn out to be the same for uniformly elliptic operators on manifolds of bounded geometry. Indeed, any such operator -when viewed as an unbounded operator on
L^{2}
- has a canonical closed extension, whose domain is the Sobolev space of degree given by the order of the operator. The nice behavior of Sobolev spaces thus implies a nice
L^{2}
-theory for elliptic operators.

If we consider manifolds with boundary instead, or more generally domains in manifold, the situation becomes more complicated. Due to the presence of a boundary, there need not be a canonical extension, but there might be many. One should think of these extensions as arising from different boundary conditions. These extensions may or may not be contained in Sobolev space. Every boundary condition satisfies so-called
interior regularity
, stating roughly that in the interior the domain of any boundary condition coincides with Sobolev space. Further, we consider
regular
boundary condtition, whose domain is fully contained in Sobolev space.
66
66

66

Technically, we will call boundary conditions with
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq H^{m}(\overline{\Omega})

semi-regular
, with the term
regular
being reserved for boundary conditions that additionally satisfy
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e}^{*})\subseteq H^{m}(\overline{\Omega})
as well. For the purpose of this introductory discussion we skim over this distinction.
While interior regularity suffices to prove that uniformly elliptic operators define uniform K-homology classes on the interior, regularity is needed to obtain relative uniform K-homology classes up to the boundary. This is because the uniform compactness properties of Sobolev spaces, in particular the uniformly summable Rellich-Kondrachov theorem, are necessary to obtain analogous uniform compactness statements for the domains of boundary conditions, and these are needed to prove the existence of uniform K-homology classes.

Boundary conditions for uniformly elliptic operators on manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry and their boundary conditions have been discussed in the literature already. The theory of boundary conditions and regularity for first-order elliptic operators on non-compact manifolds with boundary has been developed Bär and Bandara
[
BB22
]
, building on earlier work for compact boundaries
[
BB12
]
. In
[
GN14
]
Große and Nakad treats the special case of Dirac operators on spin
c
manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry. Schick formulated regularity for uniformly elliptic operators on manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry in terms of the existence of a uniform pseudo-differential solution operator, and applied this to a discussion of the Laplacian and
L^{2}
-Hodge theory in the presence of boundaries
[
Sch98a
]
. For second order operators in this setting various notions of regularity have been discussed and compared by Große and Nistor
[
GN20
]
. We will discuss how these regularity notions relate to the one used in this thesis below.

In Section 5.1 we introduce uniformly elliptic operators on manifolds of bounded geometry, and on domains in these manifolds. Section 5.2 initiates the study of boundary conditions. Besides a proof of interior regularity it contains a discussion of certain grading and locality properties that will also be relevant to the existence of uniform K-homology classes. In Section 5.3 we study the regularity notion used here, and also connect it to those of
[
BB22
]
,
[
Sch98a
]
, and
[
GN20
]
. Section 5.4 is devoted to examples. The list of examples is by no means exhaustive, but rather supposed to illustrate the different properties of boundary conditions that have been introduced in the preceding sections. Lastly, Section 5.5 contains a brief account of the finite-propagation property of wave operators and more general functions of a local boundary condition for a first-order operator.

18
Definition

We begin with the definition of uniformly elliptic operators over manifolds of bounded geometry and domains in such manifolds. Let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry, and
E,F\to M
vector bundles of bounded geometry. Let
D:\,\Gamma_{c}(M;E)\to\Gamma_{c}(M;F)
be a differential operator of order
m
with principal symbol
\sigma
. Let
(U_{i},\kappa_{i},\phi_{i}^{E},\phi_{i}^{F})
be an admissible trivialization of
M
,
E
and
F
. Consider the differential operator

D_{i}=\left(\phi^{F}_{i}D(\phi^{E}_{i})^{-1}\right)\circ\kappa_{i}^{-1}=:\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}a_{i}^{\alpha}\partial^{\alpha}

over
\kappa_{i}(U_{i})
. Its principal symbol is given by
\sigma_{i}:=(\phi^{F}_{i}\sigma\phi^{E}_{i})^{-1}\circ\kappa_{i}^{-1}
.

Definition 5.1
.

The differential operator
D:\,\Gamma_{c}(M;E)\to\Gamma_{c}(M;F)
is called
bounded
if for all
k\geq 0
there exists
C_{k}\geq 0
such that

\left|\partial^{\beta}a_{i}^{\alpha}(x)\right|\leq C_{k}

for all
i\in I
,
|\alpha|\leq m
and
|\beta|\leq k
.

Moreover,
D
is called
uniformly elliptic
if
D
is bounded, the principal symbol
\sigma(\xi)
is invertible for all
\xi\in T^{*}M
,
\xi\neq 0
, and there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\left|\sigma_{i}(x,\xi)^{-1}\right|\leq C\cdot(1+|\xi|)^{-m}

for all
i\in I
.

Since the transition functions of an admissible trivialization have uniformly bounded derivatives, uniform ellipticity is independent of the choice of admissible trivialization.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq M
be a domain. A differential operator
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;F)
is called
uniformly elliptic over
\overline{\Omega}
if there exists a uniformly elliptic differential operator
\tilde{D}:\,\Gamma_{c}(M;E)\to\Gamma_{c}(M;F)
such that
\tilde{D}u=Du
for all
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
.

This rather extrinsic definition of uniformly elliptic operators has the advantage that we can transfer knowledge of uniformly elliptic operators over manifolds of bounded geometry rather directly to such operators over domains. Thus it is technically convenient. Moreover, since this definition does not require use of any coordinates on the domain directly, it works for arbitrarily rough domains. However, for manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry there is also an intrinsic definition of uniformly elliptic operator. It is analogous to Definition
5.1
, only that uniform boundedness and ellipticity is also required in boundary charts. This definition is essentially equivalent to that given here. More concretely, we have the following.

Lemma B
.
67
67

67

The alphabetic index means that this lemma was not present in the original version of this thesis. Alphabetic indexing was chosen so that the numerical indexing is consistent with the original version.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be an intrinsically defined manifold with boundary and bounded geometry, and
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega,E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega,F)
a differential operator mapping between sections of bounded-geometry vector bundles over
\overline{\Omega}
.

Suppose
M
is a manifold of bounded geometry containing an isometric copy of
\overline{\Omega}
as a bounded-geometry domain. Then, if
D
is uniformly elliptic in the in the sense of Definition
5.2
, it is also uniformly elliptic in the intrinsic sense.

Conversely, if
D
is uniformly elliptic in the intrinsic sense, there exists a manifold
M
of bounded geometry containing an isometric copy of
\overline{\Omega}
as a bounded-geometry domain, such that
D
admits a uniformly elliptic extension to
M
, i.e.
D
uniformly elliptic in the sense of Definition
5.2
.

Proof.

The first statement follows directly from the fact that any uniformly elliptic extension of
D
to
M
must necessarily satisfy the bounds necessary for the intrinsic definition of being uniformly elliptic.

For the reverse implication, suppose for simplicity that
\overline{\Omega}
has product structure on a uniformly thick collar of the boundary. This can always be arranged via a bi-Lipschitz equivalence
[
Sch98a
, Proposition 7.3]
, and the property of being uniformly elliptic is preserved under this change of metric. Form
M:=\overline{\Omega}\cup_{\partial\Omega}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\partial\Omega)
by attaching cylinders to the boundary. This is a manifold of bounded geometry containing
\overline{\Omega}
as a bounded-geometry domain. Suppose
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega,E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega,F)
is intrinsically uniformly elliptic. Up to a uniformly bounded homotopy
E
and
F
are constant in normal direction on a uniformly thick tubular neighborhood of
\partial\Omega
in
M
. By keeping them constant in that direction,
E
and
F
can be extended to bounded-geometry vector bundles over
M
. Moreover, near the boundary we may write
D=a_{t}\partial_{t}+A_{t}
, with
A_{t}
a smooth family of differential operators over
\partial\Omega
. Since
D
is intrinsically uniformly elliptic,
a_{t}
and
A_{t}
can be extended to
[0,\delta)\times\partial\Omega
for some
\delta>0
in such a way that
a_{t}\partial_{t}+A_{t}
is also uniformly elliptic for those
t
. Fix some smooth
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to[0,1]
with
\psi(t)=1
for
t\leq\frac{\delta}{3}
, and
\psi(t)=0
for
t\geq\frac{2\delta}{3}
. Then

\tilde{D}=\psi(t)\cdot\left(a_{t}\partial_{t}+A_{t}\right)+(1-\psi(t))\cdot\left(a_{0}\partial_{t}+A_{0}\right)

is a uniformly elliptic extension of
D
, whence
D
is uniformly elliptic in the sense of Definition
5.2
.
∎

Example 5.3
.

The Laplace operator, the spin and Clifford-linear Dirac operator, the Euler characteristic operator, and related geometric operators over a manifold of bounded geometry are uniformly elliptic, hence so are their restrictions to arbitrary domains. Moreover, strongly elliptic operators over domains in Euclidean space (see e.g.
[
McL00
]
) are uniformly elliptic in our sense.

Associated to any differential operator
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega,F)
there is a differential operator
D^{\dagger}:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;F)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega,E)
called the
formal adjoint
of
D
, which is uniquely defined by the requirement that

\langle Du,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega;F)}=\langle u,D^{\dagger}v\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega;E)}

hold for all
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega,E)
and
v\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega,F)
. The operator
D
is called
symmetric
(or
formally self-adjoint
) if
D=D^{\dagger}
. Clearly it holds that
(D^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=D
. The coefficients of
D^{\dagger}
in local charts are given by expressions involving the adjoints of those of
D
as well as their derivatives. In particular the principal symbol
\sigma_{D^{\dagger}}
of
D^{\dagger}
is the point-wise adjoint
\sigma_{D}^{*}
of that of
D
. Then, the following is easily seen by taking the formal adjoint of any uniformly elliptic extension.

Lemma 5.4
.

A differential operator over
\overline{\Omega}
is uniformly elliptic if and only if its formal adjoint is.

Uniformly elliptic theory over manifolds of bounded geometry is well-developed. The essential property of elliptic operators over closed manifolds is that they admit pseudo-differential quasi-inverses modulo smoothing operators, and the same is true over manifolds of bounded geometry as well. The precise set-up depends on the pseudo-differential calculus one wishes to use, see for example
[
Kor91
]
,
[
Shu92
]
,
[
Tay08
]
or
[
Eng18a
]
. We will not employ pseudo-differential techniques in this thesis, but one consequence of the existence of a pseudo-differential quasi-inverse will be important to us, namely elliptic regularity. It provides a bound for the Sobolev norm of a section in terms of lower-degree Sobolev norms of that section and its image under the uniformly elliptic operator. Given our definition of uniformly elliptic operators over domains it is easily deduced that this form of regularity also holds for these operators, at least as long as the section in question is supported away from the boundary. This is regularity estimate is the core of interior regularity, which will be discussed below.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
D
be uniformly elliptic over
\overline{\Omega}
. Then, for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
there exists a constant
C_{s}\geq 0
such that for all
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
it holds that

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s}(M;E)}\leq C_{s}\cdot\left(\|u\|_{H^{s-m}(M;E)}+\|Du\|_{H^{s-m}(M;F)}\right)\;.

Proof.

This follows from applying the fundamental elliptic estimate (for example
[
Eng18a
, Theorem 6.6]
) to any uniformly elliptic extension of
D
to
M
.
∎

We draw explicit attention to the fact that the interior regularity estimate uses the norms on
H^{s}(M)
instead of
H^{s}(\overline{\Omega})
. Of course, these are equivalent if
s
is an integer.

19
Boundary conditions for uniformly elliptic operators

In this section we study the properties of uniformly elliptic operators, viewed as unbounded operators on the Hilbert space of
L^{2}
-sections. Standard facts about unbounded operators are collected in Appendix
11
, and will be used without reference. In particular, the notions of closed extensions and adjoints of unbounded operators will be used heavily in our discussion of boundary conditions.

Let
M
a manifold of bounded geometry,
E,F\to M
vector bundles of bounded geometry, and
\Omega\subseteq M
a domain. Let
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;F)
be a differential operator. We view
D
as a densely defined, unbounded operator from
\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\subset L^{2}(\Omega;E)
to
L^{2}(\Omega;F)
. Similarly, the formal adjoint
D^{\dagger}
is an unbounded operator from
L^{2}(\Omega;F)
to
L^{2}(\Omega;E)
.

Definition 5.6
.

A
boundary condition
for
D
s a closed extension
D_{e}
of
D
, such that the Hilbert space adjoint
D_{e}^{*}
is a closed extension of
D^{\dagger}
.

It may not be clear how this definition captures what we intuitively understand as a boundary condition. We will see in Proposition
5.8
below that -at least for uniformly elliptic operators- boundary conditions do in fact only depend on the behavior on the boundary. But there is also a more sophisticated description of this phenomenon. Indeed, in favorable circumstances there is a one-to-one correspondence between boundary conditions for an elliptic operator
D
in the above sense and closed subspaces of a certain Sobolev-like space on the boundary, so that a section is in the domain of the boundary condition if and only if its restriction to the boundary lies in the corresponding subspace. In the case of first-order operators and compact boundaries this is described in
[
BB12
]
, and extended to non-compact boundaries in
[
BB22
]
. Dirac operators on spin
c
manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry are treated in
[
GN14
]
. Higher-order elliptic operators on compact manifolds with boundary are treated in
[
BGS23
]
. While this description of boundary conditions is conceptually satisfying the definition in terms of closed extensions suffices for our purposes.

Definition 5.7
.

The
minimal boundary condition

D_{\min}
of
D
is the closure of
D
as an unbounded operator from
L^{2}(\Omega;E)
to
L^{2}(\Omega;F)
. The
maximal boundary condition

D_{\max}
is the (Hilbert space) adjoint
D_{\max}=(D^{\dagger})^{*}
of
D^{\dagger}
as an unbounded operator from
L^{2}(\Omega;F)
to
L^{2}(\Omega;E)
.

A section
u\in L^{2}(\Omega;E)
is in the domain of
D_{\min}
if and only if there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
u_{n}\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
, such that
||u-u_{n}||_{L^{2}}\to 0
and
\sup_{n}||Du_{n}||_{L^{2}}<\infty
. Equivalently,
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})
is the closure of
\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
in the graph norm
||u||_{D}=||u||_{L^{2}}+||Du||_{L^{2}}
, and
D_{\min}
is the unique extension of
D
to this closure. A section
u\in L^{2}(\Omega;E)
is in the domain of
D_{\max}
if and only if there exists
v\in L^{2}(M;F)
such that for all
w\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;F)
it holds that

\langle u,D^{\dagger}w\rangle_{E}=\langle v,w\rangle_{F}\;.

Then
D_{\max}u=v
.

Both
D_{\min}
and
D_{\max}
are closed extensions of
D
, and it holds that
D_{\min}\subseteq D_{\max}
. Moreover, it holds that
D_{\max}=(D^{\dagger})^{*}=(D_{\min}^{\dagger})^{*}
, which implies that both the minimal and the maximal boundary conditions are indeed boundary conditions in the sense of Definition
5.6
. Moreover, any
D_{e}
boundary condition for
D
satisfies
D_{\min}\subseteq D_{e}\subseteq D_{\max}
. In fact, a closed extension satisfies Definition
5.6
if and only if this condition holds. This explains the nomenclature of minimal and maximal boundary condition.

The following proposition states that on the interior of the domain
\Omega
there is no difference between minimal and maximal boundary condition. Consequently, all boundary conditions do indeed behave the same away from the boundary.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;F)
be uniformly elliptic over
\overline{\Omega}
. Let
u\in L^{2}(M;E)
have
\mathrm{supp}(u)\subset\Omega
. Then

u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})\;.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{D}:\,\Gamma_{c}(M;E)\to\Gamma_{c}(M;F)
be a uniformly elliptic extension of
D
. It holds that
\mathrm{dom}(\tilde{D}_{\min})=\mathrm{dom}(\tilde{D}_{\max})

[
Shu92
, Proposition 4.1]
. If
u\in L^{2}(\Omega;E)
has support inside
\Omega
, then it is straight-forward to see that
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})
if and only if
u\in\mathrm{dom}(\tilde{D}_{\max})
. Thus, in that case there exists a sequence
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\Gamma_{c}(M;E)
converging to
u
in the graph norm of
\tilde{D}
. The supports of the
v_{n}
can be arranged to lie inside
\Omega
via cut-offs. Since the graph norm of
\tilde{D}
on
\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
coincides with the one of
D
, it follows that
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})
.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

The statement of Proposition
5.8
holds for first-order differential operators without the assumption of ellipticity, see for example
[
HR00
, Lemma 10.2.5]
. However, it can fail for non-elliptic higher-order operators, see for example
[
Tau10
]
.

The next proposition identifies the domain of the minimal boundary condition with the Sobolev space
\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega;E)
. This essential fact allows for the transfer of knowledge about Sobolev spaces to knowledge about boundary conditions of uniformly elliptic operators. Note that by Lemma
4.15
we could also use the Sobolev space
H_{0}^{m}(\Omega;E)
instead. However, since we use the
H^{m}(M;E)
-norm instead of the
H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
-norm here, and the difference between the two becomes substantial when we consider fractional degrees later, we prefer to use
\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega;E)
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;F)
be uniformly elliptic over
\overline{\Omega}
. Then, it holds that

\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})=\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega;E)

with equivalent norms.

Proof.

The estimate of Proposition
5.5
for the special case
s=m
expresses that

||u||_{H^{m}(M;E)}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{2}(M;E)}+\|Du\|_{L^{2}(M;E)}=||u||_{D}

for
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
. Conversely,
D
is bounded over
\overline{\Omega}
, we have
||Du||_{L^{2}(M;E)}\lesssim||u||_{H^{m}(M;E)}
and hence
||u||_{D}\lesssim||u||_{H^{m}(M;E)}
for
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
. We conclude that the
H^{m}(M;E)
-norm and the graph norm of
D
are equivalent norms on
\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
, meaning that closures of
\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
with respect to the two norms agree. This proves the claim.
∎

We refer to the combination of Propositions
5.8
and
5.10
as
interior regularity
. It expresses that for a uniformly elliptic operator a section supported in the interior is in the domain of any boundary condition if and only if it is in the Sobolev space
\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega;E)
, and that the graph norm and the Sobolev norm are equivalent for these sections. Interior regularity allows the transfer of the nice properties of Sobolev spaces to the domains of arbitrary boundary conditions, at least over the interior. This will be an important ingredient in our discussion of the uniform K-homology classes of boundary conditions for uniformly elliptic operators.

Let us already note a first consequence of interior regularity concerning multiplication by functions supported in the interior.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
D
be uniformly elliptic over
\overline{\Omega}
, and let
f\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
. Multiplication by
f
yields a bounded map

\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})\longrightarrow\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})\;,\;u\longmapsto f\cdot u

with norm proportional
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
.

Proof.

Consider the subspace of
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})
containing all sections supported at least distance
\delta
from
\partial\Omega
for some
0<\delta<d(\mathrm{supp}(f),\partial\Omega)
, and let
P:\,\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})\to\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})
be the orthogonal projection onto that subspace.
68
68

68

Here we consider
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})
as a Hilbert space under the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{D}=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle\cdot,|D_{\max}|\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}
, which induces the norm
\|\cdot\|_{D}
.
Note that if
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})
lies in the kernel of
P
, then its support is contained in the
\delta
-neighborhood of
\partial\Omega
. By Proposition
5.8

\mathrm{im}(P)
is contained in
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})
, so that
u\mapsto fu,\,\mathrm{im}(P)\to\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})
is bounded with norm bound proportional to
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
by Proposition
5.10
and Lemma
4.13
. On the other hand, if
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})
lies in the kernel of
P
, then its support is contained in the
\delta
-neighborhood of
\partial\Omega
, whence
fu=0
. We conclude that
u\mapsto fu
factors as

\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})\xlongrightarrow{P}\mathrm{im}(P)\xlongrightarrow{f}\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})

with claimed norm bound. The claim follows.
∎

From the previous proposition we can draw conclusions about arbitrary boundary conditions. Indeed, we have seen that multiplication by a smooth function with compact support in the interior maps
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})
to
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})
. Since the domain of any boundary condition is contained in that of the maximal boundary condition, we conclude the following.

Corollary 5.12
.

Suppose
D
is uniformly elliptic, and
D_{e}
a boundary condition for
D
. Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(M)
. Then, multiplication by
f
is a bounded map

\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\longrightarrow\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})

with norm proportional to
\|f\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
.

In order for a boundary condition to define a class in certain uniform K-homology groups it needs to satisfy certain properties. Besides regularity, which will be discussed in the next section, this concerns compatibility with (multi)gradings, but also with multiplication by functions. We conclude this section with a discussion of these compatibility conditions.

Definition 5.13
.

Let
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
be a differential operator acting on sections of a
p
-multigraded vector bundle,
p\geq 0
. Suppose that
D
is odd and
p
-multigraded. A boundary condition
D_{e}
is called
odd
if the grading operator
\kappa
of
E
preserves
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
, and
D_{e}\kappa=-\kappa D_{e}
. The boundary condition is called
p
-multigraded
if every multigrading operator
\varepsilon_{j}
of
E
preserves
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
and
D_{e}\varepsilon_{j}=\varepsilon_{j}D_{e}
.

The following lemma is deduced rather directly from the definition of the adjoint boundary condition.

Lemma 5.14
.

A boundary condition is odd resp. mulitgraded if and only if its adjoint is odd resp. multigraded.

Compatibility with multiplication by smooth functions is expressed through the following notion of locality.

Definition 5.15
.

A boundary condition
D_{e}
for
D
is called
local
if
f\cdot\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
.

The nomenclature of calling such boundary conditions local is adopted from
[
BDT89
]
. However, we advise caution that for higher-order operators there can be differential boundary conditions that are not local in this sense (see Example
5.31
below). This situation does not appear for first-order operators (which is the case considered in
[
BDT89
]
). There any differential boundary condition must have order
0
, i.e. be given by the kernel of a bundle map on the boundary, and these boundary conditions are easily seen to be local. The first-order case is also convenient in the following way.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
D_{e}
be a boundary condition for the first-order differential operator
D
. Then,
D_{e}
is local if and only if
D_{e}^{*}
is local.

Proof.

The roles of
D_{e}
and
D_{e}^{*}
are symmetric, so it suffices to prove that if
D_{e}
is local, so is
D_{e}^{*}
. It further suffices to prove that any real-valued function
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
preserves
\mathrm{dom}(D^{*}_{e})
. Let
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{e}^{*})
. We need to produce a
v\in L^{2}(\Omega)
such for every
w\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
it holds that
\langle v,w\rangle=\langle fu,Dw\rangle
. Write

\langle fu,Dw\rangle=\langle u,fDw\rangle=\langle u,D(fw)\rangle+\langle u,[f,D]w\rangle=\langle fD_{e}^{*}u,w\rangle+\langle u,[f,D]w\rangle\;.

Since
D
is first-order
[f,D]=-i\sigma_{D}(df)
extends to a bounded operator on
L^{2}(\Omega)
, so that
[f,D_{e}]
is bounded as well. Thus,
v:=fD_{e}^{*}u+[f,D_{e}]^{*}u
is a well-defined element of
L^{2}(\Omega)
, for which it holds that

\langle fu,Dw\rangle=\langle fD_{e}^{*}u,w\rangle+\langle u,[f,D]w\rangle=\langle v,w\rangle\;.

This concludes the proof.
∎

For higher-order operators the proof fails because
[f,D]
is no longer a bounded operator. Indeed Proposition
5.16
is not generally true for higher-order operators, see Example
5.27
for an example.

20
Regularity of boundary conditions

We come to regularity of boundary conditions. Regularity is crucial for our treatment of uniform K-homology classes, as it allows for the transfer of the favorable properties of Sobolev spaces to the entire domain of the boundary conditions, as opposed to interior regularity, which provides these properties only in the interior.

Throughout we consider a domain
\Omega
in the manifold
M
of bounded geometry, and the uniformly elliptic operator
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;F)
over
\overline{\Omega}
mapping between sections of the bounded-geometry vector bundles
E,F\to M
.

Definition 5.17
.

The boundary condition
D_{e}
is called
semi-regular
if there is a bounded inclusion
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\hookrightarrow H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
with equivalent norms. It is called
regular
if both
D_{e}
and
D_{e}^{*}
are semi-regular.

We phrase the definition this way to draw explicit attention to the fact that the inclusion
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\hookrightarrow H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
comes with a norm equivalence attached. It is a consequence of the open mapping theorem that an inclusion
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
as a closed subspace already gives rise to such a norm equivalence.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
D_{e}
be a boundary condition. Then
D_{e}
is semi-regular if and only if
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
is a closed subspace of
H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
.

Proof.

If
D_{e}
is semi-regular, then
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
with equivalent norms on the image. As the domain of a closed extension
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
is complete with respect to the graph norm, and hence also with respect to the Sobolev norm. Thus
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
is closed.

Suppose conversely that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
is closed. Then,
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
is complete with respect to both the graph norm and the Sobolev norm. Moreover, because
D
induces bounded maps between Sobolev spaces it holds that

\|u\|_{D}=\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;E)}+\|Du\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;F)}\lesssim\|u\|_{H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)}

for
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
. Consequently the identity map is bounded as a map from
(\mathrm{dom}(D_{e}),\|\cdot\|_{H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)})
to
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e}),\|\cdot\|_{D})
. As a bounded bijection between Banach spaces it is already a Banach space isomorphism as well, meaning there is the converse estimate

\|u\|_{H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;E)}+\|Du\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;F)}=\|u\|_{D}

(30)

for all
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
.
∎

To reiterate: A semi-regular boundary condition automatically comes with a regularity estimate as in (
30
). Conversely, regularity estimates are sufficient to provide semi-regularity of a boundary condition. Let us state this in the particular case of pseudo-differential boundary conditions.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry. Let
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;F)
be a uniformly elliptic operator of order
m
over
\overline{\Omega}
. Moreover, let
P:\,\Gamma_{c}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega})\to\Gamma_{c}(\partial\Omega;V)
be a uniform pseudo-differential operator
69
69

69

The precise pseudo-differential calculus is irrelevant here, any of the calculi of
[
Kor91
]
,
[
Tay08
]
or
[
Eng18a
]
work. All that is needed is that
P
gives rise to a bounded map
H^{m-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega})\to H^{m-\ell-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega,V)
.
of order
0\leq\ell\leq m-1
. Suppose that there exists
C>0
such that

\|u\|_{H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)}\leq C\cdot\left(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;E)}+\|Du\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;F)}+\|Pu|_{\partial\Omega}\|_{H^{m-\ell-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega;V)}\right)

for all
u\in H^{k}(\overline{\Omega};E)
. Then

\mathrm{dom}(D_{P}):=\left\{u\in H^{k}(\overline{\Omega};E)\,|\,Pu|_{\partial\Omega}=0\right\}\subseteq L^{2}(\Omega;E)

defines a semi-regular boundary condition for
D
.

Proof.

The space
\mathrm{dom}(D_{P})\subseteq H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
is closed in the Sobolev norm. We need to prove that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{P})
is closed in the graph-norm
\|\cdot\|_{D}
, i.e. that it indeed is a boundary condition. If this is done, semi-regularity follows from Lemma
5.18
. To that end note first that the Sobolev norm dominates the graph norm. Conversely, for
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{P})
it holds that
Pu|_{\partial\Omega}=0
so that the assumed regularity estimate for
u
reads

\|u\|_{H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;E)}+\|Du\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;E)}=\|u\|_{D}\;.

We conclude that
H^{m}
-norm and graph norm are equivalent on
\mathrm{dom}(D_{P})
. Since
\mathrm{dom}(D_{P})
is closed in the former, it is also closed in the latter. This proves the claim.
∎

Norm estimates as in Proposition
5.19
have been studied extensively. There are different approaches to obtain them. One is through the existence of a pseudo-differential quasi-inverse (see for example
[
Hör63
]
for the classical case). This is analogous to regularity for elliptic operators on manifolds without boundary, where the existence of a pseudo-differential quasi-inverse also gave rise to a regularity estimate. In the case of manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry this approach to regularity of boundary conditions has been studied by Schick in
[
Sch98a
]
. There a boundary condition admitting a uniform pseudo-differential quasi-inverse is called
uniformly elliptic
, and it is shown that such boundary conditions admit a regularity estimate.

Another approach is to require the existence of regularity estimate of associated local problems or problems with frozen coefficients that are suitable uniform. From this one then produces a global regularity estimate (see for example
[
Tay96
, Chapter 5]
for the compact case). For second-order operators on manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry this approach (among others) was investigated by Große and Nistor in
[
GN20
]
. For example they present a uniform version of the classical Shapiro-Lopatinski condition, and show that any boundary condition satisfying this condition admits a regularity estimate.

Let us also mention the work of Bär and Bandara on boundary conditions for first-order elliptic operators on manifolds with non-compact operators
[
BB22
]
. This extends earlier work by Bär and Ballmann treating the case of compact boundaries
[
BB12
]
, and by Große and Nakad treating Dirac operators on spin
c
manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry
[
GN14
]
. Bär and Bandara parametrize boundary conditions via trace maps in terms of closed subspaces of a certain Sobolev-like space of Cauchy-data on the boundary. There, a boundary condition is called semi-regular if its associated subspace is (locally) contained in
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega)
.

The following proposition illuminates the connection between these different regularity notions present in the literature to the one used here.

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry. Let
D_{e}
be a boundary condition for the uniformly elliptic operator
D
over
\overline{\Omega}
.

(i)

Suppose that
D
is first-order. Then, the following are equivalent:

–

D_{e}
is (semi-)regular in the sense of Definition
5.17
above.

–

D_{e}
is
A
-(semi-)regular in the sense of
[
BB22
, Definition 6.8]
.

These equivalent conditions imply that
D_{e}
is (semi-)regular in the sense of
[
BB22
, Definition 6.5]
.

(ii)

Suppose that
D_{e}=D_{P}
is defined by a differential operator
P
on the boundary. If
(D,P)
is a uniformly elliptic boundary condition in the sense of
[
Sch98a
, Definition 4.11]
, then
D_{P}
is semi-regular.

(iii)

Suppose that
D
is a second-order differential operator in divergence form, and that
D_{e}=D_{P}
is defined by a differential operator
P
on the boundary. If
(D,P)
has uniform regularity of the local problems in the sense of
[
GN20
, Theorem 5.6]
, satisfies the uniform Shapiro-Lopatinski condition of
[
GN20
, Definition 6.8]
, or is well-posed in energy space in the sense of
[
GN20
, Theorem 7.2]
, then
D_{P}
is semi-regular.

Proof.

Ad (i): We freely use the notation from
[
BB22
]
. Under our assumption
A
is a uniformly elliptic first-order operator on the bounded-geometry manifold
\partial\Omega
. Hence
\mathrm{dom}(A)=H^{1}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega})
. Then
\mathrm{dom}(|A|^{\frac{1}{2}})=H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega})
as well.
70
70

70

It holds that
\mathrm{dom}(|A|^{\mu})=[L^{2}(\partial\Omega;E),\mathrm{dom}(A)]_{\mu}=[L^{2}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega}),H^{1}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega})]_{\mu}=H^{\mu}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega})
for
0\leq\mu\leq 1
, where we used Propositions
4.25
and
6.1
below, as well as the fact that
\mathrm{dom}(|A|^{\mu})=\mathrm{dom}((A^{*}A+I)^{\mu/2})
.
Suppose that
D_{e}
is
A
-semi-regular, meaning that the trace of
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
is contained in
\mathrm{dom}(|A|^{\frac{1}{2}})=H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega,E|_{\partial\Omega})
. By the trace theorem for Sobolev spaces this implies
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq H^{1}(\overline{\Omega};E)
. We have to argue that it is closed in the Sobolev norm. To that end note that
\|\cdot\|_{D}\lesssim\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}}
. Then, if
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy with respect to the Sobolev norm, then it is also Cauchy with respect to the graph norm. Since
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
is closed with respect to the latter by definition,
(u_{n})_{n}
has a graph-norm limit
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
. On the other hand
H^{1}(\overline{\Omega};E)
is a Banach space, so that
(u_{n})_{n}
also has a Sobolev norm limit in
H^{1}(\overline{\Omega};E)
. This second limit must coincide with the graph-norm limit
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
. We conclude that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
is closed in the
H^{1}
-norm as well, hence semi-regular in our sense. Conversely, if
D_{e}
is semi-regular in our sense, then
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq H^{1}(\overline{\Omega};E)
has trace contained in
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega})=\mathrm{dom}(|A|^{\frac{1}{2}})
, hence it is also
A
-semi-regular. Any
A
-semi-regular boundary condition is automatically semi-regular in the sense of
[
BB22
]
. This is Corollary 6.10 in
[
BB22
]
, but also follows directly from the inclusion
\mathrm{dom}(|A|^{\frac{1}{2}})=H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega})\subseteq H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{loc}(\partial\Omega;E|_{\partial\Omega})
in our setting.

Ad (ii), (iii): In either case the stated condition is sufficient for a regularity estimate as in Proposition
5.19
. In the case of (ii) this is
[
Sch98a
, Theorem 4.14]
. In the case of (iii) these are Theorems 5.6, 6.9, and 7.2 in
[
GN20
]
, respectively.
∎

If
D_{e}
is semi-regular in the sense of
[
BB22
]
, then
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
is contained in
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})\cap H^{1}_{loc}(\overline{\Omega};E)
. There is no immediate reason to expect such a boundary condition to be contained in
H^{1}(\overline{\Omega};E)
. Generally we can therefore not expect a boundary condition that is (semi-)regular in the sense of
[
BB22
]
to be (semi-)regular in our sense as well.

Before discussing another more technical regularity condition let us briefly note that the norm of multiplication operators on the domains of semi-regular boundary conditions can be bounded by the
C^{m}_{b}
-norm.

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
D_{e}
be a semi-regular boundary condition, and let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
preserve the domain of
D_{e}
, i.e.
f\cdot\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
. Then,

\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\longrightarrow\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\;,\;u\longmapsto fu

is a bounded map with norm bounded by a multiple of
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
.

Proof.

Since
D_{e}
is semi-regular, its domain is a closed subspace of
H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
, and Sobolev and graph norm are equivalent. The statement thus follows from the fact that the corresponding norm estimate holds on Sobolev spaces, see Lemma
4.19
.
∎

In our application to uniform K-homology we intend to use interpolation techniques. To that end we shall need another stronger notion of regularity that also provides uniform finite summability statements in intermediate degrees. Over rough domains we cannot expect Sobolev spaces to interpolate favorably, so that we are forced to impose this regularity in intermediate degrees manually.

Definition 5.22
.

A boundary condition
D_{e}
for
D
is said to be
semi-interpolation-regular
if for all
0\leq\mu\leq 1
there is an extension map

\mathrm{dom}(|D_{e}|^{\mu})\xlongrightarrow{e_{\mu}}H^{m\mu}(M;E)\;,\;e_{\mu}(u)|_{\Omega}=u\;,

which is a Banach space isomorphism onto its image, such that whenever
\mu\geq\mu^{\prime}
it holds that
e_{\mu}(u)=e_{\mu^{\prime}}(u)\in H^{m\mu^{\prime}}(M;E)
for all
H^{m\mu}(M;E)
.

Moreover,
D_{e}
will be said to be
interpolation-regular
if both
D_{e}
and
D_{e}^{*}
are semi-interpolation-regular.

If the boundary of
\Omega
is sufficiently smooth, then any semi-regular boundary condition is automatically semi-interpolation-regular, owing to the fact that Sobolev spaces interpolate favorably on sufficiently smooth domains.

Proposition 5.23
.

Let
D
be a uniformly elliptic operator of order
m
over
\overline{\Omega}
. Any semi-interpolation-regular boundary condition for
D_{e}
is also semi-regular. If
\Omega
is a uniform
C^{m-1,1}
-domain, then the converse holds as well.

Proof.

Suppose that
D_{e}
is semi-interpolation-regular. In particular, there is an extension map
e_{1}:\,\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\to H^{m}(M;E)
. Composing it with the restriction
H^{m}(M;E)\to H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
produces a bounded inclusion
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
. Then, it holds that
\|u\|_{D}\lesssim\|u\|_{H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)}
for all
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
, because
D
is a bounded operator
H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)\to L^{2}(\Omega;F)
. Put together we deduce that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
with equivalent norms, i.e. that
D_{e}
is semi-regular.

Suppose conversely that
D_{e}
is semi-regular, and also that
\Omega
is a uniform
C^{m-1,1}
-domain. The latter assumption implies that
[L^{2}(\Omega;E),H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)]_{\mu}=H^{m\mu}(\overline{\Omega};E)
. It also holds that
\mathrm{dom}(|D_{e}|^{\mu})=[L^{2}(\overline{\Omega};E),\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})]_{\mu}
, see Proposition
6.1
below. Thus, if there is a continuous inclusion
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\hookrightarrow H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)
with equivalent norms, then there is also a continuous inclusion

\mathrm{dom}(|D_{e}|^{\mu})=[L^{2}(\overline{\Omega};E),\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})]_{\mu}\hookrightarrow[L^{2}(\Omega;E),H^{m}(\overline{\Omega};E)]_{\mu}=H^{m\mu}(\overline{\Omega};E)

with equivalent norms. Composing this inclusion map with the extension map
H^{m\mu}(\overline{\Omega};E)\to H^{m\mu}(M;E)
from Proposition
4.23
produces the desired extension map
\mathrm{dom}(|D_{e}|^{\mu})\to H^{m\mu}(M;E)
. That it has the necessary properties follows from the fact that the extension map
H^{m\mu}(\overline{\Omega};E)\to H^{m\mu}(M;E)
has those properties.
∎

Our notion of semi-interpolation-regularity therefore only becomes relevant on rough domains. However, we better show that even on rough domains there always exist such boundary conditions.

Proposition 5.24
.

The minimal boundary condition
D_{\min}
is semi-interpolation-regular on any domain
\Omega\subseteq M
.

Proof.

It holds that
\mathrm{dom}(|D_{\min}|^{\mu})=[L^{2}(\overline{\Omega}),\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})]_{\mu}=[L^{2}(\overline{\Omega}),\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega;E)]_{\mu}
with equivalent norms. Fix some
\delta>0
. Then Proposition
4.29
provides us with an inclusion
\mathrm{dom}(|D_{\min}|^{\mu})\subseteq\tilde{H}^{m\mu}(U_{\delta}(\Omega);E)
with equivalent norms. Since the latter space is in turn a subspace of
H^{m\mu}(M;E)
this yields an extension map
\mathrm{dom}(|D_{\min}|^{\mu})\to H^{m\mu}(M;E)
. Since it is the identity map on the set-level, the required compatibility with inclusions for smaller
\mu^{\prime}
holds trivially.
∎

21
Examples

The purpose of this section is to give examples of boundary conditions for different geometrically relevant operators, and discuss which of the above-discussed properties they possess, concretely if they are self-adjoint, local, (semi-)regular, odd or multigraded. The goal is by no means an exhaustive account but rather concrete illustrations. Thus, for our examples concerning Dirac operators we focus on spin Dirac operators, though the informed reader can no doubt extrapolate to spin
c
Dirac operators or more generally Dirac-type operators wherever appropriate (we refer also to
[
GN14
]
for a detailed treatment of boundary conditions for spin
c
Dirac operators). A summary of the examples can be found in Table
1
.

Remark 5.25

(Self-adjoint boundary conditions)
.

Before properly diving into the examples we wish to remark on self-adjointness. Coming from boundaryless manifolds, where every symmetric differential operator automatically has a unique self-adjoint extension (see e.g.
[
HR00
, Corollary 10.2.6]
), it is easy to take self-adjointness for granted. This attitude is misleading in the presence of boundaries. Indeed, there are symmetric differential operators that do not admit any self-adjoint extension! The most basic example of this is the operator
i\frac{d}{dx}
on
[0,\infty)
, see
[
Sch12
, Example 3.2 and Theorem 13.10]
. Even if self-adjoint boundary conditions exist, self-adjointness may be incompatible with properties like locality and regularity. Corollary
6.42
below provides an obstruction for a uniformly elliptic operator to admit a self-adjoint, local and regular boundary condition. Since this obstruction is always non-zero for the Dirac operator, it can never admit such a boundary condition.

Example 5.26

(Minimal boundary condition)
.

Every (uniformly elliptic) differential operator admits a minimal boundary condition. If the operator is symmetric, then the adjoint of the minimal boundary condition is the maximal boundary condition. Minimal and maximal boundary condition are never equal unless the boundary is empty, hence neither is self-adjoint. Proposition
5.10
above states that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\min})=\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega;E)
. It follows that
D_{\min}
is semi-regular, and we have seen in Proposition
5.24
above that it is even semi-interpolation-regular. Moreover, it is not hard to see that
\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega;E)
is closed under multiplication by functions from
C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
, meaning the minimal boundary condition is local. Lastly, if
D
is odd resp. multigraded, these properties extend to the closure, i.e. the minimal boundary condition, so that
D_{\min}
is also odd resp. multigraded in this case.

Example 5.27

(Maximal boundary condition)
.

As discussed in the previous example the maximal boundary condition is generally not self-adjoint. It is also not regular unless
\overline{\Omega}
is one-dimensional. For an explicit example consider the cylinder
\overline{\Omega}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times S^{1}
with the uniformly elliptic operator
D=i\partial_{\vartheta}+\partial_{y}
. Here
\vartheta
is the circle-variable and
y
the height-variable. Fix some
u_{0}\in L^{2}(S^{1})
,
u\notin H^{\frac{1}{2}}(S^{1})
, and define
\tilde{u}(\vartheta,y):=\left(e^{-y\partial_{\vartheta}^{2}}u_{0}\right)(\vartheta)
. The function
\tilde{u}
arises via heat-flow from
u_{0}
, thus is smooth for
y>0
. Smoothly cutting off
\tilde{u}
at
y\geq 1
results in an
L^{2}
-function
u
on
\overline{\Omega}
that is smooth for all
y>0
. Thus
u
is in
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})
. If
u
were also in
H^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
, it would have a trace
u(\cdot,0)
in
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(S^{1})
. Since the heat kernel converges to the identity for
y\to 0
, this trace would coincide with
u_{0}
. But we have explicitly chosen
u_{0}
not to lie in
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(S^{1})
, making this a contradiction.

If
D
is first-order, then
D_{\max}
is local, because
D_{\min}
is local, see Proposition
5.16
above. For higher-order operators the maximal boundary condition need no longer be local. We give an example that is adapted from
[
Fri25
, Remark 3.10]
. Consider the Laplace operator
\Delta
on functions on the square
\Omega=\{0<x,y<1\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The function
u(x,y)=\log(x^{2}+y^{2})
is smooth away from
0
and square-integrable on
\Omega
. Moreover, it holds that
\Delta u=0
away from
0
. Thus
u
is in the kernel - hence in particular in the domain- of
\Delta_{\max}
. Consider also the smooth function
f(x,y):=\frac{1}{4}\left((1-x)^{2}-y^{2}\right)
. An explicit calculation shows that

\Delta(fu)=-\frac{2x}{x^{2}+y^{2}}+2+u\;.

The function
x(x^{2}+y^{2})^{-1}
is not integrable over
\Omega
, hence neither is
\Delta(fu)
. Thus we have found
u\in\mathrm{dom}(\Delta_{\max})
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
such that
fu\notin\mathrm{dom}(\Delta_{\max})
.

Lastly, if
D
is odd resp. multigraded, then so is
D_{\max}
. This follows from Lemma
5.14
and the corresponding property of the minimal boundary condition.

Example 5.28

(APS-boundary condition for the Dirac operator)
.

Assume that
\overline{\Omega}
is a spin manifold with boundary and bounded geometry, and assume that
\overline{\Omega}
has product form near the boundary. Let
\not{S}(\overline{\Omega})\to\overline{\Omega}
denote the complex spinor bundle, and
\not{D}:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;\not{S}(\overline{\Omega}))\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;\not{S}(\overline{\Omega}))
the spin Dirac operator. If the dimension of
\Omega
is odd, then
\not{S}(\overline{\Omega})|_{\partial\Omega}
is naturally isomorphic to
\not{S}(\partial\Omega)
, and if the dimension is even, then it has two graded components, both of which are isomorphic to
\not{S}(\partial\Omega)
. Fix an inward-pointing unit normal
\nu
. On the boundary
\not{D}
takes the form

\not{D}=c(\nu)\left(\partial_{\nu}+A\right)\;,

where
c(\nu)
denotes Clifford multiplication by
\nu
, and
c(\nu)A=\not{D}_{\partial\Omega}
if
\dim(\Omega)
is odd, and
c(\nu)A=\begin{pmatrix}0&\not{D}_{\partial\Omega}\\
\not{D}_{\partial\Omega}&0\end{pmatrix}
if it is even. In either case
A
is an essentially self-adjoint operator, and thus admits a spectral projection
\pi_{(-\infty,0)}=\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0)}(A)
. The
APS-boundary condition
is defined by

\mathrm{dom}(\not{D}_{\mathrm{APS}})=\left\{u\in\mathrm{dom}(\not{D}_{\max})\,|\,\pi_{(-\infty,0)}u|_{\partial\Omega}=0\right\}

The spectral projection
\pi_{(-\infty,0)}
is a
non-local
operator, therefore
\not{D}_{\mathrm{APS}}
is not local. It is however regular
[
BB22
, Proposition 6.23]
. The domain of
\mathrm{dom}(\not{D}_{\mathrm{APS}}^{*})
equals
\mathrm{dom}(\not{D}_{\mathrm{APS}})\oplus\ker(A)
, so that the APS-boundary condition is self-adjoint if and only if
A
has trivial kernel, which in turn is the case if and only if
\not{D}_{\partial\Omega}
has trivial kernel. Moreover, if
\overline{\Omega}
is even-dimensional, then the spinor bundle is graded by the complex volume element, which locally can be written as
\kappa=\pm ic(e_{1})\cdots c(e_{n-1})c(\nu)
, where
e_{1},\cdots,e_{n-1}
form a local orthonormal frame of
\nu^{\perp}=T(\partial\Omega)\subseteq T\overline{\Omega}
. The operator
\not{D}
is odd with respect to this grading. Since both
c(\nu)
and the operator
\begin{pmatrix}0&\not{D}_{\partial\Omega}\\
\not{D}_{\partial\Omega}&0\end{pmatrix}
are odd with respect to this grading, the operator
A
is commutes with the grading operator
\kappa
. Hence the same holds for
\pi_{(-\infty,0)}
, and we conclude that
\kappa
preserves
\mathrm{dom}(\not{D}_{\mathrm{APS}})
. Hence the APS-condition is odd in even dimensions.

Example 5.29

(Chiral boundary conditions for the Dirac operator)
.

We still consider the spin Dirac operator
\not{D}
on a spin manifold with boundary and bounded geometry with inward-pointing unit normal
\nu
. Then, the Clifford multiplication by
i\nu
is a self-adjoint involution on
\not{S}(\overline{\Omega})|_{\partial\Omega}
. Let
P_{\pm}
denote projection onto its
(\pm 1)
-eigenspace, and define the
chirality boundary conditions

\not{D}_{\pm}
by

\mathrm{dom}(\not{D}_{\pm})=\left\{u\in\mathrm{dom}(\not{D}_{\max})\,|\,P_{\pm}u|_{\partial\Omega}=0\right\}\;.

Since they are defined by bundle morphisms the chirality boundary conditions are local. It holds that
\not{D}_{\pm}^{*}=\not{D}_{\mp}

[
GN14
, Lemma 5.1]
. The chirality boundary conditions are regular
[
BB22
, Proposition 6.32 and Corollary 6.10]
. However, they are not graded. Indeed, suppose that
\dim(\Omega)
is even. Then the spinor bundle is graded by the complex volume element
\kappa
, which we recall is locally given by
\kappa=\pm ic(e_{1})\cdots c(e_{n-1})c(\nu)
. Then,
\kappa c(\nu)=-c(\nu)\kappa
. Thus the grading operator
\kappa
permutes the kernels of
P_{\pm}
, and we conclude that
\kappa\not{D}_{\pm}=-\not{D}_{\mp}\kappa
.

Example 5.30

(Absolute and relative boundary condition for the Euler characteristic operator)
.

Assume that
\overline{\Omega}
is a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry. We consider
d+d^{*}
acting on the bundle
\bigwedge\overline{\Omega}=\bigoplus_{p=0}^{\dim(\Omega)}\bigwedge^{p}T^{*}\overline{\Omega}
, graded by even/odd degree. The resulting graded operator will be denoted by
D^{\chi}
. It is odd if
\overline{\Omega}
is even-dimensional. Fix an inward-pointing unit normal
\nu
. Then, there is an orthogonal splitting

\left(\bigwedge T^{*}\overline{\Omega}\right)\bigg|_{\partial\Omega}=\left(\bigwedge T^{*}(\partial\Omega)\right)\oplus\left(\bigwedge T^{*}(\partial\Omega)\right)\wedge(\mathbb{C}\cdot\nu^{\flat})

Denote by
P_{1}
the projection onto the first summand, and by
P_{2}
that onto the second. The principal symbol
\sigma_{D^{\chi}}(\nu^{\flat})
is the sum of the wedge product and the interior product by
\nu
. It therefore permutes the kernels of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
. On the other hand
\sigma_{D^{\chi}}(\xi)
preserves the kernels of
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
if
\xi\in T^{*}(\partial\Omega)
. Define the
absolute
and
relative
boundary conditions
D^{\chi}_{\mathrm{abs}}
and
D^{\chi}_{\mathrm{rel}}
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(D^{\chi}_{\mathrm{abs}})

\displaystyle=\left\{\alpha\in\mathrm{dom}(D^{\chi}_{\max})\,|\,P_{2}\alpha=0\right\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(D^{\chi}_{\mathrm{rel}})

\displaystyle=\left\{\alpha\in\mathrm{dom}(D^{\chi}_{\max})\,|\,P_{1}\alpha=0\right\}\;.

They are defined by bundle morphism, and hence local. Moreover, they are both self-adjoint and regular.
71
71

71

Both self-adjointness and regularity follow from results in
[
BB22
]
. All references in this footnote refer to the corresponding part of that paper. The adapted boundary operator is given by
A=\sigma_{D^{\chi}}(\nu)^{-1}D^{\chi}_{\partial\Omega}
. Then, the operator
\Xi=P_{1}\oplus(-P_{2})
anti-commutes with
A
, and it squares to the identity. Thus
\Xi
is a chirality operator for
A
in the sense of Definition 6.31. Its
(\pm 1)
-eigenspaces are the kernels of
P_{2}
and
P_{1}
, respectively. Proposition 6.32 and Corollary 6.10 combine to show that the absolute and relative boundary conditions are regular. Using that
\Xi
is self-adjoint and anti-commutes with
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{D^{\chi}}(\nu)
we conclude self-adjointness from Remark 6.34(b).
The grading by degree preserves the domains of
D^{\chi}_{\mathrm{abs/rel}}
, hence
D^{\chi}_{\mathrm{abs/rel}}
is odd in even dimensions.

Table 1:
Summary of the properties of the boundary conditions discussed in this section. A check-mark in brackets indicates that the property is possessed if the prerequisites are met. For example the mark (✓) for
D_{\min}
in the „odd“-column means that if
D
is odd, then so is
D_{\min}
. Similarly, no mark is made if the grading property cannot meaningfully applied to the operator to begin with. For example the box for
\Delta_{\mathrm{Dir}}
in the „odd“-column is left empty because the Laplacian is not a graded operator.

Example 5.31

(Dirichlet and Neumann Laplacian)
.

Assume that
\overline{\Omega}
is a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry. Consider the Laplace operator
\Delta=d^{*}d
on functions. Again, we fix an inward-pointing unit normal
\nu
. Define the
Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(\Delta_{\mathrm{Dir}})

\displaystyle=\left\{u\in\mathrm{dom}(\Delta_{\max})\,|\,u|_{\partial\Omega}=0\right\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(\Delta_{\mathrm{Neu}})

\displaystyle=\left\{u\in\mathrm{dom}(\Delta_{\max})\,|\,(\partial_{\nu}u)|_{\partial\Omega}=0\right\}\;.

Both are self-adjoint
[
AGN19
, Theorem 1.2]
and regular
[
Sch98a
, Proposition 5.9]
. The Dirichlet boundary condition is local in our sense, but the Neumann condition is not. Indeed, a function
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
preserves
\mathrm{dom}(\Delta_{\mathrm{Neu}})
if and only if
(\partial_{\nu}f)|_{\partial\Omega}=0
. This illustrates that differential boundary conditions need not be local in our sense. Indeed, if the differential operator defining the boundary condition has order at least
1
, there is no reason to expect the boundary condition to be local in this sense.

22
Finite propagation

In this section we establish the finite-propagation property for wave operators and certain functions of local self-adjoint boundary conditions for uniformly elliptic differential operators of first-order. Using these results we will obtain finite-propagation representatives of the uniform K-homology classes of such operators in Chapter 6. While we know from Lemma
2.25
that any uniform K-homology class admits a finite-propagation representative, the truncation procedure that gives rise to that lemma is not exactly convenient to use in practice. In contrast the propagation results of this section will allow us to work with functions of boundary conditions directly.

We assume throughout that
D
is a uniformly elliptic first-order differential operator. Since
D
is in particular bounded the quantity

c_{D}\;:=\;\sup_{x\in M}\;\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in T^{*}_{x}M\\
||\xi||=1\end{subarray}}\left|\left|\sigma_{D}(x,\xi)\right|\right|

is finite. It is called the
propagation speed
of
D
. The following proposition establishes that solutions of the wave equation propagate at most with speed
c_{D}
.

Proposition 5.32
.

Let
D_{e}
be a local self-adjoint boundary condition for
D
. Then, for any compactly supported
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
it holds that

\mathrm{supp}(e^{itD_{e}}u)\subseteq N_{c_{D}|t|}\left(\mathrm{supp}(u)\right)\;.

The proof is exactly analogous to the situation without boundary. Since it might not be entirely standard that this result carries over to local boundary conditions, we repeat it here. We follow the proof provided in
[
WY19
]
, Proposition 8.2.1.

Proof.

We may take
t
to be positive, otherwise replace
D
by
-D
. Set
K:=\mathrm{supp}(u)
and
u_{t}:=e^{itD_{e}}u
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta>0
, and choose
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
such that

(i)

g|_{K}\equiv 1
and
g(x)=0
if
d(x,K)\geq\delta
,

(ii)

\sup_{d(x,K)\leq\delta}\left|g(x)-\frac{1}{\delta}\left(1-d(x,K)\right)\right|<\varepsilon
,

(iii)

||dg||<\varepsilon+\frac{1}{\delta}
.

Choose
f:\,\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
smooth and non-decreasing such that
f(t)=1
if and only if
t\geq 1
. Furthermore, we set

\displaystyle\gamma\;

\displaystyle:=\;c_{D}(1+\delta\varepsilon)

\displaystyle h_{t}\;

\displaystyle:=\;f\left(g(x)+\frac{\gamma}{\delta}t\right)\;.

Observe that
h_{t}(x)
is smooth in
t
and
x
, and that

h^{-1}_{t}\left(\{1\}\right)\;\subseteq\;N_{\gamma t+\varepsilon}(K)\;.

Moreover, we may calculate the derivatives of
h_{t}(x)
:

\displaystyle\dot{h}_{t}(x)\;

\displaystyle=\;\frac{\gamma}{\delta}\cdot f^{\prime}\left(g(x)+\frac{\gamma}{\delta}t\right)

\displaystyle dh_{t}(x)\;

\displaystyle=\;f^{\prime}\left(g(x)+\frac{\gamma}{\delta}t\right)\cdot dg(x)\;.

Since
D_{e}
is local, multiplication by
h_{t}
preserves
\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
, so that we may form the commutator
[D_{e},h_{t}]
. Then

[D_{e},h_{t}]=-i\sigma_{D}(dh_{t})=-\frac{i\delta}{\gamma}\dot{h}_{t}(x)\cdot\sigma_{D}(dg)\;.

As per our assumptions on
g
we have

||\sigma_{D}(dg)||\;\leq\;\frac{\gamma}{1+\delta\varepsilon}||dg||\;\leq\;\frac{\gamma}{1+\delta\varepsilon}(\frac{1}{\delta}+\varepsilon)\;=\;\frac{\gamma}{\delta}\;,

from which we may deduce that the operator

\dot{h}_{t}-i[D_{e},h_{t}]\;=\;\dot{h}_{t}\left(I-\frac{\delta}{\gamma}\sigma_{D}(dg)\right)

is positive, because
\dot{h}_{t}\geq 0
as well.

By definition
u_{t}
satisfies
\dot{u_{t}}=iD_{e}u_{t}
. Conclude that that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\langle h_{t}u_{t},u_{t}\rangle\;

\displaystyle=\;\langle\dot{h}_{t}u_{t},u_{t}\rangle+\langle h_{t}(iD_{e})u_{t},u_{t}\rangle+\langle h_{t}u_{t},(iD_{e})u_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\;\langle\dot{h}_{t}-i[D_{e},h_{t}]u_{t},u_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\;0\;.

Moreover, since
e^{itD_{e}}
is unitary, we have

\langle u_{t},u_{t}\rangle\;=\;\langle u_{0},u_{0}\rangle\;=\;\langle h_{0}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle\;\leq\;\langle h_{t}u_{t},u_{t}\rangle\;.

On the other hand, Cauchy-Schwarz gives

\langle h_{t}u_{t},u_{t}\rangle\;\leq\;||h_{t}u_{t}||\cdot||u_{t}||\;\leq\;||u_{t}||^{2}\;,

where we used that
h_{t}\leq 1
. It follows that all inequalities in the above two displays are actually equalities. Because equality in Cauchy-Schwarz occurs if and only if the two vectors are linearly dependent, it follows that
h_{t}u_{t}=u_{t}
. We deduce that

\mathrm{supp}(u_{t})\;\subseteq\;h_{t}^{-1}\left(\{1\}\right)\;\subseteq\;N_{\gamma t+\varepsilon}(K)\;.

Letting
\varepsilon
tend to
0
completes the proof.
∎

Proposition
5.32
also holds for non-local self-adjoint boundary conditions if we assume that
u
is supported in the interior, and that
c_{D}|t|\leq d(\mathrm{supp}(u),\partial\Omega)
. In this case
e^{itD_{e}}u
is actually independent of the boundary condition
D_{e}
. Using this one may actually establish the existence of the K-homology class of a first-order elliptic operator over the interior of a manifold with boundary, and show that it is independent of the choice of boundary condition. This is the strategy used by Baum, Douglas and Taylor in
[
BDT89
]
. We will however employ a different strategy that also works for higher-order operators.

For Proposition
5.32
to hold up to the boundary it is however necessary that
D
is first-order, and that
D_{e}
is local. That the order of the operator matters can be seen like this: The wave operator
e^{itD}
is the solution operator of the equation
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u=iDu
. If
D
is first-order, this is a hyperbolic equation, which have finite propagation speed. If
D
is of higher order, then the equation is diffusive instead, and such equations generally have infinite propagation speed.

To see that some form of locality is necessary, consider
D=i\frac{d}{dx}
on
M=[0,1]
, and
D_{e}
the self-adjoint extension of
D
determined by

\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})=\{\,u\in H^{1}[0,1]\,|\,u(0)=u(1)\,\}\;.

Then
(e^{itD_{e}}u)(x)=\tilde{u}(t-x)
, where
\tilde{u}
is the
1
-periodic continuation of
u
to a function on
\mathbb{R}
. Thus, a function propagating into the left side of the interval immediately appears at the right side of the interval. Considering functions with supports getting closer to the left end of
[0,1]
, it takes less time for
e^{itD_{e}}
to propagate them to the right side of the interval. Thus
e^{itD_{e}}
has infinite propagation speed.

Using Fourier inversion we extend the finite-propagation result from wave operators to more general functions of
D_{e}
. Indeed, suppose that
f
is a bounded Borel function with Fourier transform supported inside
[-R,R]
. Then we have the Fourier inversion formula

f(D_{e})u=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-R}^{R}\hat{f}(t)e^{itD_{e}}u\,dt

for
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
. Then Proposition
5.32
implies the following.

Corollary 5.33

(Compare
[
HR00
, Lemma 10.5.5]
)
.

Let
D_{e}
be a local self-adjoint boundary condition for
D
. Let
f
be a bounded Borel function with
\mathrm{supp}(\hat{f})\subseteq[-R,R]
. Then, for any compactly supported
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
it holds that

\mathrm{supp}\left(f(D_{e})u\right)\subseteq N_{c_{D}R}\left(\mathrm{supp}(u)\right)\;.

Chapter 6
Elliptic operators and uniform K-homology

At this point all the technical machinery is in place, and we are ready to prove the existence and properties of the uniform K-homology classes of uniformly elliptic operators over arbitrary domains in manifolds of bounded geometry.

To prove the existence of the uniform K-homology classes we use a formal operator calculus. This calculus is motivated and described further in the beginning of Section 6.1. That section contains the prerequisite definitions and facts concerning this calculus, as well as most calculations necessary to deduce existence of the uniform K-homology classes. The results of this section rely on the analysis done in the previous two chapters. As such Section 6.1 is the technical core of this chapter, and indeed the entire thesis. Existence of uniform K-homology classes is proved in Section 6.2, where we also discuss finite-propagation representatives that will be useful in our discussion of index maps later. In Section 6.3 we prove compatibility of the classes with restrictions and products, and we prove the boundary-of-Dirac-is-Dirac formula. Lastly, in Section 6.4 we use boundary-of-Dirac-is-Dirac to deduce bordism invariance of the relative uniform index, and combine it with the abstract results of Chapter 3 to deduce the uniform partitioned-manifold index theorems.

23
The formal operator calculus

Let
D_{e}
be a boundary condition for a uniformly elliptic symmetric operator over a domain
\overline{\Omega}
, and let
F=D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. We wish to show that
F
defines a uniform Fredholm module, i.e. that

[f,F]\quad,\quad\left(I-F^{2}\right)f\quad,\quad\left(F-F^{*}\right)f

are uniformly approximable for all relevant functions
f
. Recall from the introduction how this can be done in the case where
D_{e}=D
is an elliptic operator over a closed manifold. We noted that in this case
(D^{*}D+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is an elliptic pseudo-differential operator of order
-m
(with
m
the order of
D
as usual). Then, the commutator

[f,F]=[f,D](D^{*}D+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}+D\left[f,(D^{*}D+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]

extends to a bounded map
L^{2}(\Omega)\to H^{1}(\Omega)
, because the commutator of a (pseudo-)differential operator with a smooth function is an operator of one order lower. Compactness then follows from the Rellich-Kondrachov theorem. Similarly,
I-F^{2}=(D^{*}D+I)^{-1}
is a pseudo-differential operator of order
-2m
, hence extends to a bounded map
L^{2}(\Omega)\to H^{2m}(\Omega)
. Compactness again follows from the Rellich-Kondrachov theorem. Lastly,
F=F^{*}
yields the compactness of the thrid operator trivially.

The used result that
(D^{*}D+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is an elliptic pseudo-differential operator of order
-m
is already non-trivial for closed manifolds (see
[
See67
]
). While interesting a discussion of functions of elliptic operators on possibly non-compact manifolds with boundary is miles beyond the scope of this thesis (though see
[
Amm+04
]
for the case of boundaryless manifolds of bounded geometry). Therefore we adopt the formal argument, but instead of mapping properties of pseudo-differential operators on the Sobolev scale we use a formal scale
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, of spaces associated to the fixed boundary condition
D_{e}
. In its properties this formal scale is very similar to the Sobolev scale, but its intrinsically operator-theoretic nature makes it behave much nicer under functional calculus. For example the statement that
(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
has negative order is true almost by definition on the formal scale of
D_{e}
. The formal scale of a closed operator and its basic mapping properties are introduced in Subsection 6.1.1.

To deduce the properties necessary for the existence of uniform K-homology classes we need uniform compactness statements for operators that have negative order on the formal scale. This will be achieved by connecting the formal scale of a boundary condition back to the Sobolev scale via regularity. This is the content of Subsection 6.1.2.

The uniform pseudo-locality of
F
will require us to handle commutators of functions of
D_{e}
with functions on
\overline{\Omega}
. We will do this by means of holomorphic functional calculus, as this provides a convenient tool to reduce the case of more general functions to resolvents. This is done in Subsection 6.1.3.

The uniform compactness of
\left(F-F^{*}\right)f
was trivial in the absence of boundaries, but becomes a subtle matter in their presence: It holds that
F^{*}=D_{e}^{*}(D_{e}D_{e}^{*}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, so that
F^{*}
is better interpreted as arising from the adjoint boundary condition
D_{e}^{*}
. Viewed in this light the uniform local compactness of
F-F^{*}
becomes a comparison statement for different boundary conditions. This introduces technical complications, as the spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})
will be different for different boundary conditions. The common ground is given by interior regularity; on the interior all boundary conditions are the same. This observation enables us make a more general statement: If
D_{e}
and
D_{e}^{\prime}
are boundary conditions for
different
uniformly elliptic operators, but we assume that
D
and
D^{\prime}
differ only by a lower order operator -meaning they have the same principal symbol- then on the interior the domains of
D_{e}
and
D_{e}^{\prime}
are both given by Sobolev space, and there
D_{e}-D_{e}^{\prime}
extends to an operator of lower order. From this observation we can use holomorphic functional calculus piece together the statement that
\left(D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-(D_{e}^{\prime}((D_{e}^{\prime})^{*}D_{e}^{\prime}+I)^{-1/2}\right)f
has negative order and thus is uniformly locally compact. This will imply that
D_{e}
and
D_{e}^{\prime}
define the same class in uniform K-homology. We carry out this calculation in Subsection 6.1.4. Lastly, Subsection 6.1.5 contains a few remarks on extensions of these results to uniformly elliptic pseudo-differential operators, and elliptic operators with certain types of singularities on the boundary.

The use of the formal operator calculus as a more convenient substitute for pseudo-differential calculus and the Sobolev scale is well-established. Its use in index theory goes back at least to Connes and Moscovici
[
CM95
]
, and is also used in the recent article by Fries
[
Fri25
]
extending the results of Baum, Douglas and Taylor
[
BDT89
]
to higher-order operators. For an example outside index theory, see for example the investigations of the Calderón projection in
[
BLZ09
]
.

23.1
The abstract framework

We begin with a general discussion of the scale associated to a fixed closed operator
T
on a Hilbert space
H
. For
\alpha\geq 0
let
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
denote the domain of
(T^{*}T+I)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
. Equip
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
with the norm
||u||_{\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)}:=||(T^{*}T+I)^{\alpha/2}u||
, it is equivalent to the graph norm of
(T^{*}T+I)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
. Moreover, we set
\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha}(T):=(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T))^{*}
. The spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
are Hilbert spaces, and if
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
there is a bounded inclusion
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}}(D_{e})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})
. Lastly, set
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(T):=\bigcap_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
and
\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}(T):=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
. When the operator
T
is clear from context we shall take the liberty of writing
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
,
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
, etc. instead of
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
,
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(T)
, etc.

Proposition 6.1
.

(i)

For all
\alpha\geq 0
the inner product on
H
extends to a perfect pairing between
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
and
\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha}(T)
.

(ii)

For all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha\leq\beta
, the embedding
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}(T)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
has dense image. In fact,
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(T)
is dense in each
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
.

(iii)

For
\alpha\geq 0
, the norm of
u\in H
as an element of
\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha}(T)
is given by

||u||_{\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha}(T)}=\left|\left|\left(T^{*}T+I\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}u\right|\right|\;.

(iv)

For all
\alpha<\beta
the interpolation space
[\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T),\mathcal{H}^{\beta}(T)]_{\theta}
is given by
\mathcal{H}^{(1-\theta)\alpha+\theta\beta}(T)
.

Proof.

Item (i), (ii) and (iii) are proved in Appendix A.1 of Bär-Bandara. Item (iv) is proved in Proposition 4.2.2 of Taylor
72
72

72

Strictly speaking, in that proposition it is only proved that
[H,\mathcal{H}^{k}]_{\theta}=\mathcal{H}^{k\theta}
. This takes care of the case
\alpha\geq 0
.For
\beta<0
note that via the dense embedding
H\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
,
\mathrm{dom}(T)=\mathrm{dom}((T^{*}T+I)^{1/2})
is also the domain of the operator
T^{(\alpha)}:=(T^{*}T+I)^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}
viewed as an unbounded operator on
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
. In fact, since
(T^{*}T+I)^{-\alpha/2}
is an isometric isomorphism from
H
to
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
, the operator
T^{(\alpha)}
is closed, and it holds that
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(T^{\alpha})=\mathcal{H}^{(1-\alpha)\gamma+\alpha}(T)
. The case
\alpha<0
may thus be reduced to the case
\alpha=0
.
.
∎

Let
A:\,\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(T)\to\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}(T)
be a linear map. The majority of our investigations will revolve around the question whether there is
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
A
extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)\to\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}(T)
for some or even all
\alpha
. In the case where
T=D_{e}
is a suitable boundary condition, such operators have similar mapping properties as pseudo-differential operators on the Sobolev scale, so we should think of them as operators of order
\beta
on the scale associated to
T
. Our first lemma is an application of duality and interpolation.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
A,B:\,\mathcal{H}^{\infty}\to H
be linear maps such that
\langle u,Av\rangle_{H}=\langle Bu,v\rangle_{H}
. Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.

(i)

If
A
extends to a bounded map from
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
to
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}
, then
B
extends to a bounded map from
\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha+\beta}
to
\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha}
.

(ii)

If
A
and
B
extend to bounded maps
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}\to\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}
, then
A
and
B
extend to bounded maps
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{H}^{\gamma-\beta}
for all
\gamma
between
-\alpha+\beta
and
\alpha
.

Proof.

Ad (i):
\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha}
is the dual of
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
, by definition if
\alpha\geq 0
and by reflexivity if
\alpha<0
. Consider the dual operator
A^{\prime}
to
A:\,\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}\to\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}
, it maps
(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta})^{*}=\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha+\beta}
to
(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha})^{*}=\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha}
. By Proposition
6.1
it is fully determined by the images of
\varphi_{u}\in(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha})^{*}
,
\varphi_{u}(v)=\langle u,v\rangle_{H}
, for
u\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}
. For
v\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha+\beta}
this image is given by

\left(A^{\prime}\varphi_{u}\right)v=\varphi_{u}(Av)=\langle u,Av\rangle_{H}=\langle Bu,v\rangle_{H}=\varphi_{Bu}(v)\;.

Note that since
B
extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}\to\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}
,
\varphi_{Bu}
is an element of the dual of
\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha+\beta}
. Since
A^{\prime}
is bounded as the dual operator to a bounded operator, it follows that
B
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha+\beta}\to\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha}
via
\varphi_{u}\mapsto\varphi_{Bu}
.

Ad (ii): If
A,B
both extend to bounded maps from
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
to
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}
, then both maps also extend to bounded maps from
\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha+\beta}
to
\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha}
by item (i). Therefore
A
and
B
also extend to bounded maps from
[\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha+\beta},\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}]_{\theta}
to
[\mathcal{H}^{-\alpha},\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}]_{\theta}
by interpolation, and by Proposition
6.1
these exhaust all intermediate exponents.
∎

Next we come to functions of the operators. Applying a function to the operator
T
produces an operator of order
\beta
on the scale of
T
, where the order
\beta
depends on the growth of the function. Concretely, for
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
let
\mathcal{F}^{\beta}
denote the space of measurable functions
f
on
\mathbb{R}
for which
(1+x^{2})^{-\frac{\beta}{2}}f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. We equip
\mathcal{F}^{\beta}
with the norm
||f||_{\mathcal{F}^{\beta}}:=||(1+x^{2})^{-\frac{\beta}{2}}f||_{\infty}
. Then, we have the following.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}^{\beta}
.

(i)

T
is a bounded operator from
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
to
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-1}(T^{*})
, and
T^{*}
a bounded operator from
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T^{*})
to
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-1}(T)
.

(ii)

f(|T|)
is a bounded operator from
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
to
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}(T)
, and
||f(|T|)||\leq||f||_{\mathcal{F}^{\beta}}
independently of
\alpha
.

(iii)

Suppose
T
is self-adjoint. Then,
f(T)
is a bounded operator from
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
to
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}(T)
, and
||f(T)||\leq||f||_{\mathcal{F}^{\beta}}
independently of
\alpha
.

Proof.

Ad (i): Let
u\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}(T)
. Then,

||Tu||_{\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-1}(T^{*})}=||(TT^{*}+I)^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}Tu||=||T(T^{*}T+I)^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}u||\leq||(T^{*}T+I)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u||\leq||u||_{\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)}\;,

for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Using that
\mathcal{H}^{1}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
for
\alpha\leq 1
the claim for
T
follows. For
T^{*}
it is proved analogously.

Ad (ii): By definition of
\mathcal{F}^{\beta}
and the properties of the functional calculus the operator
f(|T|)
can be written as the product of
(T^{*}T+I)^{\beta/2}
with a bounded operator. For
u\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(T)
we have

||f(|T|)u||_{\mathcal{H}^{\alpha-\beta}}=\left|\left|\left((T^{*}T+I)^{-\beta/2}f(|T|)\right)\cdot(T^{*}T+I)^{\alpha/2}u\right|\right|\leq||f||_{\mathcal{F}^{\beta}}\cdot||u||_{\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)}\;,

where we used commutativity and isometry of the functional calculus. Using that
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(T)
is dense in
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(T)
, the claim for
f(|T|)
follows. The proof of (iii) is completely analogous.
∎

23.2
The case of uniformly elliptic operators

In our application to uniform K-homology we shall need uniform compactness statements related to boundary conditions for uniformly elliptic operators. These must come from the corresponding statements for Sobolev spaces. We will handle calculations with boundary conditions with the formal operator calculus. Therefore we need to connect the scale associated to suitable boundary conditions to the Sobolev scale, so that we can then deduce uniform compactness statements based on behavior in the operator calculus. More concretely we will see how statements that operators have negative order in the abstract operator calculus of a boundary condition imply uniform compactness statements.

Throughout this section we consider two situations simultaneously: The behavior of arbitrary boundary conditions in the interior, and that of semi-interpolation-regular boundary conditions up to the boundary. In either case we can connect the domain of the boundary condition to Sobolev space, in the former case by interior regularity and in the latter by assumed semi-regularity. In either case we will deduce the desired statements for the operator calculus from the corresponding statement for Sobolev spaces. The behavior of arbitrary boundary conditions on the interior will eventually give rise to the fact that any boundary condition defines a uniform K-homology class on the interior, while that of (local) semi-regular boundary conditions will yield the relative uniform K-homology classes associated to such boundary conditions.

Let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry,
\Omega\subseteq M
a domain, and
E\to M
vector bundles of bounded geometry. Let
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
be a differential operator of order
m
that is uniformly elliptic over
\overline{\Omega}
. Let
D_{e}
be a boundary condition for
D
. Then,
D_{e}
is a closed operator on the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\Omega)
, and we will investigate its formal scale
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})
. We begin with a lemma concerning multiplication by functions.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
D_{e}
be a boundary condition for
D
, and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
. Suppose that either
D_{e}
is arbitrary and
\mathrm{supp}(f)\subset\Omega
, or
D_{e}
is semi-regular and
f\cdot\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})
. Then multiplication by
f
is a bounded map
