\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})

for all
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
, with norm proportional to
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
.

Proof.

For
\alpha=1
the statement holds by Corollary
5.12
or Lemma
5.21
, for
\alpha=0
it is true because
\mathcal{H}^{0}(D_{e})=L^{2}(\Omega;E)
. For intermediate values it follows by interpolation.
∎

The following lemma may appear to be an unassuming consequence of interior regularity, and while this is not incorrect, we point out that the proof uses the interpolation behavior of the Sobolev spaces
\tilde{H}^{s}(\Omega)
. Indeed, being able to make inferences for fractional degrees on the formal scale of boundary conditions was the motivation for our investigations into interpolation o Sobolev spaces in the first place.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
D_{e}
be any boundary condition for
D
. Let
K\subset\Omega
be a compact subspace in the interior. Then,

\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})\cap L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)=H^{m\alpha}_{K}(M;E)

with equivalent norms. The constants in the norm equivalence depend only on the distance of
K
to
\partial\Omega
but not the specific
K
.

Proof.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
a non-negative integer this is a consequence of interior regularity, because in these cases
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})
is the domain of a uniformly elliptic operator. Indeed, if
\alpha=2k
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})=\mathrm{dom}((D^{*}_{e}D_{e})^{k})
. If
\alpha=2k+1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})=\mathrm{dom}(D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e})^{k})
. The claim then follows from a combination of Propositions
5.8
and
5.10
.

For intermediate values of
\alpha
we interpolate. Using a bump function around
K
and interior regularity we find that

\displaystyle\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),\mathcal{H}^{2k}(D_{e})\right]_{\theta}\cap L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)

\displaystyle=\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),\mathrm{dom}((D^{\dagger}D)_{\min})\right]_{\theta}\cap L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)

\displaystyle=\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),\tilde{H}^{2km}(\Omega;E)\right]_{\theta}\cap L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)

with equivalent norms, where the constants in the norm equivalence depend on
K
only through
d(K,\partial\Omega)
, which enters through the norm of the bump function. Then, fix
\delta>0
. By means of Proposition
4.29
we conclude for
0<\theta<1
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2k\theta}(D_{e})\cap L^{2}_{K}(\overline{\Omega};E)

\displaystyle=\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),\mathcal{H}^{2k}(D_{e})\right]_{\theta}\cap L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)

\displaystyle=\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),\tilde{H}^{2mk}(\Omega;E)\right]_{\theta}\cap L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)

\displaystyle\subseteq\tilde{H}^{2mk\theta}(U_{\delta}(\Omega);E)\cap L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)

\displaystyle=H^{2mk\theta}_{K}(M;E)

with equivalent norms, where the constants of the equivalence still depend on
K
only through
d(K,\partial\Omega)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

In the following proposition we transfer the uniformly summable Rellich-Kondrachov theorem to the spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})
. This forms the technical backbone of our subsequent investigations into the uniform K-homology classes of uniformly elliptic operators. Again, we feel obligated to point out that this result is the derived from technical overhead developed in Chapters 4 and 5, thereby necessitating analysis developed in these chapters.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
K\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
be a compact subset. Assume that either:

(a)

K\subset\Omega
, and
D_{e}
is arbitrary,

(b)

or
K
is arbitrary, and
D_{e}
is semi-interpolation-regular.

Let
0<\mu\leq 1
. Set
R:=\mathrm{diam}(K)
, and
\delta:=d(K,\partial\Omega)
. The maps

\mathcal{H}^{\mu}(D_{e})\cap L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)\xlongrightarrow{j_{\mu}}L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)\quad\mathrm{and}\quad L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)\xlongrightarrow{j_{-\mu}}\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})

are
p
-summable for all
p>\frac{n}{m\mu}
. In case (a) it holds that

\|j_{\mu}\|_{p},\,\|j_{-\mu}\|_{p}\leq C(\mu,p,R,\delta)\;.

In case (b) it holds that

\|j_{\mu}\|_{p},\,\|j_{-\mu}\|_{p}\leq C^{\prime}(\mu,p,R)\;.

Here,
C
and
C^{\prime}
depend only on
\overline{\Omega}
,
D_{e}
, and the indicated data, but not the specific
K
.

Proof.

We begin with analysis of the map
j_{\mu}
. In case (a) the claim follows from a combination of Lemma
6.5
and the uniformly summable Rellich-Kondrachov theorem
4.30
. In case (b) one instead uses the extension map
e_{\mu}:\,\mathcal{H}^{\mu}(D_{e})\to H^{m\mu}(M;E)
of Definition
5.22
to again reduce to the uniformly summable Rellich-Kondrachov theorem. The argument is completely analogous to the reduction to the boundaryless case in the proof of Theorem
4.30
.

We turn to the map
j_{-\mu}
. Summability and norm estimate for this map follow algebraically from that of
j_{\mu}
, so that we may treat both cases for
K
and
D_{e}
simultaneously. For
u,v\in L^{2}(\Omega)
we have

\langle u,(j_{-\mu})^{*}j_{-\mu}v\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle j_{-\mu}u,j_{-\mu}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}}=\langle u,(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\mu}v\rangle_{L^{2}}\;,

so that
(j_{-\mu})^{*}j_{-\mu}=(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\mu}
. The polar decomposition of
j_{-\mu}
thus takes the form
j_{-\mu}=U(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{\mu}{2}}
with
U:\,L^{2}(\Omega;E)\to\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})
a partial isometry. Let
P_{K}=\mathbf{1}_{K}
denote the projection
L^{2}(\Omega;E)\to L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)
. For
u\in L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)
we may then write

j_{-\mu}u=j_{\mu}P_{K}u=U(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{\mu}{2}}P_{K}u=U\left((P_{K}j_{\mu})\cdot(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{\mu}{2}}\right)^{*}u\;.

The map
P_{K}j_{\mu}:\,\mathcal{H}^{\mu}(D_{e})\to L_{K}^{2}(\Omega;E)
coincides with the map

\mathcal{H}^{\mu}(D_{e})\xlongrightarrow{P_{K}^{\mu}}\mathcal{H}^{\mu}(D_{e})\cap L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)\xlongrightarrow{j_{\mu}}L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)\;,

where
P_{K}^{\mu}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto the closed subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}(D_{e})\cap L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)
. Thus
P_{K}j_{\mu}
is
p
-summable for
p>\frac{n}{m\mu}
, and satisfies the corresponding norm estimate. It follows that the same is true for
j_{-\mu}:\,L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)\to\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})
.
∎

Using this adaptation of the uniformly summable Rellich-Kondrachov theorem we deduce that if an operator has negative order on the formal scale of a boundary condition, then it is uniformly locally compact.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
D_{e}
be a boundary condition for
D
. Let
\mu>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
T\in\mathfrak{B}(L^{2}(\Omega;E))
, and
\mu>0
.

(i)

If
Tf
extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
with norm satisfying

\|Tf\|\leq\|f\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot C(\mu,R,\delta)

for some function
C
of
\mu
, the diameter
R
of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
, and the distance
\delta
of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
, then
Tf\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\Omega)
.

(ii)

Suppose that
D_{e}
is semi-interpolation-regular. If
Tf
extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
with norm satisfying

\|Tf\|\leq\|f\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot C^{\prime}(\mu,R)

for some function
C^{\prime}
of
\mu
and the diameter
R
of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
, then
Tf\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\overline{\Omega})
.

Proof.

We prove (i), the proof of (ii) is analogous. Fix
L,R\geq 0
and
\delta>0
, and suppose
f\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R,\delta}(\Omega)
. Lemma
4.21
provides a function
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
with
\psi=1
on
K:=\mathrm{supp}(f)
with
\mathrm{supp}(\psi)\subseteq N_{\delta/2}(K)
and
\|\psi\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
bounded by some constant dependent only on
L
,
R
and
\delta
. It follows that
T\psi
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
with norm bounded by some constant also depending only on
L
,
R
and
\delta
. Then
Tf=(T\psi)f
factors as

L^{2}(\Omega;E)\xlongrightarrow{f}L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)\xlongrightarrow{j_{-\mu}}\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\xlongrightarrow{T\psi}L^{2}(\Omega;E)\;.

The composition of the first two maps is
p
-summable for all sufficiently large
p
, hence so is the composition of all maps. Combining this with the relevant norm estimates we conclude that
Tf
is
p
-summable for all
f\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R,\delta}(\Omega)
with
p
-norm bounded by a uniform constant depending only on
L
,
R
and
\delta
. By Lemmas
4.20
and
10.9
this suffices to prove the claim.
∎

We note that there is an analogous version of Proposition
6.7
for operators
T
with the property that
fT
extends to a bounded map
L^{2}(\Omega;E)\to\mathcal{H}^{\mu}(D_{e})
with an analogous norm estimate. Such operators then satisfy
fT\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
. This remark also applies to the following corollary. Observe that the conditions of Proposition
6.7
are in particular satisfied if
T
itself extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
for some
\mu>0
. Thus we may draw the following corollary.

Corollary 6.8
.

Suppose
T\in\mathfrak{B}(L^{2}(\Omega;E))
extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
for some
\mu>0
. Then
Tf\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\Omega)
. If
D_{e}
is semi-interpolation-regular, then
Tf\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\overline{\Omega})
.

An analogous statement also holds for commutators: If the commutator of an operator with all functions has negative order, then that operator is uniformly pseudo-local.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
D_{e}
be a boundary condition
D
. Let
\mu>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
T\in\mathfrak{B}(L^{2}(\Omega;E))
.

(i)

If the commutators
[f,T]
and
[f,T^{*}]
extend to bounded maps
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
with norms satisfying

\left\|[f,T]\right\|,\,\left\|[f,T^{*}]\right\|\leq\|f\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot C(\mu,R,\delta)\;,

for some function
C
of
\mu
, the diameter
R
of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
, and the distance
\delta
of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
, then
[f,T]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\Omega)
.

(ii)

Suppose that
D_{e}
is semi-interpolation-regular. If the commutators
[f,T]
and
[f,T^{*}]
extend to bounded maps
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
with norms satisfying

\left\|[f,T]\right\|,\,\left\|[f,T^{*}]\right\|\leq\|f\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot C^{\prime}(\mu,R)\;,

for some function
C^{\prime}
of
\mu
and the diameter
R
of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
, then
[f,T]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\overline{\Omega})
.

Proof.

We prove (i), the proof of (ii) is again analogous. Fix
L,R\geq 0
and
\delta>0
, and let
f\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R,k,\delta}
. Use Lemma
4.21
to produce a function
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
such that
\psi=1
on the support of
f
,
\mathrm{supp}(\psi)\subseteq N_{\delta/2}(\mathrm{supp}(\psi))
, and such that
\|\psi\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
is bounded by a constant depending only on
L
,
R
and
\delta
. Write

[f,T]=[\psi f,T]=\psi[f,T]+[\psi,T]f=-\left([f,T^{*}]\bar{\psi}\right)^{*}+[\psi,T]f\;.

Both
[f,T^{*}]
and
[\psi,T]
extend to bounded operators
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
by assumption, and their norms are bounded by constants dependent only on
L
,
R
and
\delta
. The claim then follows from Corollary
6.8
. To be a bit more precise: The operator
[\psi,T]f
factors as

L^{2}(\Omega;E)\xlongrightarrow{f}L^{2}_{K}(\Omega;E)\xlongrightarrow{j_{-\mu}}\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\xlongrightarrow{[\psi,T]}L^{2}(\Omega;E)\;,

with
K
denoting the support of
f
. The middle map is
p
-summable for sufficiently large
p
with
p
-norm dependent only on
R
, hence so is the entire composition, and the norm estimate for
[f,T]
implies the
p
-norm to be bounded by a constant depending only on
L
,
R
and
\delta
. The term
[f,T^{*}]\bar{\psi}
admits an analogous conclusion. Proposition
10.9
then yields the claim.
∎

23.3
Commutators with functions

In this section we investigate commutators of functions of a boundary condition
D_{e}
with smooth functions on
\Omega
. The result will be analogous to the fact that the commutator of an operator of order
\beta
with a smooth function has order
\beta-1
. In particular we will be able to conclude the uniform pseudo-locality of the bounded transform
D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Holomorphic functional calculus is our tool of choice. Suppose that
T
is a closed operator,
U\subseteq\mathbb{C}
an open neighborhood of the spectrum
\sigma(T)
,
\psi
a holomorphic function on
U
, and
\Gamma
a curve in
U\setminus\sigma(T)
circumscribing
\sigma(T)
in positive direction. Then the holomorphic functional calculus is defined as

\psi(T)=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{\Gamma}\psi(z)(T-z)^{-1}dz\;.

If
T
is self-adjoint, then it coincides with the Borel functional calculus. In this case the holomorphic functional calculus is a convenient way to obtain an integral representation in terms of resolvents, and this is the way in which we use it. Indeed, it essentially suffices to prove the desired uniform pseudo-compactness for resolvents, the statement for holomorphic functions then follows via holomorphic functional calculus. The use of integral representations to reduce pseudo-locality to the case of resolvents is a standard technique (see for example
[
Kas88
]
or
[
BDT89
]
). We use the holomorphic functional calculus here because it covers a reasonably large class of functions
73
73

73

Note that the holomorphic functional calculus can also be extended to functions with almost-analytic extensions (see for example
[
DS99
, Chapter 8]
), including for example compactly supported smooth functions, so that our results can be extended to this class of functions. We will not have need for this however.
, but the choice is ultimately arbitrary.

As usual let
D_{e}
be boundary condition for the uniformly elliptic operator
D
of order
m
. Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
be a real-valued function. Throughout this section we shall assume that either:

•

D_{e}
is arbitrary, and
f\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
, or

•

D_{e}
is local and semi-interpolation-regular, and
f
is arbitrary.

Throughout, we also let
\mu:=\frac{1}{m}
.

Lemma 6.10
.

The commutator
[f,D_{e}]
extends to a bounded operator from
\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})
to
L^{2}(\Omega;E)
, and
[f,D_{e}^{*}D_{e}]
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{-1}(D_{e})
. In either case the operator norm of the commutator has a bound proportional to
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
, where the proportionality constant may depend on the distance of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
if
D_{e}
is arbitrary and
f
supported in the interior.

Proof.

First consider the case that
D_{e}
is arbitrary and
f\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
. It suffices to prove that
\|[f,D_{e}]u\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})}
for all
u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(D_{e})
. To that end note first that
[f,D_{e}]u=0
if
u
has support disjoint from that of
f
. Using a smooth cut-off we see that it suffices to prove the claim for
u
supported in a
\delta
-neighborhood of the support of
f
, where
\delta
can be chosen sufficiently small so that this neighborhood does not intersect
\partial\Omega
. By Lemma
6.5
we have
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})\cap L^{2}_{N_{\delta}(K)}(\Omega;E)\subseteq H^{m\alpha}_{N_{\delta}(K)}(M;E)
with equivalent norms for all
\alpha\geq 0
, where the constants in the norm equivalence may depend on
\delta
, but not the specific
f
. Therefore it holds that

\|[f,D_{e}]u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;E)}\lesssim\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\|[f,D_{e}]u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;E)}=\|[f,D]u\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|u\|_{H^{m-1}(\overline{\Omega};E)}

for
u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(D_{e})
. The latter estimate holds because
[f,D]
is an operator of order
m-1
. This proves that
[f,D_{e}]
extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
in this case.

In the case where
D_{e}
is local and semi-interpolation-regular the proof is similar. Pick an extension
\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
of
f
such that
\|\tilde{f}\|_{C^{m}_{b}(M)}\leq 2\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
. Also, pick an extension
\tilde{D}
of
D
to a uniformly elliptic operator over
M
. Recall that by semi-interpolation-regularity there are bounded extension maps
e_{\alpha}:\,\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})\to H^{m\alpha}(M;E)
,
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\|[f,D_{e}]u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;E)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|[\tilde{f},\tilde{D}]e_{1}(u)\right\|_{L^{2}(M;E)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\|e_{1}(u)\|_{H^{m-1}(M;E)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})}\;,

where we have used that
e_{1}(u)=e_{1-\mu}(u)\in\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})
, and that the map
e_{1-\mu}
is a Banach space isomorphism onto its image. The shows that
[f,D_{e}]
extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
in this case as well.

We deduce the mapping properties of
[f,D^{*}_{e}D_{e}]
from the mapping properties on Sobolev spaces again. We prove it in the case where
D_{e}
is local and semi-interpolation-regular. The other case is analogous. Note first that it suffices by Proposition
6.1
that

\left|\left\langle[f,D_{e}^{*}D_{e}]u,v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right|\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})}\cdot\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(D_{e})}

for all
u,v\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(D_{e})
. One quickly computes that

\left\langle[f,D_{e}^{*}D_{e}]u,v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\left\langle[f,D_{e}]u,D_{e}v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-\left\langle D_{e}u,[f,D_{e}]v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\;.

The first summand satisfies the desired estimate because of the first part of the claim. For the second summand we again make use of the extension maps. With
\tilde{f}
and
\tilde{D}
as above one has

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle D_{e}u,[f,D_{e}]v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\left|\langle D_{e}u,[f,D_{e}]v\rangle_{E}\right|d\mathrm{vol}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\left|\langle\tilde{D}e_{1}(u),[\tilde{f},\tilde{D}]e_{1}(v)\rangle_{E}\right|d\mathrm{vol}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\left|\langle\tilde{D}e_{1}(u),[\tilde{f},\tilde{D}]e_{1}(v)\rangle_{E}\right|d\mathrm{vol}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\left\|e_{1}(u)\right\|_{H^{m-1}(M)}\cdot\|e_{1}(v)\|_{H^{m}(M)}\;.

To get to the last line we used that
[\tilde{f},\tilde{D}]
is a bounded map
H^{m}(M;E)\to H^{1}(M;E)
with norm bounded by a multiple of
\|\tilde{f}\|_{C^{m}_{b}(M)}\leq 2\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
, that
\tilde{D}
is a bounded map
H^{m-1}(M;E)\to H^{-1}(M;E)
, and that the
L^{2}
-inner product is a perfect pairing between
H^{-1}(M;E)
and
H^{1}(M;E)
. We conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle D_{e}u,[f,D_{e}]v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\left\|e_{1}(u)\right\|_{H^{m-1}(M)}\cdot\|e_{1}(v)\|_{H^{m}(M)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})}\cdot\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(D_{e})}\;.

Combination with the corresponding estimate for the first summand we deduce the claim for the commutator
[f,D_{e}^{*}D_{e}]
.
∎

From here on out the calculations are purely formal, and dependent only on the validity of the above lemma. We first deduce that the commutator of the resolvent with
f
has lower order.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)
and let
\gamma\geq 0
. The commutator
\left[f,\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-z\right)^{-1}\right]
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-1-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{1-\gamma}(D_{e})
. Its norm satisfies

\left\|\left[f,\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-z\right)^{-1}\right]\right\|\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot|z|^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\;,

(31)

where the implicit constant in (
31
) may depend on the distance of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
if
D_{e}
is arbitrary and
f
supported in the interior.

Proof.

The formula

\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-z\right)\left[f,\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-z\right)^{-1}\right]\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-z\right)=\left[D_{e}^{*}D_{e},f\right]

is straightforwardly verified to hold on
\mathcal{H}^{1}(D_{e})
. It follows that the left-hand side extends to a map
\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{-1}(D_{e})
, or equivalently that
\left[f,\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I\right)^{-1}\right]
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-1-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{1}(D_{e})
. Thus it also extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-1-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{1-\gamma}(D_{e})
for all
\gamma\geq 0
. Moreover, the norm of
(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-z)^{-1}
as a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{\alpha+2-\gamma}(D_{e})
is bounded by
\|(x^{2}-z)^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{2-\gamma}}
by Proposition
6.3
. An elementary calculation shows this to be proportional to
|z|^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}
. Together with the norm estimate for
[f,D^{*}_{e}D_{e}]
this implies the estimate (
31
).
∎

From resolvents we then extend to holomorphic functions via the holomorphic functional calculus. Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and define
\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}
as the space of holomorphic functions
\psi:\,U\to\mathbb{C}
with
U\subseteq\mathbb{C}
an open neighborhhod of the positive real axis, such that
\sup_{z\in U}|z|^{\beta}|\psi(z)|<\infty
.

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
\psi\in\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}
,
0<\beta\leq 1
. Then
\left[f,\psi(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)\right]
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-1-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{-1+2\beta-\gamma}(D_{e})
for all
\gamma>0
, with norm bound proportional to
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
, where the proportionality constant may depend on the distance of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
if
D_{e}
is arbitrary and
f
supported in the interior.

Proof.

According to the holomorphic functional calculus we may write

\psi(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{\Gamma}\psi(z)\cdot(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+1-z)^{-1}\,dz\;,

so that

\left[f,\psi(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)\right]=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{\Gamma}\psi(z)\cdot\left[f,(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+1-z)^{-1}\right]\,dz

By Lemma
6.11

\left[f,\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-z\right)^{-1}\right]
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-1-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{1-\gamma^{\prime}}(D_{e})
for all
\gamma^{\prime}\geq 0
, and its norm is bounded by
\left\|f\right\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot|z|^{-\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{2}}
. It follows that
\left[f,\psi(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)\right]
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-1-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{1-\gamma^{\prime}}(D_{e})
whenever the integral
\int_{\Gamma}\left|\psi(z)\right|\cdot|z|^{-\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{2}}\,dz
converges, and that in this case the norm is bounded by
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
times the value of that integral. Since
\psi\in\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}
, meaning that
|\psi(z)|\lesssim|z|^{-\beta}
, this is the case if
-\beta-\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{2}<-1
, i.e. if
\gamma^{\prime}>2(1-\beta)
. Defining
\gamma:=2(\beta-1)+\gamma^{\prime}
the claim follows.
∎

We cannot extend the result of Proposition
6.12
to cover
\gamma=0
. This is because an infinitessimal amount of
\gamma
is needed to ensure that the integral representation of
[f,\psi(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)]
to converge in the operator norm of maps from
\mathcal{H}^{-1-\mu}\to\mathcal{H}^{-1+2\beta-\gamma}
. This is not a fundamental limitation, but rather an artifact of our choice to treat
\psi(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)
via an integral representation. However, using duality and interpolation we can extend the statement to more domains. Indeed, suppose that
\psi\in\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}
. It holds that

\left\langle\left[f,\psi(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)\right]u,v\right\rangle_{H}=\left\langle u,\left[\bar{\psi}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I),f\right]v\right\rangle_{H}\;,

for all
u,v\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(D_{e})
. It (almost) never holds that
\bar{\psi}
is in
\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}
, but if
\psi
is real-valued on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, then
\bar{\psi}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)=\psi(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)
. In this case the conditions for Lemma
6.2
are satisfied, and Proposition
6.12
can be strengthened as follows.

Corollary 6.13
.

Let
\psi\in\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}
,
\beta\geq 0
, and suppose that
\psi
is real-valued on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Then
\left[f,\psi(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)\right]
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{\alpha+2\beta+\mu-\gamma}(D_{e})
for all
\alpha\in[-1-\mu,1-2\beta]
and all
\gamma>0
, with norm bound proportional to
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
, where the proportionality constant may depend on the distance of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
if
D_{e}
is arbitrary and
f
supported in the interior.

The additional range provided by the previous corollary allows us to prove that the the commutator of the bounded transform with
f
has negative order.

Corollary 6.14
.

The commutator
\left[f,D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\gamma}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
for all
0<\gamma<\mu
, with norm bound proportional to
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
, where the proportionality constant may depend on the distance of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
if
D_{e}
is arbitrary and
f
supported in the interior.

Proof.

Write

\left[f,D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]=\left[f,D_{e}\right](D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}+D_{e}\left[f,(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]\;.

The first summand extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
, with norm bounded by a multiple of
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
. For the second summand note that
(z^{2}+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathcal{O}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is real-valued for
z\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. The commutator
\left[f,(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]
thus extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\gamma}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{-\gamma+1+\mu-\gamma^{\prime}}(D_{e})
for all
0\leq\gamma\leq 1+\mu
and all
\gamma^{\prime}>0
by the previous corollary, again with norm bounded by a multiple of
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
. In either case the norm bound may depend on the distance of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
if
D_{e}
is arbitrary and
f
supported in the interior. Taking
0<\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma<\mu
we get that
\left[f,(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\gamma}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{1}(D_{e})
. Since
D_{e}
maps
\mathcal{H}^{1}(D_{e})
to
L^{2}(\Omega;E)
the claim follows.
∎

Then, the appeal to Proposition
6.9
lets us conclude the desired uniform pseudo-locality.

Corollary 6.15
.

Let
D_{e}
be a boundary condition for the uniformly elliptic operator
D
over
\overline{\Omega}
.

(i)

\left[f,D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\Omega)
.

(ii)

If
D_{e}
is local and semi-interpolation-regular, then
\left[f,D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\overline{\Omega})
.

23.4
Lower-order perturbations

We come to the comparison of boundary conditions for two uniformly elliptic operators that differ only by lower-order terms. In particular this includes the case of two boundary conditions for the same operator. Our strategy is analogous to that of the previous subsection: Use comparison to Sobolev spaces to argue that the difference between the operators has lower order on the formal scale, deduce that the analogous statement holds for resolvents as well, then extend to holomorphic functions via holomorphic functional calculus, and finally derive the desired result for the bounded transforms. This process is slightly complicated by the fact that the formal scales of the two boundary conditions are generally different, so that there is no common scale on which to work. This issue is solved by interior regularity. Thus the results of this subsection work only on the interior
\Omega
. While an extension to
\overline{\Omega}
might be possible in favorable circumstances, we will not investigate this further.

Throughout this section we consider the following situation:
Let
D
and
\tilde{D}
be uniformly elliptic operators over
\overline{\Omega}
acting on sections of the same bundles. Assume that
D
and
\tilde{D}
both have order
m
, and that
D-\tilde{D}
is a differential operator of order
\leq m-1
. Let
D_{e}
and
\tilde{D}_{e}
be boundary conditions for
D
and
\tilde{D}
respectively. Again, we set
\mu:=\frac{1}{m}
. Lastly, let
f\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
be a smooth function with compact support in the interior.

Let us start with the following observation. Interior regularity for the uniformly elliptic operators
D
and
\tilde{D}
implies that
f
maps the domains of
D_{e}
and
\tilde{D}_{e}
to
\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega;E)\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\cap\mathrm{dom}(\tilde{D}_{e})
, and the norm of
f
is bounded by a multiple of
\|f\|_{C^{m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
with regards to any of the equivalent norms to put on
\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega;E)\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(D_{e})\cap\mathrm{dom}(\tilde{D}_{e})
. Similarly,
f
maps the domains of
D^{*}_{e}D_{e}
and
\tilde{D}^{*}_{e}\tilde{D}_{e}
to
\tilde{H}^{2m}(\Omega;E)\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(D^{*}_{e}D_{e})\cap\mathrm{dom}(\tilde{D}^{*}_{e}\tilde{D}_{e})
with norm bounded by a multiple of
\|f\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
. In either case the norm bound may depend on the distance of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
. As a consequence of the latter, it makes sense to consider
(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e})f
as a bounded operator from
\mathcal{H}^{2}(D_{e})=\mathrm{dom}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
. Then, by arguing via Sobolev spaces in an analogous manner to the last subsection one proves the following.

Lemma 6.16
.

The operators
[f,D_{e}^{*}D_{e}]
and
\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}\right)f
extend to bounded operators
\mathcal{H}^{2-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
, with norms bounded by
\|f\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
and a constant depending on the distance of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
.

From this we again derive the corresponding statement for resolvents. Now we multiply the difference of the resolvents from both sides by functions with compact support in the interior. This produces an operator that acts on a single scale.

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
f,g\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
, and let
\gamma\geq 0
,
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)
. The operator

g\left((D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-z)^{-1}-(\tilde{D}^{*}_{e}\tilde{D}_{e}-z)^{-1}\right)f

extends to bounded operators
\mathcal{H}^{-1-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{1-\gamma}(D_{e})
, with norm bounded by
\|g\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\|f\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot|z|^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}
, and a constant depending on the distances of the supports of
f
and
g
to
\partial\Omega
.

Proof.

Write
R:=D_{e}^{*}D_{e}-z
and
\tilde{R}:=\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}-z
. On the intersection
\mathrm{dom}(R)\cap\mathrm{dom}(\tilde{R})
the formula

\left(R^{-1}-\tilde{R}^{-1}\right)R=\tilde{R}^{-1}\left(\tilde{R}-R\right)

holds. Since
f
maps
\mathrm{dom}(R)
to
\mathrm{dom}(R)\cap\mathrm{dom}(\tilde{R})
we can thus calculate as follows:

\displaystyle\left(R^{-1}-\tilde{R}^{-1}\right)f

\displaystyle=\left(R^{-1}-\tilde{R}^{-1}\right)\left(RfR^{-1}+\left[f,R\right]R^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{R}^{-1}\left(\left(\tilde{R}-R\right)f-\left[f,R\right]\right)R^{-1}+R^{-1}\left[f,R\right]R^{-1}\;.

By the previous lemma the operator
\left(\left(\tilde{R}-R\right)f-\left[f,R\right]\right)R^{-1}
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
with norm bounded by
\|f\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
and a constant dependent on the distance of
\mathrm{supp}(f)
to
\partial\Omega
, so that the first summand extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{2-\gamma}(\tilde{D}_{e})
with norm bounded by
\|f\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot|z|^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}
. The second summand extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{2-\gamma}(D_{e})
, again with an analogous norm bound. Now, multiplication by
g
is a bounded map on
\mathcal{H}^{2-\gamma}(D_{e})
with norm bounded by
\|g\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
and a constant depending on the distance of
\mathrm{supp}(g)
to
\partial\Omega
. We conclude that
g(R^{-1}-\tilde{R}^{-1})f
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{2-\gamma}(D_{e})
with norm bounded by
\|g\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\|f\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}|z|^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}
and a constant depending on the distance of the respective supports to
\partial\Omega
. Note that the same is also true for

\left(g\left(R^{-1}-\tilde{R}^{-1}\right)f\right)^{*}=\bar{f}\left(R^{-1}-\tilde{R}^{-1}\right)\bar{g}\;.

Thus Lemma
6.2
implies that
g(R^{-1}-\tilde{R}^{-1})f
actually extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{\alpha+\mu+2-\gamma}(D_{e})
for all
\alpha
between
-2
and
-\mu
with an analogous norm bound. The claim is the special case of
\alpha=-1-\mu
.
∎

Analogously to Proposition
6.12
and Corollary
6.13
the result may again be extended to functions of
D_{e}^{*}D_{e}
and
\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}
via holomorphic functional calculus.

Proposition 6.18
.

Let
f,g\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
, and let
\psi\in\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}
,
0\leq\beta\leq 1
, be real-valued on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Then, the operator

g\left(\psi(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)-\psi(\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}+I)\right)f

extends to bounded maps
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(D_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{\alpha+\mu+2\beta-\gamma}(D_{e})
and
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\tilde{D}_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{\alpha+\mu+2\beta-\gamma}(\tilde{D}_{e})
for all
\alpha\in[-1-\mu,1-2\beta]
and all
\gamma>0
, with norms bounded by
\|g\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\|f\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
, and a constant depending on the distances of the supports of
f
and
g
to
\partial\Omega
.

Again, we use this to derive the uniform local compactness statement for the bounded-transforms that we are ultimately after.

Corollary 6.19
.

\left(D_{e}\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\tilde{D}_{e}\left(\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)f\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0\quad\mathrm{for}\;f\in C_{0}(\Omega)\;.

Proof.

Fix
L,R\geq 0
and
\delta>0
, and let
f\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R,2m,\delta}(\Omega)
. Also, fix a bump function
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
with
\psi=1
on the support of
f
,
\mathrm{supp}(\psi)\subseteq N_{\delta/2}(\mathrm{supp}(f))
, and
\|\psi\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
bounded by a constant dependent only on
L
,
R
and
\delta
. Then, write

\displaystyle\left(D_{e}\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\tilde{D}_{e}\left(\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)f

\displaystyle=D_{e}f\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\tilde{D}_{e}f\left(\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\psi

\displaystyle\quad-D_{e}\left[f,\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]+\tilde{D}_{e}\left[f,\left(\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]\psi

\displaystyle=\left(D_{e}-\tilde{D}_{e}\right)f\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\tilde{D}_{e}f\left(\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\left(\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\psi

\displaystyle\quad-D_{e}\left[f,\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]+\tilde{D}_{e}\left[f,\left(\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right]\psi

The first summand extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
, because
(D_{e}-\tilde{D}_{e})f
extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{1-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
. The third summand extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
by the results of the previous subsection. The fourth summand analogously extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\mu}(\tilde{D}_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
. The norm of each summand is bounded by
\|\psi\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\|f\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
, and a constant depending on
\delta
. It remains to treat the second summand. Proposition
6.18
implies via an analogous argument to that in the proof of Corollary
6.14
that

f\left(\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\left(\tilde{D}_{e}^{*}\tilde{D}_{e}+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\psi

extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-\gamma}(\tilde{D}_{e})\to\mathcal{H}^{1}(\tilde{D}_{e})
for
0<\gamma<\mu
, with norm again bounded by
\|\psi\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\|f\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
and a
\delta
-dependent constant. Thus the second summand extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{-\gamma}(\tilde{D}_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
with an analogous norm bound. Now,
\|\psi\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}\cdot\|f\|_{C^{2m}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})}
is bounded by a constant dependent only on
L
,
R
and
\delta
. We conclude that each of the summands is
p
-summable for all
p>\frac{n}{m\mu}
with
p
-norm bounded by a constant dependent only on
L
,
R
and
\delta
. Proposition
10.9
thus yields the claim.
∎

23.5
A note on pseudo-differential operators

Pseudo-differential operators are not treated in this thesis, but let us remark upon them here briefly. Consider a domain
\Omega
in a manifold of bounded geometry
M
. In line with Definition
5.2
we could call a linear operator
P:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega)\to\Gamma(\overline{\Omega})
a
uniformly elliptic pseudo-differential operator of order
m
over
\overline{\Omega}
if there exists a uniform pseudo-differential operator
74
74

74

There are slightly different notions of pseudo-differential operators over manifolds of bounded geometry in use
[
Kor91
]
,
[
Shu92
]
,
[
Tay08
]
,
[
Eng18a
]
, and hence also of uniformly elliptic pseudo-differential operators. The author had the definitions used in
[
Eng18a
]
in mind, but all that we need here is that any uniform pseudo-differential operator can be made to have arbitrarily small propagation by a uniformly smoothing perturbation (for example
[
Eng18a
, Remark 4.3]
), and that uniformly elliptic pseudo-differential operators satisfy an elliptic estimate (for example
[
Eng18a
, Theorem 6.7]
).

\tilde{P}:\,\Gamma_{c}(M)\to\Gamma(M)
of order
m
such that
Pu=(\tilde{P}u)|_{\Omega}
for all
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega)
. We do not claim that this is necessarily the correct notion for uniformly elliptic pseudo-differential operators on domains, but it is the one that is most immediately accessible by our techniques. Note that at least any uniformly elliptic differential operator over
\overline{\Omega}
has a uniformly elliptic parametrix (i.e. uniformly pseudo-differential quasi-inverse) of this type, obtained by restricting a uniformly elliptic parametrix (existent for example by
[
Eng18a
, Theorem 6.5]
) for any uniformly elliptic extension
\tilde{P}:\,\Gamma_{c}(M)\to\Gamma_{c}(M)
to
\Omega
. In any case, such an operator
P
satisfies an interior regularity estimate of the form

\|u\|_{H^{s}(M)}\leq C_{\delta,s}\left(\|Pu\|_{H^{s-m}(M)}+\|u\|_{H^{s-m}(M)}\right)

for all
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega)
with
d(\mathrm{supp}(u),\partial\Omega)\geq\delta
. This estimate can be derived from the elliptic estimate for any uniformly elliptic extension
\tilde{P}
and the fact that
\tilde{P}
and hence
P
can be made to have propagation
<\delta
by a uniformly smoothing perturbation. The constant
C_{\delta,s}
generally depends on
\delta
, and there is no a priori reason why it should not blow up as
\delta\to 0
. In particular we cannot conclude that the minimal domain of
P
, again defined as the graph-norm closure of
\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega)
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, coincides with
\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega)
. This
\delta
-stratified interior regularity estimate suffices to show that
\mathrm{dom}(P_{\min})\cap L^{2}_{\Omega\setminus U_{\delta}(\partial\Omega)}(\Omega)
and
\tilde{H}^{m}(\Omega\setminus N_{\delta}(\partial\Omega))
are equal with equivalent norms for every
\delta
. Using Lemma
6.5
this is enough to extend the conclusions of this section regarding uniform compactness over the interior
\Omega
to uniformly elliptic pseudo-differential operators over
\overline{\Omega}
. In principle the uniform compactness of certain commutators over
\overline{\Omega}
could also be concluded for these operators, if we were willing to assume the existence of semi-interpolation-regular boundary condition. However, given that we cannot even say that the minimal boundary condition has this property the author sees no reason to expect such extension to exist.

In a similar way one could also embed elliptic differential operators exhibiting certain kinds of singularity on the boundary into our setting. Indeed, let
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega)
such that for every
\delta>0
the operator
D
is uniformly elliptic over
\overline{\Omega}\setminus U_{\delta}(\partial\Omega)
. Such an operator admits a
\delta
-stratified interior regularity estimate just as that for uniformly elliptic pseudo-differential operators above. Analogously the conclusion of this section regarding uniform compactness on the interior also hold for these operators. Examples include operators with singular coefficients, such as
x\partial_{x}+i\partial_{y}
and
x^{-1}\partial_{x}+i\partial_{y}
on the half-plane
\{x>0\}
, or operators that fail to admit extensions to
M
for topological reasons, such as
e^{i\varphi/2}(\partial_{x}+i\partial_{y})
,
\varphi
being the argument of
(x,y)
, on the slit plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
, viewed as a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

24
The uniform K-homology classes of an elliptic operator

With the tools developed in the previous section we are ready to prove the existence of the uniform K-homology classes associated to uniformly elliptic operators over domains. There is one more technicality to take care of first: the metric in use. Let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry, and
\Omega\subseteq M
a domain. So far we have exclusively worked with the subspace metric on
\overline{\Omega}
. However, if
\overline{\Omega}
is a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry, then the Riemannian metric induces a path-length metric on
\overline{\Omega}
. Especially when taking the intrinsic viewpoint on manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry this is the natural metric to put on
\overline{\Omega}
. The path-length metric is different from the subspace metric unless
\overline{\Omega}
is geodesically convex in
M
. It is always true that the subspace metric is dominated by the path-length metric, but generally the two do not even have to be equivalent.
75
75

75

As an example consider
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
to be the complement of
\{|x|\leq 1\}\cup\{x_{1}\geq 0,\,|x_{2}|\leq 1\}
. Technically speaking the boundary
\partial\Omega
is differentiable but not smooth in this example, but this does not effect the spirit of the example. Then, we consider the points
x_{n},y_{n}\in\Omega
,
x_{n}=(n,2)
,
y_{n}=(n,-2)
. Then, the difference between
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
in the subspace metric induced by the standard metric on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is constant in
n
. On the other hand any path in
\Omega
connecting
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
must run around the origin, so that the distance between the two in the path-length metric of
\Omega
diverges as
n\to\infty
. Thus the two metrics cannot be equivalent.
Since we would also like to state that uniformly elliptic operators define uniform Fredholm modules over
\overline{\Omega}
equipped with the path-length metric, this requires further discussion.

There is an ad-hoc solution. Any Riemannian metric making
\overline{\Omega}
into a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry is boundedly homotopic to one that has product structure near the boundary
[
Sch98a
, Proposition 7.3]
. Then,
\overline{\Omega}
can be isometrically embedded as a geodesically convex submanifold by attaching cylinders with product metric to the boundary. In this case the subspace and path-length metric agree, and the path-length metric of the homotoped Riemannian metric is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to the original one.

Instead we can also note that due to bounded geometry the path-length and the subspace metric are equivalent on sufficiently small scales. Together with the knowledge that the subspace metric is dominated by the path-length metric this suffices to argue that the identity map provides a filterd map from
\overline{\Omega}
with the former metric to
\overline{\Omega}
with the latter. Thus, if we show that uniformly elliptic operators give rise to uniform Fredholm modules over
\overline{\Omega}
with the subspace metric, then they automatically do so with the path-length metric as well.

This and related statements are collected in the following lemma. Let us introduce some notation first. Denote by
d_{\overline{\Omega}}
the path-length metric on
\overline{\Omega}
induced by the restriction of the Riemannian metric of
M
to
\overline{\Omega}
. Denote by
d_{M}
the subspace metric on
\overline{\Omega}
induced by the path length metric on
M
. Moreover, denote by
d_{\partial\Omega}
the path-length metric on
\partial\Omega
induced by the restriction of the Riemannian metric on
M
to
\partial\Omega
. Further denote by
d_{M}
and
d_{\overline{\Omega}}
denote the subspace metrics on
\partial\Omega
coming from the respective inclusions
\partial\Omega\subseteq M
and
\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
. Lastly, let
d_{\Omega}
denote the path-length metric on
\Omega
, and
d_{\overline{\Omega}}
the subspace metric coming from the inclusion
\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry, and
\Omega\subseteq M
a bounded-geometry domain.

(i)

The identity map
(\overline{\Omega},d_{M})\to(\overline{\Omega},d_{\overline{\Omega}})
is filtered.

(ii)

The identity maps
(\partial\Omega,d_{M})\to(\partial\Omega,d_{\partial\Omega})
and
(\partial\Omega,d_{\overline{\Omega}})\to(\partial\Omega,d_{\partial\Omega})
are filtered.

(iii)

The metrics
d_{\Omega}
and
d_{\overline{\Omega}}
are equal. In particular the identity map
(\Omega,d_{M})\to(\Omega,d_{\Omega})
is also filtered.

(iv)

Let
M^{\prime}
be another manifold of bounded geometry, and let
d_{1}=d_{M}+d_{M^{\prime}}
denote the product metric, and
d_{g}
the path-length metric induced by the product Riemannian metric
g_{M}\oplus g_{M^{\prime}}
. Then the identity map
(M\times M^{\prime},d_{g})\to(M\times M^{\prime},d_{1})
is a bi-Lipschitz equivalence.

Proof.

We prove (i), the proof of (ii) is similar. Fix
L,R\geq 0
. It suffices to prove that there exist
L^{\prime},R^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(\overline{\Omega},d_{\overline{\Omega}})\subseteq L^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R^{\prime}}(\overline{\Omega},d_{M})
. Let
f\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(\overline{\Omega},d_{\overline{\Omega}})
. Because
d_{M}\leq d_{\overline{\Omega}}
it holds that

\mathrm{diam}_{d_{M}}\left(\mathrm{supp}(f)\right)\leq\mathrm{diam}_{d_{\overline{\Omega}}}\left(\mathrm{supp}(f)\right)\leq R\;.

For the Lipschitz property fix some small
\delta>0
, say smaller than the injectivity radii of
M
and
\partial\Omega
as well as half the thickness of a uniformly thick tubular neighborhood of
\partial\Omega
. If
d_{M}(x,y)\geq\delta
, then

\left|f(x)-f(y)\right|\leq 2\|f\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2}{\delta}d_{M}(x,y)\;,

where we used that
f
as the element of some
L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}
has
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Now suppose that
d_{M}(x,y)<\delta
. Due to the bounded geometry of
M
and
\overline{\Omega}
there is a uniform bi-Lipschitz equivalence from
B_{\delta}^{d_{M}}(x)\cap\overline{\Omega}
to the intersection of a Euclidean ball with half-space. The distance between
x
and
y
in this (potentially capped) ball is realized by the segment between them. Mapping this back to
\overline{\Omega}
produces a curve
\gamma
in
\overline{\Omega}
connecting
x
and
y
. Thus

d_{\overline{\Omega}}(x,y)\leq\mathrm{length}(\gamma)\leq C\cdot d_{M}(x,y)\;,

where
C
is a uniform constant coming from the uniform local bi-Lipschitz equivalence to half-space. We conclude that

\left|f(x)-f(y)\right|\leq L\cdot d_{\overline{\Omega}}(x,y)\leq LC\cdot d_{M}(x,y)\;.

Putting
L^{\prime}:=\max\{2\delta^{-1},LC\}
we have found that
f\in L^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R}(\overline{\Omega},d_{M})
.

For (iii) simply note that every curve in
\overline{\Omega}
between points in
\Omega
can be approximated arbitrarily well by curves through
\Omega
. For (iv) note that
d_{1}
is equivalent to
d_{2}=\sqrt{d_{M}^{2}+d_{M^{\prime}}^{2}}
. The metrics
d_{2}
and
d_{g}
can be shown to be equivalent by explicit inspection of curve lengths (see for example
[
Ngu22
]
).
∎

It is a consequence of Lemma
6.20
(i) that if
(H,\rho,F)
is a uniform Fredholm module over
(\overline{\Omega},d_{M})
, then it is also a uniform Fredholm module over
(\overline{\Omega},d_{\overline{\Omega}})
. Its class in
K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega},d_{\overline{\Omega}})
is the image of the class in
K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega},d_{M})
under the push-forward along the map
\mathrm{id}:\,(\overline{\Omega},d_{M})\to(\overline{\Omega},d_{\overline{\Omega}})
. Analogous remarks apply to the remaining items of that lemma. Consequently, we shall always work with the subspace metric on any domain, and leave implicit that on bounded-geometry domains any resulting (relative) uniform K-homology class can be pushed forward to the path-length metric as well.

Remark 6.21
.

Item (i) of the Lemma
6.20
straight-forwardly generalizes to the case where
\overline{\Omega}
is a uniform Lipschitz domain. In this case
\partial\Omega
could be understood as a Lipschitz hypersurface of bounded geometry. Since Lipschitz maps are smooth almost everywhere, so is
\partial\Omega
. Thus we could make sense of an a.e. defined Riemannian metric on
\partial\Omega
, which is still induces a path-length metric. Then item (ii) carries over to this situation as well. We will not pursue the geometry on Lipschitz hypersurfaces in this thesis, though see Remark
6.45
below. However, the second part of item (iii) cannot hold with out at least some boundedness and regularity assumption on the boundary, consider
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
in
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

With the metric-related technicalities sorted out we are ready to state and prove our cornerstone theorem on the existence of uniform K-homology classes of uniformly elliptic operators over domains.

Theorem 6.22
.

Let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry, and
\Omega\subseteq M
a domain. Let
E\to M
be a
p
-multigraded vector bundle of bounded geometry,
p\geq-1
. Let
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
be a symmetric and uniformly elliptic differential operator over
\overline{\Omega}
. Let
D_{e}
be a boundary condition for
D
. If
p\geq 0
, assume that
D
and
D_{e}
are odd and multigraded. Set
H:=L^{2}(\Omega;E)
, and let
\rho
the representation of either
C_{0}(\overline{\Omega})
or
C_{0}(\Omega)
on
H
by multiplication operators. Lastly, set

F_{e}:=D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\;.

Then:

(i)

(H,\rho,F_{e})
is a
p
-multigraded uniform Fredholm module over
\Omega
.

(ii)

Assume that
D_{e}
is local and semi-interpolation-regular. Then,
(H,\rho,F_{e})
is a
p
-multigraded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
.

(iii)

Assume that
D_{e}
is local, self-adjoint and interpolation-regular. Then,
(H,\rho,F_{e})
is a
p
-multigraded uniform Fredholm module over
\overline{\Omega}
.

In each case
\Omega
and
\overline{\Omega}
is endowed with the subspace metric from the inclusions
\Omega,\overline{\Omega}\subseteq M
. The resulting uniform K-homology classes enjoy the following properties:

(a)

Assume that
D_{e}
is local and semi-interpolation-regular. The excision map
\mathrm{exc}:\,K_{-p}^{u}(\overline{\Omega};\partial\Omega)\to K^{u}_{-p}(\Omega)
maps the class defined by (ii) to the class defined by (i).

(b)

Assume that
D_{e}
is local, self-adjoint and interpolation-regular. The restriction map
K^{u}_{-p}(\overline{\Omega})\to K^{u}_{-p}(\overline{\Omega};\partial\Omega)
maps the class defined by (iii) to the class defined by (ii).

(c)

The classes in
K^{u}_{-p}(\Omega)
and
K^{u}_{-p}(\overline{\Omega};\partial\Omega)
defined by (i) and (ii), respectively, are independent of the boundary condition, and depends only on the principal symbol of
D
.

(d)

Assume that the boundary condition
D_{e}
is self-adjoint. Then, the conclusions of (i), (ii), and (iii) also hold with
D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
replaced by
\chi(D_{e})
for any continuous odd function
\chi:\,\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\chi(\lambda)=1
. The resulting uniform K-homology classes are independent of
\chi
.

Proof.

The properties necessary to establish (i), (ii) and (iii) were essentially proved over the course of Section
23
. Let us begin with (i). Then
[F_{e},f]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\Omega)
by Corollary
6.15
. Furthermore,
I-F_{e}^{*}F_{e}=(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-1}
extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{-2}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
, so that Corollary
6.8
yields that
(I-F_{e}^{*}F_{e})f\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\Omega)
. Now, suppose that
D_{d}
is another boundary condition for
D
, and let
F_{d}:=D_{d}(D_{d}^{*}D_{d}+I)^{-1/2}
. It follows from Corollary
6.19
that
(F_{e}-F_{d})f\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\Omega)
. Since
D
is assumed symmetric,
D_{d}:=D_{e}^{*}
is also a boundary condition for
D
, and this particular case yields
(F-F^{*})f\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\Omega)
. Lastly, if
p\geq 0
and
D_{e}
is odd and multigraded, then
D^{*}_{e}D_{e}
is even and multigraded, hence so is
(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. It follows that
F_{e}
is odd and multigraded. This proves (i). Moreover, we have already seen that if
F_{d}
is the bounded transform of a different extension
D_{d}
, then
(F_{e}-F_{d})f\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\Omega)
. Hence
F_{e}
and
F_{d}
differ by a uniformly compact perturbation, and thus define the same class in
K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
. More generally, Corollary
6.19
yields that if
D
and
\tilde{D}
are two uniformly elliptic operators of order
m
over
\overline{\Omega}
that differ by an operator of order
\leq m-1
, then the Fredholm modules defined by any boundary conditions for
D
and
\tilde{D}
differ by a uniformly compact perturbation over
\Omega
, proving (c) in the case of absolute classes over the interior.

For (ii) it remains to show that
[f,F_{e}]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\overline{\Omega})
if
D_{e}
is local and semi-interpolation-regular. This was shown in Corollary
6.15
. Note that (a) then holds because the excision map is the identity at the level of Fredholm modules. If
\tilde{D}_{d}
is a local and semi-interpolation-regular boundary condition of another uniformly elliptic operator with the same principal symbol (or in particular another such boundary condition for
D
), Corollary
6.19
again gives that
F_{e}
and
\tilde{F}_{d}
differ by a uniformly compact perturbation. Hence the relative case of (c) follows.

For (iii) we have already noted that
[f,F_{e}]\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\overline{\Omega})
, and
(F_{e}-F_{e}^{*})f\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
holds trivially due to the assumed self-adjointness. It remains to show
(I-F_{e}^{*}F_{e})f\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(\overline{\Omega})
. Since
I-F_{e}^{*}F_{e}=(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-1}
extends to a bounded map
\mathcal{H}^{-2}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
, this is again implied by Corollary
6.8
via (semi-)interpolation-regularity. This concludes the proof of (iii). Then (b) again holds because the restriction map
K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega})\to K_{*}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
is the identity at the level of Fredholm modules.

It remains to prove (d). Note that the operator
F_{e}
considered thus far corresponds to the normalizing function
\chi_{0}(x)=x(x^{2}+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. By a density argument it suffices to consider normalizing functions
\chi
that differ from
\chi_{0}
by a function in
\mathcal{F}^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
. Then,
(\chi-\chi_{0})(D_{e})
extends to a map
\mathcal{H}^{-\beta}(D_{e})\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
by Proposition
6.3
. If
D_{e}
is arbitrary, we conclude from Corollary
6.8
that
\chi(D_{e})=F_{e}+(\chi-\chi_{0})(D_{e})
is uniformly pseudo-local over
\Omega
, and differs from
F_{e}
by a uniformly compact perturbation. If
D_{e}
is local and semi-interpolation-regular, we conclude from the same corollary that
\chi(D_{e})
is uniformly pseudo-local over
\overline{\Omega}
, and that it differs from
F_{e}
by a uniformly compact perturbation over
\overline{\Omega}
. This proves (d), completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

Items (i) and (ii) of Theorem
6.22
in conjunction with property (c) provide well-defined classes

[D]\in K^{u}_{-p}(\Omega)\quad\text{and}\quad[D]\in K^{u}_{-p}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)

defined as the uniform K-homology classes of any boundary condition with the required properties. To make sure that the latter class always exists, i.e. that any uniformly elliptic operator admits a local, semi-interpolation-regular and suitably graded boundary condition, it suffices to recall from Chapter 5 that the minimal boundary condition always has these properties.

Example 6.23

(Minimal boundary condition)
.

The minimal boundary condition for any uniformly elliptic operator is local and semi-interpolation-regular, and possesses the same grading properties as
D
, and thus defines a relative uniform Fredholm module over
(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
. Thus any uniformly elliptic operator
D
over
\overline{\Omega}
admits a class in
[D]\in K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
.

Let us consider some of the examples of Section
21
.

Example 6.24

(APS- and chirality boundary conditions)
.

The APS-boundary conditions are self-adjoint (if
\ker(\not{D}_{\partial\Omega})=0
) and regular, but not local. Thus they define a uniform Fredholm module over
\Omega
, but not one over
\overline{\Omega}
. The chirality boundary conditions are local and regular, but not self-adjoint. In even dimensions they further lack the correct grading properties, but in odd dimensions this is no problem. In this case they define an ungraded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
, but due to lack of self-adjointness we cannot conclude from Theorem
6.22
(iii) that they define a uniform Fredholm module over
\overline{\Omega}
in this case. In fact, we will see in Example
6.43
below that they cannot define an ungraded uniform Fredholm module over
\overline{\Omega}
. Thus, while we do not expect self-adjointness to be generally required to get a uniform Fredholm module over
\overline{\Omega}
, some condition on the adjoint will be necessary.

Example 6.25

(Absolute and relative boundary conditions)
.

Both the absolute and the relative boundary conditions for the Euler characteristic operator are local, self-adjoint and regular, and thus define uniform Fredholm modules over
\overline{\Omega}
. On a closed connected even-dimensional manifold
M
the index of the Euler characteristic operator
D^{\chi}
equals the Euler characteristic
\chi(M)
. In fact it holds that

[D^{\chi}]=\chi(M)\cdot i_{*}(\mathbf{1})\;\in\;K_{0}(M)\;,

where
i_{*}(\mathbf{1})
is the image of the generator of
K_{0}(\mathrm{pt})
under the inclusion of a point
[
Ros99
]
. Thus the K-homology class of the Euler-characteristic operator contains no information beyond its index. It would be interesting to know to what extent this is true in the bounded-geometry setting, and for manifolds with boundary. One would conjecture that the uniform coarse index of
D^{\chi}_{abs/rel}
computes some sort of absolute/relative
L^{2}
-Euler characteristic, but of course both of these objects require correct interpretation before we can even ask whether they are equal. Let us mention the related result of Schick that the kernel of the absolute/relative boundary conditions compute the absolute/relative
L^{2}
-cohomology via bounded Hodge-theory
[
Sch98a
]
.

In any case absolute and relative boundary condition in particular define K-homology classes over a compact manifold with boundary. Their indices are the absolute and relative Euler characteristic respectively, hence generally not equal. We conclude that even in the compact case
[D^{\chi}_{abs}]\neq[D^{\chi}_{rel}]
, so that Theorem
6.22
(c) is not generally true for the classes over
\overline{\Omega}
provided by (iii).

Example 6.26

(Dirichlet Laplacian)
.

The Dirichlet Laplacian is local, regular, and self-adjoint. Thus
\left(L^{2}(\overline{\Omega}),\rho,\Delta_{\mathrm{Dir}}(\Delta_{\mathrm{Dir}}^{2}+I)^{-1/2}\right)
is an ungraded uniform Fredholm module over
\overline{\Omega}
. Unfortunately, its class in uniform K-homology is trivial. Indeed, let
F=\Delta_{\mathrm{Dir}}(\Delta_{\mathrm{Dir}}^{2}+I)^{-1/2}
and note that
F
is positive. Then, we see that

F_{t}=\left(\sin(t)+\cos(t)F\right)\left(I+\cos(t)\sin(t)F\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

is an operator-homotopy from
F
to the degenerate Fredholm module
(L^{2}(\overline{\Omega}),\rho,I)
. More generally suppose that
D
is any positive symmetric uniformly elliptic operator, and
D_{e}\geq 0
a positive self-adjoint boundary condition. Then, the class
[D_{e}]\in K^{u}_{1}(\Omega)
vanishes. If
D_{e}
is also local and regular, then the class
[D_{e}]\in K^{u}_{1}(\overline{\Omega})
vanishes as well.

Remark 6.27
.

Following the discussion of Subsection
23.5
one can extend Conclusion (i) of Theorem
6.22
to uniformly pseudo-differential operators over
\overline{\Omega}
, and to elliptic differential operators over
\Omega
exhibiting uniformly controlled singularities on the boundary. In principle, conclusions (ii) and (iii) could also hold for these operators provided one assumes the existence of (semi-)regular boundary conditions, but as discussed in that subsection this seems unlikely.

In their work
[
BDT89
]
on K-homology classes for elliptic operators on manifolds with boundary Baum, Douglas and Taylor approach the case of relative classes slightly differently. They consider graded odd operators
D=\begin{pmatrix}0&D^{-}\\
D^{+}&0\end{pmatrix}
, and instead of looking at semi-regular boundary condition for
D
directly -which is what we did above- they consider semi-regular boundary conditions
D_{e}^{+}
for
D^{+}
, and form the boundary condition

D_{e}=\begin{pmatrix}0&(D^{+}_{e})^{*}\\
D_{e}^{+}&0\end{pmatrix}\;.

It is not semi-regular unless
D^{+}_{e}
is already regular. They then show that the boundary condition
D_{e}
defines a relative Fredholm module. We can do the same in our setting.

Proposition 6.28
.

Let
D
be a graded uniformly elliptic operator, and let
D^{\pm}
denote the graded components of
D
. Let
D_{e}^{+}
be a local and semi-interpolation-regular boundary condition for
D^{+}
, and set

D_{e}=\begin{pmatrix}0&(D^{+}_{e})^{*}\\
D_{e}^{+}&0\end{pmatrix}\;.

Then,
\left(L^{2}(\Omega;E),\rho,\chi(D_{e})\right)
is a graded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
. Its class in
K_{0}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
coincides with that defined in Theorem
6.22
. In particular it is independent of the choice of normalizing function.

We need a lemma.

Lemma 6.29
.

Let
D
and
D_{e}
be as above. Then, for any odd function
C_{0}(\mathbb{R})
with
\varphi(0)=0
it holds that
\varphi(D_{e})\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
.

Proof.

By way of a density argument it suffices to consider the case where
\varphi(x)=x(x^{2}+\lambda)^{-\beta}
for
\lambda>0
and
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
. In this case

\varphi(D_{e})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\left(D_{e}^{+}((D_{e}^{+})^{*}D^{+}_{e}+\lambda)^{-\beta}\right)^{*}\\
D_{e}^{+}((D_{e}^{+})^{*}D^{+}_{e}+\lambda)^{-\beta}\end{pmatrix}\;,

so that it suffices to prove that
D_{e}^{+}((D_{e}^{+})^{*}D^{+}_{e}+\lambda)^{-\beta}\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
. To see this it suffices to note that
(x^{2}+\lambda)^{-\beta}\in\mathcal{F}^{-2\beta}
, so that
D_{e}^{+}((D_{e}^{+})^{*}D^{+}_{e}+\lambda)^{-\beta}
extends to a bounded operator
\mathcal{H}^{-2\beta+1}(D_{e}^{+})\to\mathcal{H}^{0}((D_{e}^{+})^{*})=L^{2}(\Omega,E^{-})\subseteq L^{2}(\Omega;E)
. Then Proposition
6.7
implies that
D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+\lambda)^{-\beta}\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Prop.
6.28
.

Since
D_{e}
is an odd boundary condition
D
the triple
(L^{2}(\Omega;E),\rho,\chi(D_{e}))
is a graded uniform Fredholm module over
\Omega
by Theorem
6.22
, (i). Thus it remains to show that
\chi(D_{e})
is also in uniformly pseudo-local over
\overline{\Omega}
. First, consider
\chi_{0}(x)=x(x^{2}+1)^{-1/2}
. Then,
\chi_{0}(D_{e})=\begin{pmatrix}0&F^{*}\\
F&0\end{pmatrix}
with
F=D_{e}^{+}((D_{e}^{+})^{*}D^{+}_{e}+I)^{-1/2}
. Since
D^{+}_{e}
is local and semi-interpolation-regular,
F
and hence also
F^{*}
are in
\mathfrak{D}^{u}(\overline{\Omega})
. Thus
\chi_{0}(D_{e})\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}(\overline{\Omega})
as well. Now, an arbitrary normalizing function
\chi
differs from
\chi_{0}
by an odd function vanishing both at infinity and at
0
. Thus Lemma
6.29
implies that
\chi(D_{e})\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
for arbitrary
\chi
. This proves that
(L^{2}(\Omega;E),\rho,\chi(D_{e}))
is a graded relative uniform Fredholm module over
(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
. That it defines the same relative uniform K-homology class as that constructed in Theorem
6.22
(ii), follows from excision.
∎

Property (d) of Theorem
6.22
allows for the representation of the uniform K-homology classes of uniformly elliptic operators by means of functional calculus if the boundary condition is self-adjoint. However, self-adjoint extensions are potentially sparse, and sometimes it is technically convenient to have a representation via functional calculus nonetheless. This is what the following lemma provides.

Lemma 6.30
.

Let
D_{e}
be a semi-interpolation-regular, odd and
p
-multigraded boundary condition for the odd and
p
-multigraded uniformly elliptic operator
D
. Let
\hat{D_{e}}
be the self-adjoint operator

\hat{D_{e}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&D_{e}^{*}\\
D_{e}&0\end{pmatrix}

on the Hilbert space
\hat{H}:=L^{2}(\Omega;E)\oplus L^{2}(\Omega;E)
. Let
P:\,\hat{H}\to L^{2}(\Omega;E)
be the projection onto the first summand. Then, for any normalizing function
\chi
the triple

\left(L^{2}(\Omega;E),\rho,(I-P)\chi(\hat{D}_{e})P\right)

is a
p
-multigraded relative uniform Fredholm module that differs from

\left(L^{2}(\Omega;E),\rho,D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)

by a uniformly compact perturbation. In particular

\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),\rho,D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-1/2}\right]=\left[L^{2}(\Omega;E),\rho,(I-P)\chi(\hat{D}_{e})P\right]\;\in\;K^{u}_{-p}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\;.

Proof.

Set
H=L^{2}(\Omega;E)
so that
\hat{H}:=H\oplus H
. The space
\hat{H}
is graded by
\hat{\kappa}=\kappa\oplus\kappa
and admits a
p
-multigrading with multigrading operators
\hat{\varepsilon}_{j}:=\varepsilon_{j}\oplus\varepsilon_{j}
,
j=1,\cdots,p
.
The operator
\hat{D}_{e}
is odd and
p
-multigraded. If
\chi
is any normalizing function, then
\chi(\hat{D_{e}})
is odd and
p
-multigraded as well. In particular it takes the form
\chi(\hat{D_{e}})=\begin{pmatrix}0&F^{*}\\
F&0\end{pmatrix}
. Consider the projection
P
onto the first summand
H\subseteq\hat{H}
. It is even and
p
-multigraded as a map
\hat{H}\to H
. It follows that
F=(I-P)\chi(\hat{D}_{e})P\in\mathfrak{B}(H)
is odd and
p
-multigraded. By Proposition
6.28

(\hat{H},\rho\oplus\rho,\chi(\hat{D}_{e}))
is a relative uniform Fredholm module over
(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
, and thus the same holds for
(H,\rho,F)
. This shows that
(H,\rho,F)
is a
p
-multigraded relative uniform Fredholm module.

By Lemma
6.29
the operator
\chi(\hat{D}_{e})
is independent of the choice of
\chi
up to a uniformly compact perturbation, thus the same is true for
F
. Choosing
\chi(x)=x(x^{2}+1)^{-1/2}
leads to
F=D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-1/2}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

For applications to relative uniform index maps it will be useful to have know explicit representatives of the uniform K-homology classes of a uniformly elliptic operator that have finite propagation, or are limits of operators with this property. For first-order operators this can be achieved by means of the propagation estimates discussed in Section
22
.

Proposition 6.31
.

Let
D
be a symmetric first-order uniformly elliptic operator, and
D_{e}
a local semi-interpolation-regular boundary condition for
D
. Then,
D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is an element of
D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})
, and is an involution modulo
D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{D}_{e}
and
P
be as in Lemma
6.30
. Then
\hat{D}_{e}
is a local and self-adjoint extension of a uniformly elliptic first-order differential operator. Thus, if
\chi
is a normalizing function with compactly supported Fourier transform, the operator
\chi(\hat{D}_{e})
has finite propagation by Corollary
5.33
. In combination with Proposition
6.28
we conclude that
\chi(\hat{D}_{e})\in D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})
. The projection
P
has finite propagation, in fact zero propagation, so that
(I-P)\chi(\hat{D}_{e})P
is a uniformly pseudo-local finite-propagation operator as well. The same holds for
P\chi(\hat{D}_{e})(I-P)
. Since moreover
(I-P)\chi(\hat{D}_{e})P-P\chi(\hat{D}_{e})(I-P)\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
it follows that
(I-P)\chi(\hat{D}_{e})P
is self-adjoint modulo
D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
. Now, normalizing functions with compactly supported Fourier transform are dense in the space of all normalizing functions. Thus
D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-1/2}=(I-P)\chi_{0}(\hat{D}_{e})P
(with
\chi_{0}(x)=x(x^{2}+1)^{-1/2}
) is the limit of uniformly pseudolocal finite-propagation operators that are self-adjoint modulo
D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
. Hence it is also in
D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})
, and self-adjoint modulo
D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
. Lastly, an analogous approximation argument shows that the operator

\left(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I\right)^{-1}=I-\left(D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{*}\left(D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)

is in
D_{u}^{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
, proving that
D_{e}(D_{e}^{*}D_{e}+I)^{-1/2}
is an involution modulo
D_{u}^{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
.
∎

We remark that in case one only cares about first-order differential operators, one can use propagation arguments to show that such operators give rise to classes in K-homology. In the boundary-less case that approach is taken in
[
HR00
]
. Baum, Douglas and Taylor also use these techniques in
[
BDT89
]
to treat classes in the interior, and obtain independence of the choice of boundary condition. It seems likely that these arguments can be made uniform to prove the analogous statements for uniform K-homology. In fact Engel used wave operators to treat uniformly elliptic pseudo-differential operators on manifolds of bounded geometry
[
Eng18a
]
. As indicated above these wave operators no longer have finite propagation (even if one restricts to differential operators), but they are quasi-local, meaning there is a uniform bound on the amount of mass they propagate over a certain distance.

Let us conclude this section with a brief discussion of Dirac operators. We recall some basics here, but refer the reader to Appendix
9
for a more detailed account. So far we have encountered the spinor Dirac operator
\not{D}
over (domains in) a spin manifold. Suppose that
\Omega
is a domain in an
n
-dimensional spin manifold of bounded geometry. If
\Omega
is even-dimensional, then
\not{D}
is graded and odd, so that it defines a class

[\not{D}]\in K_{0}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\;.

If
\dim(\Omega)=n
is instead odd,
\not{D}
is ungraded, hence defines a class

[\not{D}]\in K_{1}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\;.

There is also the Clifford-linear Dirac operator
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
acting on sections of the Clifford-module bundle
\not{\mathfrak{S}}(\overline{\Omega})
. It is
n
-multigraded and odd, hence defines a class

[\not{\mathfrak{D}}]\in K_{-n}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\;.

The operators
\not{D}
and
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
are connected via two-fold periodicity. Indeed, applying two-fold periodicity
\lceil\frac{n}{2}\rceil
times to the
n
-multigraded Clifford-module bundle
\not{\mathfrak{S}}(\overline{\Omega})
produces a graded bundle if
n
is even, and an ungraded one if
n
is odd, and in either case this bundle equals the spinor bundle
\not{S}(\overline{\Omega})
. Under this repeated application of two-fold periodicity
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
and
\not{D}
correspond to each other. Thus we conclude the following.

Proposition 6.32

(Compare
[
HR00
, Section 11.3]
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a domain in an
n
-dimensional spin manifold of bounded geometry. Then,

[\not{\mathfrak{D}}]\in K_{-n}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\quad\text{and}\quad[\not{D}]\in K_{n\,\mathrm{mod}\,2}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)

are mapped to each other by the two-fold periodicity isomorphisms.

Thus it is essentially a matter of convenience whether we work with
\not{D}
or
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
(in the complex case, see Remark
6.33
below). For the most part we will work with the latter, as it behaves favorably with respect to products. To that end we first recall multigraded tensor products of vector bundles and differential operators. Again, a more detailed account can be found in Appendix
9
. Suppose that
M
and
M^{\prime}
are manifolds (with or without boundary), and
E\to M
and
E^{\prime}\to M
are
p
- and
p^{\prime}
-multigraded vector bundles. Suppose further that
D
and
D^{\prime}
are
p
- and
p^{\prime}
-multigraded differential operators acting on sections of
E
and
E^{\prime}
respectively. Then, one may form the exterior multigraded tensor product

S\hat{\boxtimes}S^{\prime}\longrightarrow M\times M^{\prime}\;,

which is a
(p+p^{\prime})
-multigraded vector bundle. Using the multigraded tensor product one then forms the product operator

D\times D^{\prime}:=D\hat{\otimes}I+I\hat{\otimes}D^{\prime}\;,

which is a
(p+p^{\prime})
-multigraded differential operator acting on sections of
S\hat{\boxtimes}S^{\prime}
. Now, if
M
and
M^{\prime}
are spin manifold, then
M\times M^{\prime}
carries a canonical spin structure, and it holds that

\not{\mathfrak{S}}(M\times M^{\prime})=\not{\mathfrak{S}}(M)\hat{\boxtimes}\not{\mathfrak{S}}(M^{\prime})\quad,\quad\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{M\times M^{\prime}}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{M}\times\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{M^{\prime}}\;.

In particular, if
\overline{\Omega}
is a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry that has product structure near the boundary, then on a uniformly thick tubular neighborhood
U\cong[0,\delta)\times\partial\Omega
it holds that

\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Omega}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\partial\Omega}\;.

We will prove below that on uniform K-homology the multigraded tensor product of operators is implemented by the Kasparov product. This will be a crucial component of our proof of boundary-of-Dirac-is-Dirac. Because the Clifford-linear Dirac operators behave conveniently in regards to products, we will use them for our considerations below. Indeed, we will use Clifford-linear Dirac operators as a model case to phrase the condition that an operator has product structure near the boundary or more generally near a hypersurface. This multigraded framework is more convenient to work with, but one can always use two-fold periodicity to reduce to graded and ungraded operators only.

Remark 6.33

(Real uniform K-homology and periodicity)
.

The difference between
\not{D}
and
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
is only not felt because we are working with complex instead of Real K-homology. There is a Real
76
76

76

We use the convention of referring to objects like Hilbert space,
C^{*}
-algebras, vector bundles, etc., as
Real
if they are defined over the field of complex numbers, and additionally equipped with an anti-linear involution. An operator on a Real space is
Real
if it is complex-linear and commutes with the anti-linear involution. In contrast a
real
Hilbert space,
C^{*}
-algebra, vector bundle, etc., is simply one defined over the field of real numbers.
version of K-homology, whose cycles are given by Real multigraded Hilbert spaces together with a Real representation and a Real operator satisfying the usual conditions. Thus we can also define
uniform Real K-homology
in the obvious way. The contents of this chapter up to this point then hold verbatim for uniform Real K-homology if we additionally require the uniformly elliptic operators to be Real. In particular both the spinor Dirac operator and the Clifford-linear Dirac operator define classes in (relative) uniform Real K-homology. However instead of a two-fold periodicity Real K-homology satisfies an eight-fold periodicity. Thus, the uniform Real K-homology class of
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
contains information that can generally not be recovered from
\not{D}
.

Let us remark that transferring the results of Chapter 2 and 3 to uniform Real K-homology is not quite as straight-forward as adding the word „Real“  in the appropriate places. This is because Paschke duality, which laid the foundation of our discussion in Chapter 2 and hence Chapter 3 as well, uses two-fold periodicity in an essential way. One first would have to prove a Real version of Paschke duality, see for example
[
Roe04
]
for the corresponding discussion for analytic K-homology. If this was done, then the author anticipates the Real counterparts of our results to follow rather straight-forwardly.

25
Properties of the uniform K-homology classes

This section is devoted to establishing the compatibility of the uniform K-homology classes of uniformly elliptic operators over domains with some of the basic uniform K-homological operations, namely restriction to subsets, the Kasparov product, and the boundary map. These compatibility statements provide crucial computational tools that will allow us to deduce index theorems in the following section.

25.1
Restrictions

Let
\Omega
and
\Omega^{\prime}
be domains in the manifold
M
of bounded geometry. As usual we endow them with the subspace metric coming from
M
. If
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
, then the inclusion map
C_{0}(\Omega^{\prime})\hookrightarrow C_{0}(\Omega)
is filtered, thus it induces a restriction map
i^{!}:\,K^{u}_{*}(\Omega)\to K^{u}_{*}(\Omega^{\prime})
. In case domain and target of the restriction map need to be made explicit we also write
i^{!}_{\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}}
for this map.

Proposition 6.34
.

Let
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
be domains in the bounded-geometry manifold
M
. Let
D
be a symmetric uniformly elliptic differential operator over
\overline{\Omega}
. Then

i^{!}[D_{\Omega}]=[D_{\Omega^{\prime}}]\;\in K_{*}^{u}(\Omega^{\prime})\;.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the claim for the restriction from
M
to a domain
\Omega
. Indeed, assume the claim holds for the restriction from
M
to any domain. Then,

i^{!}_{\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}}[D_{\Omega}]=i^{!}_{\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}}i^{!}_{M\to\Omega}[D_{M}]=i^{!}_{M\to\Omega^{\prime}}[D_{M}]=[D_{\Omega^{\prime}}]\;.

Thus, let
D
be symmetric uniformly elliptic over
M
, and let
\Omega
be a domain. We consider the minimal boundary condition
D_{0}:=D_{\min}
for
D
as an operator over
\Omega
. Then,
[D_{\Omega}]
is represented by
(L^{2}(\Omega;E),\rho_{\Omega},D_{0}(D_{0}^{*}D_{0}+I)^{-1/2})
, where
\rho_{\Omega}
is the representation by multiplication operators. On the other hand
i^{!}[D_{M}]
is represented by
(L^{2}(M;E),\rho_{M}|_{\Omega},D(D^{*}D+I)^{-1/2})
. This Fredholm module is a uniformly compact perturbation of the sum of
