(L^{2}(\Omega;E),\rho_{\Omega},qD(D^{*}D+I)^{-1/2}q)
and a degenerate, where
q
is the projection given by the indicator function of
\Omega
. We will show that
D_{0}(D_{0}^{*}D_{0}+I)^{-1/2}
and
qD(D^{*}D+I)^{-1/2}q
differ by a uniformly compact operator.

To that end let
D^{\prime}
denote the restriction of
D
to
M\setminus\overline{\Omega}
, and let
D^{\prime}_{0}
denote its minimal boundary condition. Consider the closed operator
T=D_{0}\oplus D^{\prime}_{0}
on
L^{2}(M;E)=L^{2}(\Omega;E)\oplus L^{2}(M\setminus\overline{\Omega};E)
.
77
77

77

For this decomposition to be valid we have to assume that
\partial\Omega
has measure zero. If this is not the case, fix
\delta>0
. By Lemma
4.20
it suffices to consider functions in
C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
that are supported distance
\geq\delta
from
\partial\Omega
. Then
\partial\Omega
can be modified within a
\delta/2
-neighborhood to have measure zero, and we can proceed with the same proof.
Observe that
(D-T)f=0=(D^{*}D-T^{*}T)f
for
f\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\Omega)
. Using the arguments of Section
23.4
78
78

78

While the results of that Section were formulated for the difference of boundary conditions for two uniformly elliptic operators, everything beyond Lemma
6.10
is an abstract argument and hence also valid for an operator like
T
that still satisfies the relevant analytical properties, but is strictly speaking not a boundary condition.
one may then show that

\left(D\left(D^{*}D+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-T\left(T^{*}T+I\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)f\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0

for
f\in C_{0}(\Omega)
. Hence the same holds true when multiplying both operators with
q
on both sides. Since
qT(T^{*}T+I)^{-1/2}q=D_{0}(D_{0}^{*}D_{0}+I)^{-1/2}
, this concludes the proof.
∎

25.2
Products

We come to the relation between the multigraded tensor product of operators and the Kasparov product. Recall that if
D
and
D^{\prime}
are multigraded operators acting on sections of the multigraded bundles
E
and
E^{\prime}
, then
D\times D^{\prime}
denotes the operator
D\hat{\otimes}I+I\hat{\otimes}D^{\prime}
acting on sections of the multigraded exterior tensor product
E\hat{\boxtimes}E^{\prime}\to M\times M^{\prime}
. Recall also from Lemma
6.20
above that if
M
and
M^{\prime}
are Riemannian manifolds, then the product metric
d_{M}+d_{M^{\prime}}
and the path length metric
d_{M\times M^{\prime}}
induced by the product Riemannian metric
g_{M\times M^{\prime}}=g_{M}\oplus g_{M^{\prime}}
are equivalent. Hence either may be used for the uniform K-homology of the product
M\times M^{\prime}
.

Proposition 6.35
.

Let
M
,
M^{\prime}
be manifolds of bounded geometry, and
D
,
D^{\prime}
symmetric uniformly elliptic operators over
M
and
M^{\prime}
respectively. Then,

[D]\times[D^{\prime}]=[D\times D^{\prime}]\;\in K_{*}^{u}(M\times M^{\prime})\;.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that in analytic K-homology. Indeed, the discussion in Section 10.7 in
[
HR00
]
covers our case as well.
79
79

79

Technically, Higson and Roe treat the case where
D
and
D^{\prime}
are first-order differential operators, but it is easily verified that their arguments hold verbatim for higher-order operators as well. Indeed, their discussion is mostly operator-theoretic in nature, apart from using at one point that elements in the domain of the unique closure of
D
,
D^{\prime}
can be approximated by smooth sections. This is true in our setting as well.
We conclude that
\chi(D\times D^{\prime})
is aligned to
\chi(D)
and
\chi(D^{\prime})
in the sense of
[
HR00
, Definition 9.2]
for any normalizing function
\chi
. Apart from finite-propagation requirements that definition is the same as Definition
2.14
, and these requirements are taken care of by Remark
2.17
. Thus
\chi(D\times D^{\prime})
represents the Kasparov product of
\chi(D)
and
\chi(D^{\prime})
on uniform K-homology as well.
∎

We deduce the corresponding product formula on domains by use of restriction maps. Note that in order to consider the Kasparov product on domains in the first place we need to assume them to have jointly bounded geometry. This is satisfied in particular for bounded-geometry domains. Also, we again endow the product of two domains with the product metric of the respective subspace metrics. This coincides with the subspace metric induced by the product metric on the product of the ambient manifolds.

Corollary 6.36
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq M
,
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq M^{\prime}
be domains in manifolds of bounded geometry. Assume that
\Omega
,
\Omega^{\prime}
have jointly bounded geometry. Let
D
,
D^{\prime}
be symmetric uniformly elliptic operators over
\overline{\Omega}
,
\overline{\Omega}^{\prime}
respectively. Then,

[D]\times[D^{\prime}]=[D\times D^{\prime}]\;\in K_{*}^{u}(\Omega\times\Omega^{\prime})

Proof.

By assumption there are symmetric uniformly elliptic pseudo-differential operators
\tilde{D}
,
\tilde{D}^{\prime}
over
M
,
M^{\prime}
that restrict to
D
and
D^{\prime}
respectively. The Kasparov product is natural with respect to the restriction maps by Theorem
2.16
. Combining this with Propositions
6.34
and
6.35
we compute that

[D]\times[D^{\prime}]=(i^{!}\times(i^{\prime})^{!})\left([\tilde{D}]\times[\tilde{D}^{\prime}]\right)=(i^{!}\times(i^{\prime})^{!})[\tilde{D}\times\tilde{D}^{\prime}]=[D\times D^{\prime}]\;.

∎

In the special of Dirac operators we deduce the following from their combatibility with product.

Corollary 6.37
.

Let
\Omega
and
\Omega^{\prime}
be domains of jointly bounded geometry in spin manifolds of bounded geometry and dimension
n
and
n^{\prime}
respectively. Then,

[\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Omega\times\Omega^{\prime}}]=[\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Omega}]\times[\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Omega^{\prime}}]\;\in\;K_{-(n+n^{\prime})}^{u}(\Omega\times\Omega^{\prime})\;.

Thus, under two-fold periodicity it also holds that

[\not{D}_{\Omega\times\Omega^{\prime}}]=[\not{D}_{\Omega}]\times[\not{D}_{\Omega^{\prime}}]\;\in\;K_{(n+n^{\prime})\,\mathrm{mod}\,2}^{u}(\Omega\times\Omega^{\prime})

25.3
Boundary maps

The goal of this section is to determine the image of the relative class defined by a uniformly elliptic operator under the boundary map of the long exact sequence in uniform K-homology. We will restrict ourselves to first-order differential operators, though see Remark
6.44
below.

In our discussion we will require the operator to have product structure near the boundary. This is defined as follows.

Definition 6.38
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry,
E\to\overline{\Omega}
a vector bundle of bounded geometry, and
D:\,\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)\to\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega;E)
a differential operator. Let
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
, and suppose that
E
and
D
are
(p+1)
-multigraded, and that
D
is odd. We say that
D
has
product structure
near
\partial\Omega
if there exists a uniformly thick collar neighborhood of
\partial\Omega
, and a multigraded unitary isomorphism between
E
and
\not{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathbb{R})\hat{\boxtimes}E_{\partial\Omega}
for some
p
-multigraded vector bundle
E_{\partial\Omega}\to\partial\Omega
over that collar neighborhood, so that under this isomorphism
D
is given by

D=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{\partial\Omega}

for some odd
p
-multigraded differential operator
D_{\partial\Omega}:\,\Gamma_{c}(\partial\Omega;E_{\partial\Omega})\to\Gamma_{c}(\partial\Omega;E_{\partial\Omega})
.

We can now formulate the boundary-of-Dirac-is-Dirac formula in uniform K-homology. Its proof is rather straight-forward application of Theorem
2.54
, together with the compatibility with restrictions and products we have derived in the previous subsections. We consider
\partial\Omega
to carry the subspace metric coming from the inclusion
\partial\Omega\subseteq M
, though see Lemma
6.20
.

Theorem 6.39
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry. Let
D
be a uniformly elliptic
(p+1)
-multigraded first order differential operator over
\overline{\Omega}
, and assume that
D
has product structure
D=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{\partial\Omega}
near
\partial\Omega
. Then

\partial[D]=(-1)^{p+1}[D_{\partial\Omega}]\;\in\;K^{u}_{-p}(\partial\Omega)\;.

Proof.

By naturality of the boundary map we may restrict to a uniformly thick collar neighborhood. Indeed,
\partial\Omega
admits a uniformly thick collar neighborhood
U
that bi-Lipschitz equivalent to
[0,\delta)\times\partial\Omega
with the product metric (see
[
Sch98a
, Proposition 7.3]
). Then, the boundary map factors as

\textstyle{K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{i^{!}}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-p}(\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(U,\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-p}(\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-(p+1)}([0,\delta)\times\partial\Omega,\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-p}(\partial\Omega)}

By Proposition
6.34
the restriction map
i^{!}
maps
[D]
to
[D_{U}]\in K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(U,\partial\Omega)
. Since we assume
D
to have product structure near the boundary
[D_{U}]
is identified with

[\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{\partial\Omega}]=[\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}]\times[D_{\partial\Omega}]\;\in\;K^{u}_{-(p+1)}([0,\delta)\times\partial\Omega,\partial\Omega)\;,

where we used Corollary
6.36
. Now,
[\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}]
coincides with the generator
d\in K^{u}_{-1}([0,\delta),\{0\})
. Thus

\partial\left([\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}]\times[D_{\partial\Omega}]\right)=(-1)^{-(p+1)}[D_{\partial\Omega}]\;\in\;K^{u}_{-p}(\partial\Omega)

by Theorem
2.54
. It follows that
\partial[D]=(-1)^{p+1}[D_{\partial\Omega}]
as well, proving the claim.
∎

Remark 6.40

(Regarding the sign)
.

The sign in the formula
\partial[D]=(-1)^{p+1}[D_{\partial\Omega}]
is an artifact of our chosen construction of the boundary map. As opposed to using Paschke duality and the K-theoretical boundary map (which is what we did) it is also possible to use the Kasparov product and a mapping cone construction to construct the long exact sequence in K-homology, see
[
HR00
, Section 9.5]
. The resulting boundary maps differ by a sign of
(-1)^{p+1}
. For the latter the formula
\partial[D]=[D_{\partial\Omega}]
thus holds without the sign, which is not surprising given that its construction is organized around the Kasparov product. We chose to stay consistent in the use of the boundary map constructed in Chapter 2, hence the sign.

We obtain the following corollary for Dirac operators.

Corollary 6.41
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional spin manifold with boundary and bounded geometry that has product structure near the boundary. Then,

\partial[\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Omega}]=(-1)^{n+1}[\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\partial\Omega}]\;\in\;K_{-n}^{u}(\partial\Omega)\;.

Thus, under two-fold periodicity it also holds that

\partial[\not{D}_{\Omega}]=(-1)^{n+1}[\not{D}_{\partial\Omega}]\;\in\;K_{n\,\mathrm{mod}\,2}^{u}(\partial\Omega)\;.

By exactness of the long exact sequence we know that
[D]\in K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
can only have non-zero image under the boundary map if
[D]
does not lift to a class in
K_{*}^{u}(\overline{\Omega})
. If
D
has product structure, the class
[D_{\partial\Omega}]=\partial[D]
therefore obstructs the existence of boundary conditions for
D
that would allow for the construction of such a lift.

Corollary 6.42
.

Let
D
be a uniformly elliptic first-order differential operator with product structure
D=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{\partial\Omega}
near the boundary. If
D
admits a boundary condition that defines a uniform Fredholm module over
\overline{\Omega}
, then

[D_{\partial\Omega}]=0\;\in\;K_{*}(\partial\Omega)\;.

In particular, if
[D_{\partial\Omega}]\neq 0
, then
D
admits no local, regular and self-adjoint boundary condition.

Example 6.43

(Chirality boundary conditions revisited)
.

In Example
6.24
above we noted that even though they are local and regular the chirality boundary conditions for the spin Dirac operator on an odd-dimensional manifold are not covered by Theorem
6.22
due to lack of self-adjoitness. In principle they could of course define a uniform Fredholm module nonetheless. It is a consequence of Corollary
6.42
that they cannot. This implies that some condition relating a local regular boundary condition to its adjoint is required to get a uniform Fredholm module over
\overline{\Omega}
. Recalling how we proved that
D_{e}(D^{*}_{e}D_{e}+I)^{-1/2}
is self-adjoint modulo
\mathfrak{C}^{u}(\Omega)
for any boundary condition
D_{e}
this makes intuitive sense. Indeed, this uniformly essential self-adjointness was a consequence of the fact that different boundary conditions (in particular
D_{e}
and
D_{e}^{*}
) agree on the interior. This no longer holds over the entire manifold, as is witnessed by the fact that different boundary conditions can define different K-homology classes. In view of Section
23.4
it seems reasonable to conjecture that a regular differential boundary condition defines a uniform Fredholm module over
\overline{\Omega}
if the principal symbol of the differential operator defining the boundary condition is self-adjoint.

Remark 6.44

(Higher-order operators)
.

We discussed boundary maps only first-order elliptic operators with product structure near the boundary, meaning essentially that
D
is the sum of a tangential and a normal differential operator. If
D
is a higher-order differential operator, then splitting
D
into a tangential and a normal is not immediately possible. However, at least on Euclidean space an
m
th-order differential equation can be transformed into a system of
m
first-order equations (see for example Section VII.12 in
[
Tay11
]
), and it makes sense to require this system of first-order equations to have product form. Generally, these first-order equations will be pseudo-differential instead of differential. If
D
is a suitable
m
th-order operator on
\Omega
, we can locally transform
D
into a system of
m
first-order equations in product form encoded by some operator
\tilde{D}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times P
, where
P
is a first-order pseudo-differential operator, and we ignore details about (multi)gradings. It seems plausible that under the usual uniformness conditions, these local transformations can be patched together to globally represent
D
by a system
\tilde{D}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times P
of
m
first-order pseudo-differential equations in product form. Then, one could perform the same analysis as in the first-order case. Thus, it seems plausible that for a higher-order differential operator
D
the boundary
\partial[D]
is given by the class
[P]
. We point to
[
Fri25
]
for a recent treatment of the boundary of the relative class of higher-order operators in the compact case, which uses the Calderón projection instead.

Remark 6.45

(Less regular boundaries)
.

Even if
\partial\Omega
is not smooth, there is still a boundary map
K^{u}_{*+1}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\to K^{u}_{*}(\partial\Omega)
, and we can ask where uniformly elliptic operator over
\overline{\Omega}
get mapped to. Let us suppose that
\overline{\Omega}
is at least a uniform Lipschitz domain, so that
\partial\Omega
is smooth almost everywhere. Then, derivatives and normal vector fields can still be made sense of a.e. on
\partial\Omega
, and Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\partial\Sigma)
can be defined at least for
|s|\leq 1
. Then we may still speak of a first-order operator having product form near the boundary, where tangential operator and normal derivative are now a.e. defined. See for example
[
McL00
]
for a discussion in the case of domains in Euclidean space. The author expects that upon suitable interpretation Theorem
6.39
and the corollary results still hold in the Lipschitz setting.

26
Bordism invariance and partitioned manifolds

In the last section of this chapter we combine the results on uniform K-homology classes of uniformly elliptic operators over manifolds with boundary with our previous results on (relative) uniform index maps. First we deduce the bordism invariance of the uniform coarse index, then we prove a non-relative and a relative uniform partitioned-manifold index theorem.

26.1
Bordism invariance of the uniform coarse index

We begin with bordism invariance. As in the compact case it is straight-forwardly deduced from boundary-of-Dirac-is-Dirac. We will say that a manifold
\overline{\Omega}
with boundary and bounded geometry has
finite width
if the function
d(x,\partial\Omega)
remains uniformly bounded for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
. If
\overline{\Omega}
has finite width, the inclusion
\partial\Omega\hookrightarrow\overline{\Omega}
is a uniform coarse equivalence, which is necessary for the proof of bordism invariance below. Furthermore it is also necessary to have an interesting bordism theory, because otherwise any manifold
\Sigma
would be null-bordant via the infinite-width null-bordism
\overline{\Omega}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma
. Note also that the following proposition generally fails in this case.

Proposition 6.46
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a finite-width manifold with boundary and bounded geometry. Let
D
be a
(p+1)
-multigraded uniformly elliptic operator over
\overline{\Omega}
that has product structure
D=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{\partial\Omega}
near the boundary. Then,

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(D_{\partial\Omega})=0\;\in\;K_{-p}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)\;.

Proof.

Due to the assumption of finite width the inclusion
\iota:\,\partial\Omega\hookrightarrow\overline{\Omega}
is a uniform coarse equivalence, hence induces an isomorphism
C^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\cong C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})
. By naturality of the index map (or Theorem
3.19
) we have a diagram

\textstyle{K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-p}(\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ind}_{u}}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-p}(\overline{\Omega})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ind}_{u}}
\textstyle{K_{-p}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{K_{-p}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)}

Using Theorem
6.39
and exactness of the top row we compute that

\iota_{*}\left(\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(D_{\partial\Omega})\right)=\mathrm{Ind}_{u}\left(\iota_{*}[D_{\partial\Omega}]\right)=(-1)^{p+1}\mathrm{Ind}_{u}\left((\iota_{*}\circ\partial)[D]\right)=0\;.

Since
\iota_{*}:\,K_{-p}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)\to K_{-p}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)
is an isomorphism, it follows that
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(D_{\partial\Omega})=0
as well.
∎

Suppose that
\overline{\Omega}
is a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry so that
\partial\Omega
is the disjoint union
\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime}
. If the functions
x\mapsto d(x,\Sigma)
and
x\mapsto d(x,\Sigma^{\prime})
are both uniformly bounded
80
80

80

Note that this requirement is stronger than requiring that
\overline{\Omega}
have finite width.
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
, then
\Sigma\hookrightarrow\overline{\Omega}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\overline{\Omega}
are both uniform coarse equivalences. Thus there are canonical identifications
K_{*}(C^{*}_{u}(\Sigma))\cong K_{*}(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega}))\cong K_{*}(C^{*}_{u}(\Sigma^{\prime}))
, by means of which we may compare indices over
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
. The previous proposition then has the the following corollary for Dirac operators.

Corollary 6.47
.

Let
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
be
n
-dimensional spin manifolds of bounded geometry. Suppose there exists a spin manifold
\overline{\Omega}
with boundary and bounded geometry that has product structure near the boundary, so that
\partial\Omega=\Sigma\sqcup(-\Sigma^{\prime})
, where
-\Sigma^{\prime}
indicates
\Sigma^{\prime}
with reversed orientation. Suppose that the functions
x\mapsto d(x,\Sigma)
and
x\mapsto d(x,\Sigma^{\prime})
are both uniformly bounded for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
. Then

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma})=\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma^{\prime}})

upon identifying
K_{-n}(C^{*}_{u}(\Sigma))\cong K_{-n}(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega}))\cong K_{-n}(C^{*}_{u}(\Sigma^{\prime}))
. Thus, under two-fold periodicity it also holds that

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma})=\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\;.

Proof.

The Clifford-linear Dirac operator on
\overline{\Omega}
has product structure
\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Omega}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\partial\Omega}
near the boundary. Hence
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\partial\Omega})=0
in
K_{-n}(C^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega))
. The uniform coarse equivalences
\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\partial\Omega
induce canonical isomorphisms on the corresponding uniform Roe algebras. Under this identification it holds that
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma})-\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma^{\prime}})=\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\partial\Omega})=0
. The claim follows.
∎

The uniform coarse index
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma})
on a spin manifold
\Sigma
of bounded geometry is an obstruction to the existence of a upsc-metric of bounded geometry in the uniform coarse equivalence class of
\Sigma

[
Eng14
, Proposition 7.10]
. Via bordism invariance of the uniform coarse index we conclude that
\Sigma
can already not admit such a metric if it is bordant to a spin manifold with non-vanishing uniform coarse index.

Corollary 6.48
.

Let
\Sigma
be a spin manifold of bounded geometry. Suppose
\Sigma
is spin-bordant via a spin bordism of bounded geometry as in the previous corollary to a spin manifold
\Sigma^{\prime}
of bounded geometry such that

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\neq 0\;\in\;K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\Sigma^{\prime})\right)\;.

Then
\Sigma
does not admit a upsc-metric of bounded geometry in its uniform coarse equivalence class.

Before we turn to partitioned-manifold indices let us note one more consequence of the boundary-of-Dirac-is-Dirac formula pertaining to relative uniform indices. It follows directly from the combination of Theorems
3.37
and
6.39
.

Proposition 6.49
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a manifold with boundary and bounded geometry. Let
D
be a
(p+1)
-multigraded uniformly elliptic operator that has product structure
D=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{\partial\Omega}
near the boundary. Then, the algebraic Mayer-Vietoris boundary map

\delta:\;K_{-(p+1)}\left(D^{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\longrightarrow K_{-p}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)

satisfies

\delta\big(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(D_{\Omega})\big)=(-1)^{p+1}\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(D_{\partial\Omega})\;\in\;K_{-p}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)\;.

We note that this also implies bordism invariance of the uniform coarse index. Indeed, if
\overline{\Omega}
has finite width, then the algebraic Mayer-Vietoris map is zero, as we have noted in Remark
3.31
. Hence
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(D_{\partial\Omega})=(-1)^{p+1}\delta(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(D))=0
as well.

26.2
Uniform partitioned-manifold index theorems

To conclude this chapter we prove partitioned-manifold index theorems that relate the uniform coarse index on a manifold to that on a certain hypersurface. We begin by introducing the necessary terminology.

Definition 6.50
.

Let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry. A
bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface
in
M
is a bounded-geometry hypersurface
N\subseteq M
in the sense of Definition
4.7
such that
M\setminus N
has two-connected components. Note that each of these connected components is a bounded-geometry domain in
M
.

Let
D_{M}
be a differential operator over
M
acting on the sections of a bounded-geometry vector bundle
E
. Let
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
, and suppose that
E
and
D
are
(p+1)
-multigraded, and that
D
is odd. We say that
D
has
product structure
near the bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface
N\subseteq M
if there exists a uniformly thick tubular neighborhood of
N
, and a multigraded unitary isomorphism between
E
and
\not{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathbb{R})\hat{\boxtimes}E_{N}
for some
p
-multigraded vector bundle
E_{N}\to N
over that tubular neighborhood, so that under this isomorphism
D_{M}
is given by

D_{M}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{N}

for some odd
p
-multigraded differential operator
D_{N}
acing on sections of
E_{N}
.

The canonical example is again given by the Clifford-linear Dirac operator.

Example 6.51
.

If
M
is a spin manifold of bounded geometry and
N\subseteq M
a bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface such that
M
has product structure near
N
, then the Clifford-linear Dirac operator
\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{M}
has product structure
\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{M}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{N}
near
N

Given such a partitioning hypersurface
N\subseteq M
and a uniformly elliptic operator
D_{M}
over
M
that has product structure
D_{M}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{N}
near
N
, we wish to relate the uniform coarse index of
D_{M}
to that of
D_{N}
. Associated to the partition of
M
into the two sides of
N
there are Mayer-Vietoris boundary maps both in uniform K-homology and in K-theory of the uniform Roe algebras, and we know from Corollary
3.36
that the uniform coarse index map intertwines the two, i.e. there is a diagram

\textstyle{K^{u}_{*+1}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ind}_{u}}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{*}(N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ind}_{u}}
\textstyle{K_{*+1}\left(C^{*}_{u}(M)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(N)\right)}

Define the
uniform partitioned manifold index map
as the composition

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\mathrm{pm}}:=\delta\circ\mathrm{Ind}_{u}:\;K_{*+1}^{u}(M)\longrightarrow K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(N)\right)\;.

To compute it we may equivalently compute the index of the element
\delta[D_{M}]\in K^{u}_{*}(N)
. It is a consequence of the boundary-of-Dirac-is-Dirac formula that this element is given by
[D_{N}]
(up to a sign).

Proposition 6.52
.

Let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry, and
N
a bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface. Let
D
be a
(p+1)
-multigraded differential operator, and assume that
D
has product structure
D=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{N}
near
N
. Then the Mayer-Vietoris boundary map
\delta:\,K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(M)\to K^{u}_{-p}(N)
associated to the decomposition of
M
into the two sides of
N
satisfies

\delta[D]=(-1)^{p+1}[D_{N}]\;\in\;K^{u}_{-p}(N)\;.

Proof.

The Mayer-Vietoris boundary map on uniform K-homology equals the composition

K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(M)\xlongrightarrow{i^{!}_{*}}K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(M_{+})\cong K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\overline{M}_{+},N)\xlongrightarrow{\partial}K^{u}_{-p}(N))\;,

(32)

where
M_{+}
is the connected component of
M\setminus N
with the property that if
\nu
is a normal vector field on
N
pointing into
M_{+}
, and
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n})
is an oriented frame on
N
, then
(\nu,e_{1},\cdots,e_{n})
is an oriented frame on
M
. Then claim then follows from Proposition
6.34
and Theorem
6.39
.
∎

This computation together with the commutativity of the above diagram lets us conclude that the uniform partitioned-manifold index of
D_{M}
agrees with the uniform coarse index of
D_{N}
up to a sign.

Theorem 6.53

(Uniform partitioned-manifold index theorem)
.

Let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry, and
N\subseteq M
a bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface. Let
D_{M}
be a
(p+1)
-multigraded elliptic operator over
M
, and assume that
D_{M}
has product structure
D_{M}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{N}
near
N
. Then,

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\mathrm{pm}}(D_{M})=(-1)^{p+1}\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(D_{N})\quad\in\;K_{-p}\left(C^{*}_{u}(N)\right)\;.

Remark 6.54
.

Let us give a more explicit description of the partitioned manifold index in the case of the spinor Dirac operator on an odd-dimensional spin manifold
M
. Then
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D}_{M})\in K_{1}(C^{*}_{u}(M))
can be represented by the Cailey transform
U=(\not{D}_{M}-i)(\not{D}_{M}+i)^{-1}
.
81
81

81

Recall that the uniform coarse index map is the boundary map
K^{u}_{1}(M)\cong K_{0}(D^{*}_{u}(M)/C^{*}_{u}(M))\to K_{1}(C^{*}_{u}(M))
. The class of
\not{D}_{M}
in
K_{0}(D^{*}_{u}(M)/C^{*}_{u}(M))
is represented by the projection
(I+\chi(\not{D}_{M}))/2
in
D^{*}_{u}(M)/C^{*}_{u}(M)
. Its boundary is given by the class of the unitary
\exp(\pi i(I+\chi(\not{D}_{M}))
. For the normalizing function
\chi(\lambda)=\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan(\lambda)
this is precisely equal to the Cailey transform.
Let
\psi_{\pm}
be the characteristic function of
M_{\pm}
. Then
\psi_{-}+\psi_{+}U
defines an element in
K_{1}(C^{*}_{u}(M_{+}\subseteq M)/C^{*}_{u}(N\subseteq M))
, and its image under the boundary map to
K_{0}(C^{*}_{u}(N\subseteq M))\cong K_{0}(C^{*}_{u}(N))
represents that of
[U]
under the Mayer-Vietoris boundary map. Thus we arrive at the formula

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\mathrm{pm}}(\not{D}_{M})=\partial\left[\psi_{-}+\psi_{+}U\right]\;\in\;K_{0}\left(C^{*}_{u}(N)\right)\;.

This formula is structurally identical with definitions of the (non-uniform) partitioned-manifold index that do not appeal explicitly to the Mayer-Vietoris boundary map, see for example
[
Hig91
]
in the case of compact hypersurfaces, and the recent pre-print
[
HK25
]
for noncompact ones.

Restricting to Dirac operators we obtain the following.

Corollary 6.55
.

Let
M
be a spin manifold of bounded geometry, and
N\subseteq M
a bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface such that
M
has product structure near
N
. Then,

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\mathrm{pm}}(\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{M})=(-1)^{n+1}\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{N})\quad\in\;K_{-n}\left(C^{*}_{u}(N)\right)\;.

Thus, under two-fold periodicity it also holds that

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\mathrm{pm}}(\not{D}_{M})=(-1)^{n+1}\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D}_{N})\quad\in\;K_{n\,\mathrm{mod}\,2}\left(C^{*}_{u}(N)\right)\;.

Recall that if
M
is spin, then the uniform coarse index of its (spinor) Dirac operator obstructs the existence of bounded-geometry metric with uniformly positive scalar curvature in its uniform coarse equivalence class. In particular, if
M
admits such a metric, then the uniform partitioned manifold index
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{pm}(\not{D}_{M})
vanishes. The uniform partitioned-manifold index theorem therefore provides us an obstruction to the existence of such a metric that is based solely on the hypersurface
N
.

Corollary 6.56
.

Let
M
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional spin manifold of bounded geometry, and let
N\subseteq M
be a bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface such that
M
has product structure near
N
. If

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D}_{N})\neq 0\;\in\;K_{n\,\mathrm{mod}\,2}\left(C^{*}_{u}(N)\right)\;,

then
M
admits no bounded-geometry metric with uniformly positive scalar curvature in its uniform coarse equivalence class.

We can also carry out this discussion in the presence of a boundary. First, we need the definition of a partitioning hypersurface in this case. We find it easier to give the definition from the extrinsic viewpoint. Though it might be somewhat artificial, it is more convenient on a technical level.

Definition 6.57
.

Let
\Omega
be a domain in the manifold
M
of bounded geometry. Suppose that
N^{\prime}\subseteq M
is a bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface in
M
. Then,
N:=N^{\prime}\cap\overline{\Omega}
will be called a
bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface
in
\overline{\Omega}
.

Similarly, if
D
is a uniformly differential operator over
\overline{\Omega}
that has an extension to a uniformly elliptic differential operator over
M
that has product structure near
N^{\prime}
, we will say that
D
has
product structure
near
N
.

Given such a bounded-geometry hypersurface
N\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
the decomposition of
\overline{\Omega}
into the two sides of
N
gives rise to a Mayer-Vietoris sequence in relative uniform K-homology and one in K-theory of the relative uniform structure algebras. By Theorem
3.35
the relative uniform index maps intertwine these two sequences. In particular there is a diagram

\textstyle{K^{u}_{*+1}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{*}(N,\partial N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}}
\textstyle{K_{*+1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(N,\partial N)\right)}

Analogously to the boundaryless case we define the
relative uniform partitioned-manifold index map
as the composition

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\mathrm{rel,pm}}:=\delta\circ\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}:\;K^{u}_{*+1}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\longrightarrow K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(N,\partial N)\right)\;.

For its computation it is again sufficient to consider the Mayer-Vietoris boundary map on relative uniform K-homology. The latter map is computed by another boundary-of-Dirac-is-Dirac formula.

Proposition 6.58
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a domain in a bounded-geometry manifold, and let
N\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
be a bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface. Let
D_{\Omega}
be a
(p+1)
-multigraded uniformly elliptic operator over
\overline{\Omega}
that has product structure
D_{\Omega}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{N}
near
N
. Then,

\delta[D_{\Omega}]=(-1)^{p+1}[D_{N}]\;\in\;K^{u}_{-p}(N,\partial N)\;.

Proof.

The proof is a slightly more involved version of its boundaryless counterpart. Again, let
\Omega_{+}\subset\Omega
denote the that connected component of
\Omega\setminus N
with the property that if
\nu
is a normal vector field on
N
pointing into
\Omega_{+}
, and
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n})
is an oriented frame on
N
, then
(\nu,e_{1},\cdots,e_{n})
is an oriented frame on
\Omega
. Let
M_{+}\subseteq M\setminus N^{\prime}
denote the connected component containing
\Omega_{+}
. Consider the following diagram

\textstyle{K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-p}(N,\partial N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{!}}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\Omega_{+})}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\overline{\Omega}_{+}\setminus\partial\Omega,N\setminus\partial N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-p}(N\setminus\partial N)}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(M_{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{!}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\overline{M}_{+},N^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{-p}(N^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{!}}

The large top square commutes by definition of the Mayer-Vietoris boundary map. The bottom square commutes due to naturality of the excision and boundary maps. It suffices to calculate that the image of
[D_{\Omega}]\in K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\Omega)
under the middle row coincides with
(-1)^{p+1}[D_{N}]\in K^{u}_{-p}(N,\partial N)
.

To that end extend
D_{\Omega}
to a uniformly elliptic operator
D_{M}
over
M
that has product structure
D_{M}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{N^{\prime}}
near
N^{\prime}
. Note that
D_{N^{\prime}}=D_{N}
on
N
. Then,
i^{!}[D_{\Omega}]=i^{!}[D_{M}]\in K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\Omega_{+})
by Proposition
6.34
. Moreover, the combination of Theorem
6.39
and Proposition
6.34
computes the image of
[D_{M}]
under the composition

K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(M_{+})\xlongrightarrow{\sim}K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\overline{M}_{+},N^{\prime})\xlongrightarrow{\partial}K^{u}_{-p}(N^{\prime})\xlongrightarrow{i^{!}}K^{u}_{-p}(N\setminus\partial N)

to be the class
(-1)^{p+1}[D_{N}]\in K^{u}_{-p}(N\setminus\partial N)
. Commutativity of the diagram lets us conclude that the image of
[D_{\Omega}]\in K^{u}_{-(p+1)}(\Omega)
under the middle row is given by
(-1)^{p+1}[D_{N}]\in K^{u}_{-p}(N,\partial N)
as well, concluding the proof.
∎

The appeal to Theorem
3.35
then lets us deduce the relative uniform partitioned-manifold index theorem.

Theorem 6.59

(Relative uniform partitioned-manifold index theorem)
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a domain in a manifold of bounded geometry, and let
N\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
be a bounded-geometry partitioning hypersurface. Let
D_{\Omega}
be a
(p+1)
-multigraded uniformly elliptic operator over
\overline{\Omega}
that has product structure
D_{\Omega}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathbb{R}}\times D_{N}
near
N
. Then,

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\mathrm{rel,pm}}(D_{\Omega})=(-1)^{p+1}\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(D_{N})\;\in\;K_{-p}\left(D^{*}_{u}(N,\partial N)\right)\;.

The relative uniform indices are more subtle objects; besides a uniform coarse contribution from the interior they also have a metric contribution from the boundary. This complicates their interpretation. Say for example that
\overline{\Omega}
is a spin manifold with boundary and bounded geometry, whose metric has uniformly positive scalar curvature. Does
\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(\not{D}_{\Omega})
vanish? This question and related ones will be investigated in the next chapter. The answer turns out to be negative, but also more interesting. Nonetheless, the subtler nature of the relative uniform index forbids any simple corollary of the relative uniform partitioned-manifold index theorem as a upsc-obstructions.

Chapter 7
Relative uniform indices and scalar curvature

In this last chapter we return to the geometric application that provided a guiding theme for much of the introduction, namely metrics with (uniformly) positive scalar curvature. On spin manifolds the existence of such metrics is obstructed by the index of the spin Dirac operator.

Let
M
be a spin manifold that has uniformly positive scalar curvature outside a closed subset
Z\subseteq M
. There is a well-known localization principle (see for example
[
Roe16
]
) for coarse indices stating that in this case the Dirac operator admits a localized index
\mathrm{Ind}^{Z}(\not{D})\in K_{*}(C^{*}(Z\subseteq M))
that gets mapped to
\mathrm{Ind}(\not{D})\in K_{*}(C^{*}(M))
under the inclusion map
K_{*}(C^{*}(Z\subseteq M))\to K_{*}(C^{*}(M))
. In Section
27
we prove a uniform analogue of this localization principle that also works in the presence of boundaries. It states that if a spin manifold
\overline{\Omega}
with boundary and bounded geometry (or more generally a domain inside a spin manifold of bounded geometry) has uniformly positive scalar curvature outside a closed subset
Z\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
, then its relative uniform index refines to an index
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{Z}(\not{D})\in K_{*}(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega\cup Z\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega))
. Note that even though the scalar curvature might be uniformly positive near
\partial\Omega
, the localized index is still also supported near
\partial\Omega
. The reason is this: The localization principle is derived from the coercivity condition

\|\not{D}u\|_{L^{2}}\gtrsim\|u\|_{L^{2}}\quad\forall\,u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega\setminus Z;\not{S}(\overline{\Omega}))\;,

which is provided by the Lichnerowicz formula. This condition is not necessarily satisfied if the support of
u
intersects the boundary, due to the presence of boundary terms from Green’s formula. Therefore
\partial\Omega
also has to be treated as an area of non-coercivity. Since the localization principle for Dirac operators is the consequence of a coercivity statement, we prove it more generally for uniformly elliptic first-order operators whose minimal boundary condition is coercive with respect to a closed subspace. The focus on the minimal boundary condition is dictated by our wish to apply it to Dirac operators. On the other hand, the focus on first-order operators is a consequence of their favorable propagation properties that are not generally enjoyed by higher-order operators, see Section 5.5.

In Section
28
we compute the relative uniform index of the Dirac operators on spin manifolds under certain scalar curvature assumption. These indices turn out to be closely connected to uniform analogues of the higher
\rho
-invariants of the boundary. In particular, they are no obstructions to uniformly positive scalar curvature, but rather secondary measures of the geometry. Concretely, we will prove that if
\overline{\Omega}
is an even-dimensional spin manifold with boundary and bounded geometry, then the localized relative uniform index of
\not{D}_{\Omega}
coincides with the uniform higher
\rho
-invariant of the boundary, i.e.

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\partial\Omega}(\not{D}_{\Omega})=\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\partial\Omega})\;\in\;K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)\;.

If
\overline{\Omega}
is only assumed to have upsc on the boundary, then there is a correction term coming from the lack of upsc in the interior. This correction term is a uniform analogue of the APS-index of Piazza and Schick
[
PS14
]
. The formula for the relative uniform index then reads as

\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(\not{D}_{\Omega})=\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{APS}(\not{D}_{\Omega})+\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\partial\Omega})\;\in\;K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\;.

Modulo a sign convention this formula yields a uniform analogue of the delocalized APS-index theorem, that was proved by Piazza and Schick
[
PS14
]
in the even-dimensional case, and by Xie and Yu
[
XY14
]
in the general case. Indeed, by exactness the relative uniform index lies in the kernel of the inclusion map
K_{*}(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega))\to K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)
. Thus, our index formula implies that

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{APS}(\not{D}_{\Omega})=-\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\partial\Omega})\;\in\;K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)\;.

The non-uniform version of this formula is the delocalized APS-index theorem. We only prove our version in the even-dimensional case. The difficulties in odd dimensions are discussed below.

27
Coercivity and localized indices

As usual we let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry,
\Omega\subseteq M
a domain, and
D
as symmetric and uniformly elliptic operator over
\overline{\Omega}
. While
D
generally acts on sections of a vector bundle we do not make this explicit in the notation.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
Z\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
be a closed subset. A boundary condition
D_{e}
for
D
is said to be
coercive with respect to

Z
if there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\|D_{e}u\|\geq c\|u\|\quad\forall\;u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{e}),\,\mathrm{supp}(u)\cap Z=\emptyset\;.

If
Z
is empty, we will say that
D_{e}
is
coercive
. If there exists a compact
Z
such that
D_{e}
is coercive with respect to
Z
, we will say that
D_{e}
is
coercive at infinity
.

Example 7.2

(Dirac operators on manifolds with uniformly positive scalar curvature)
.

Suppose that
\overline{\Omega}
is a spin manifold with boundary and bounded geometry. The Lichnerowicz formula asserts that

\not{D}^{2}u=\nabla^{*}\nabla u+\frac{\mathrm{scal}}{4}u\quad\forall\,u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\overline{\Omega},\not{S}(\overline{\Omega}))\;.

(33)

If
\overline{\Omega}
has uniformly positive scalar curvature, i.e.
\mathrm{scal}\geq 4c>0
, then
\not{D}^{2}u\geq c\|u\|
for all
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\overline{\Omega},\not{S}(\overline{\Omega}))
. It follows that the minimal boundary condition
\not{D}_{\min}
is coercive. This generalizes to the case where upsc holds only away from a closed subset, i.e. if
\overline{\Omega}
has upsc outside a closed
Z\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
, then
\not{D}_{\min}
is coercive with respect to
Z
.

For sections that do not vanish on the boundary (
33
) is no longer valid due to the appearance of boundary terms from Green’s formula. Thus coercivity is generally not to be expected for any other boundary condition. It turns out that under the additional assumption that the mean curvature of the boundary is non-negative both the APS- and the chirality boundary conditions are coercive (see
[
GN14
, Section 6]
and
[
BB22
, Section 8]
).

Example 7.3

(Dirichlet Laplacian on finite-width manifolds)
.

The Dirichlet Laplacian on a manifold of finite width is coercive as a consequence of the Poincaré equality. In the case of compact domains this is classical, for manifolds with boundary and bounded geometry this was proved in much greater generality by Ammann, Große and Nistor in
[
AGN19
]
.

The main result of this section is a localization principle for first-order operators. It states that if the minimal boundary condition is coercive with respect to some
Z\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
, then there is a relative uniform index supported near
Z\cup\partial\Omega
.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
D
be a first-order uniformly elliptic differential operator. Suppose that
D_{\min}
is coercive with respect to a closed subset
Z\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
. Then, the class
[D]\in K_{*}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
has a representative that is invertible modulo
D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega\cup Z\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
. Consequently, it admits an index class

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{Z}(D)\;\in\;K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega\cup Z\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)

that is mapped to
\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(D)
under the inclusion map

K_{*}(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega\cup Z\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega))\to K_{*}(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega))\;.

We first prove a lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
D
be a first-order uniformly elliptic differential operator. Suppose that
D_{\min}
is coercive with respect to a closed subset
Z\subseteq\overline{\Omega}
, i.e. that

\|Du\|\geq c\|u\|\quad\forall\,u\in\tilde{H}^{1}(\Omega),\,\mathrm{supp}(u)\cap Z=\emptyset

for some
c>0
. Suppose
\varphi\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R})
has support inside
(-c,c)
. Then

\varphi(D_{e})\in C^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\Omega)

for any self-adjoint boundary condition
D_{e}
.

We draw attention to the fact that
\varphi(D_{e})
is only claimed to be uniformly locally compact over the interior, not up to the boundary.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of the corresponding non-uniform statement in the absence of boundaries, see for example
[
Roe16
, Lemma 2.3]
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
, and let
\psi\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\|\varphi-\psi\|<\varepsilon
and
\mathrm{supp}(\hat{\psi})\subseteq[-r,r]
for some
r\geq c>0
. Pick a Lipschitz function
g:\,\overline{\Omega}\to[0,1]
such that
g=1
on
N_{4c_{D}r}(\partial\Omega\cup Z)
and
g=0
outside
N_{5c_{D}r}(\partial\Omega\cup Z)
. Here
c_{D}
denotes the propagation speed of
D
. Then write

\varphi(D_{e})=g\psi(D_{e})g+(1-g)\psi(D_{e})g+\psi(D_{e})(1-g)+\left(\varphi(D_{e})-\psi(D_{e})\right)\;.

The first summand has finite propagation by Corollary
5.33
, is uniformly locally compact over
\Omega
,
82
82

82

Approximate
\psi
by functions
\psi^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{F}^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
. Then
\psi^{\prime}(D_{e})
is uniformly locally compact over
\Omega
by Proposition
6.3
and Corollary
6.8
. As the limit of uniformly locally compact operators,
\psi(D_{e})
is also uniformly locally compact.
and it is supported near
\partial\Omega\cup Z
. We will show that the remaining terms are
\lesssim\varepsilon
. This is clear for the last summand.

To treat the remaining summands we will show that if
\|\psi(D_{e})f\|\lesssim\|f\|\cdot\sup_{|x|\geq c}|\psi(x)|
whenever
f\in C_{cc}(\Omega)
is supported in distance at least
4c_{D}r
from
\partial\Omega\cup Z
. Since
1-g
has support of distance
\geq 4c_{D}r
from
\partial\Omega\cup Z
this will yield the claim, since

\sup_{|x|\geq r}|\psi(x)|\leq\varepsilon+\sup_{|x|\geq r}|\varphi(x)|=\varepsilon\;.

According to
[
Roe16
, Lemma 2.6]
it suffices to prove this for even functions
\psi
, and
d(\mathrm{supp}(f),\partial\Omega\cup Z)\geq 2c_{D}r
. Then, the proof of
[
Roe16
, Lemma 2.5]
applies verbatim. Indeed, letting
\Omega_{j}
denote the complement of
N_{jc_{D}r}(\partial\Omega\cup Z)
in
\Omega
,
j=1,2
, we consider the operator
D^{2}
on
L^{2}(\Omega_{1})
with domain
\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega_{1})
. Due to our coercivity assumption it is strictly positive, and thus has a self-adjoint Friedrichs extension
T=(D^{2})_{F}
satisfying
T\geq c^{2}I
. For
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega_{1})
it holds that
\sqrt{T}u=\pm Du
. In particular
\sqrt{T}
has the same propagation speed as
D
. Thus we can conclude that

\cos(t\sqrt{T})u=\cos(tD_{e})u\quad\forall\;u\in L^{2}(\Omega_{2}),\,|t|\leq r\;.

Using Fourier inversion and the assumption that
\psi
is even, we conclude that

\psi(D_{e})f=\int_{-r}^{r}\hat{\psi}(t)\cos(tD_{e})f\,dt=\int_{-r}^{r}\hat{\psi}(t)\cos(t\sqrt{T})f\,dt=\psi(\sqrt{T})f\;.

Appealing to the fact that
\sqrt{T}\geq c
concludes the proof.
∎

With this fact in hand we can proceed to prove Theorem
7.4
.

Proof of Thm.
7.4
.

We wish to apply Lemma
7.5
, but since
D
generally does not admit a self-adjoint extension that also defines an invertible element in
D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})/D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega};\partial\Omega)
we cannot apply it directly. Instead consider the operator
\hat{D}=\begin{pmatrix}0&D\\
D&0\end{pmatrix}
acting on sections of
E\oplus E
. It still satisfies
\|\hat{D}u\|\geq c\|u\|
for all
u\in\Gamma_{cc}(\Omega\setminus Z)
. Then, consider the self-adjoint extension
\hat{D}_{e}=\begin{pmatrix}0&D_{\max}\\
D_{\min}&0\end{pmatrix}
. Fix a normalizing function
\chi
so that
1-\chi^{2}
is supported inside
(-c,c)
. Then Lemma
7.5
yields that
\chi(\hat{D}_{e})
is invertible modulo
C^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\Omega;\hat{\rho})
. Here we chose to make explicit the representation
\hat{\rho}=\rho\oplus\rho
on
L^{2}(\Omega,E\oplus E)
. Let
P
denote the projection on
L^{2}(\Omega,E\oplus E)
onto
L^{2}(\Omega,E)
. Then, compression by
P
constitutes a map
83
83

83

This is because the inclusion
V:\,(L^{2}(\Omega,E),\rho)\hookrightarrow(L^{2}(\Omega,E\oplus E),\hat{\rho})
uniformly boundedly covers the identity on
\overline{\Omega}
, and
P
is given by
VV^{*}
.

T\mapsto PTP,\,C^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\Omega;\hat{\rho})\to C^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\Omega;\rho)\;.

We conclude that
I-P\chi(\hat{D}_{e})^{2}P\in C^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\Omega;\rho)
. On the other hand
(I-P)\chi(\hat{D}_{e})P
is a representative of
[D]\in K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
, so that

I-\left((I-P)\chi(\hat{D_{e}})P\right)^{*}\left((I-P)\chi(\hat{D_{e}})P\right)=I-P\chi(\hat{D_{e}})P\;\in\mathfrak{D}^{u}_{\rho}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\;.

We conclude that
I-P\chi(\hat{D}_{e})^{2}P
lies in

C^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\Omega)\cap\mathfrak{D}^{u}(\overline{\Omega};\partial\Omega)=D^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\;,

meaning that
[D]
has a representative that is invertible modulo
D^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
. Thus,
[D]\in K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
lies in the image of the map

K_{*+1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})/D^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\longrightarrow K_{*+1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})/D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\cong K_{*}^{u}(\overline{\Omega};\partial\Omega)

induced by inclusion. It fits into a commutative diagram

\textstyle{K_{*+1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})/D^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K_{*}^{u}(\overline{\Omega};\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}}
\textstyle{K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)}

where the bottom horizontal arrow is also induced by inclusion. Defining

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{Z}(D)\in K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)

as the image of

[(I-P)\chi(\hat{D}_{e})P]\in K_{*+1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})/D^{*}_{u}(Z\cup\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)

under the boundary map concludes the proof.
∎

If the subspace
Z
is compact, then being near
\partial\Omega\cup Z
is equivalent to being near
\partial\Omega
. Thus any boundary condition that is coercive at infinity has an index class in
K_{*}(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega))
. Recalling from Proposition
3.27
that the inclusion induces an isomorphism
K_{*}(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega))\cong K_{*}(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega))
if
\partial\Omega
is sufficiently regular, we obtain the following.

Corollary 7.6
.

Suppose that
D_{\min}
is coercive at infinity, and that
\Omega
is a bounded-geometry domain. Then,
D
admits an index class

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\partial\Omega}(D)\;\in\;K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)

that is mapped to
\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(D)
under the inclusion map
K_{*}(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega))\to K_{*}(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega))
. In this case

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(D)=0\;\in\;K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\;.

Proof.

The only part left to show is that
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(D)=0
in
K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)
. Recall from Section 3.5 that this uniform coarse index map factors as the composition

K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}}K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\longrightarrow K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)/D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega\right)=K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\;.

Since
\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(D)
is in the image of
D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\cong D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
, its image under the quotient map
K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\to K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)/D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega\subseteq\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega\right)
vanishes, and the claim follows.
∎

Recall from Example
7.2
above that the minimal boundary condition for the spinor Dirac operator is coercive with respect to
Z
if the scalar curvature is uniformly positive outside
Z
. Thus the uniform coarse index
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D})\in K_{*}(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega))
obstructs the existence of a upsc-metric.

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
\Omega
be a domain in a spin manifold of bounded geometry,, and suppose the scalar curvature on
\overline{\Omega}
is uniformly positive outside a compact subset of
\overline{\Omega}
. Then,

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D})=0\;\in\;K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\;.

Thus the uniform coarse index
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D})\in K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)
functions as an obstruction to the existence of a upsc-metric of bounded geometry in the uniform coarse equivalence class of
\overline{\Omega}
. However, we recall that
K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)=0
if
\overline{\Omega}
has finite width, so that this obstruction is only ever non-trivial if
\overline{\Omega}
has infinite width. Even in this case it seems to be not especially interesting, since all information near the boundary is forgotten. One might therefore hope that the relative uniform index of the Dirac operator provides a more interesting obstruction. Surprisingly, it turns out to be no obstruction at all, though it is interesting nonetheless.

28
Uniform higher
\rho
-invariants and the delocalized APS index theorem

Let
\Sigma
be an
n
-dimensional spin manifold of bounded geometry. If
\Sigma
carries a metric of uniformly positive scalar curvature, then
\not{D}_{\Sigma}
is invertible. Not only does its uniform coarse index
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma})\in K_{*}(C^{*}_{u}(\Sigma))
vanish, but
\chi(\not{D}_{\Sigma})
is an involution in
D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma)
whenever the normalizing function
\chi
is such that
1-\chi^{2}
is supported in
(-c,c)
for
c>0
such that
\not{D}^{2}_{\Sigma}\geq c^{2}
. Indeed, in this case
\chi(D_{e})
is self-adjoint and squares to the identity. Thus it defines a class

\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma}):=\left[\chi(\not{D}_{\Sigma})\right]\;\in\;K_{n+1}(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma))\;.

It is called the
uniform higher
\rho
-invariant
of the upsc-metric on
\Sigma
. The (non-uniform) higher
\rho
-invariant is an important tool in the study of the space of upsc-metrics on
\Sigma
, and it is to be expected that its uniform counterpart plays an analogous role. However, the primary use of the uniform higher
\rho
-invariants here is that they appear in index formulas for relative uniform indices.

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be an even-dimensional spin manifold with boundary and bounded geometry that carries a upsc metric having product form near the boundary. Then

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\partial\Omega}(\not{D}_{\Omega})=\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\partial\Omega})\;\in\;K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)\;.

Proof.

By use of a collar neighborhood it suffices to treat the case
\overline{\Omega}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma
, where
\Sigma
is an odd-dimensional spin manifold of bounded geometry. Then, the Dirac operator
\not{D}
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma
takes the form

\not{D}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\not{D}^{-}\\
\not{D}_{+}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\partial_{t}+\not{D}_{\Sigma}\\
\partial_{t}+\not{D}_{\Sigma}&0\end{pmatrix}\;.

We consider the boundary condition

\not{D}_{e}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\not{D}^{-}_{\min}\\
\not{D}_{\max}^{+}&0\end{pmatrix}\;.

By assumption of upsc there is
c>0
such that
\not{D}^{+}_{\max}\not{D}_{\min}^{-}\geq c^{2}
and
\not{D}_{\Sigma}^{2}\geq c^{2}
. Pick a normalizing function
\chi
such that
1-\chi^{2}
is supported inside
(-c,c)
. Write
\chi(\not{D}_{e})=\begin{pmatrix}0&U^{*}\\
U&0\end{pmatrix}
. Then,

\begin{pmatrix}U^{*}U&0\\
0&UU^{*}\end{pmatrix}=\chi^{2}(\not{D}_{e})=I-P_{\ker(\not{D}_{e})}=\begin{pmatrix}I-P_{\ker(\not{D}^{+}_{\max})}&0\\
0&I\end{pmatrix}\;.

We conclude that
U
is a partial isometry with kernel
\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})^{\perp}
and trivial cokernel. Moreover,
I-\chi(\not{D}_{e})^{2}\in D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma,\Sigma)
, meaning that the projection
P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}
is in
D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma,\Sigma)
. Now, recall that the map
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\Sigma}
is defined as

K_{1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma)/D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma,\Sigma)\right)\xlongrightarrow{\partial}K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma,\Sigma)\right)\cong K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma)\right)\;.

The operator
\chi(\not{D}_{e})
is first identified with the K-theory class

[U]\in K_{1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma)/D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma,\Sigma)\right)\;.

Its image under the boundary map is the class
[P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}]\in K_{0}(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma,\Sigma))
. The kernel of
\not{D}_{\max}^{+}=\left(\partial_{t}+\not{D}_{\Sigma}\right)_{\max}
consists of all elements of the form
u(t,\cdot)=e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}
, where
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\not{S}(\Sigma))
is in the image of the projection
P_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}(\not{D}_{\Sigma})
. Observe that
P_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(I+\chi(\not{D}_{\Sigma}))
represents the uniform higher
\rho
-invariant
\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma})
. In spirit this concludes the proof, as we have found
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\Sigma}(\not{D})
and
\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma})
to be projections onto subspaces that can be canonically identified. In practice we have to put in some legwork to show that this identification can be accomplished in a way that gives rise to an equality of K-theory classes.

Fix some small
\varepsilon>0
. Because
P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}\in D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma,\Sigma)
is the limit of operators supported near
\Sigma
, there exists some
R\geq 0
such that
\|P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}u\|<\varepsilon\|u\|
whenever
d(\mathrm{supp}(u),\Sigma)\geq R
. Then, let
P_{R}
be the projection onto the subspace containing all functions
u(t,\cdot)=\psi_{R}\cdot e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}
for
u_{0}\in\mathrm{im}(P_{0})
, where
\psi_{R}=\mathbf{1}_{[0,R]}(t)
. Then

\|(P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}-P^{R})u\|=\|P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}u\|<\varepsilon\|u\|

if
d(\mathrm{supp}(u),\Sigma)\geq R
. Suppose on the other hand that
\mathrm{supp}(u)\subseteq[0,R]\times\Sigma
. Write
u=\psi_{R}e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}+u^{\perp}
, where
u^{\perp}
is uniquely determined by the requirement that
\langle\psi_{R}e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}v_{0},u^{\perp}\rangle=0
for all
v_{0}\in\mathrm{im}(P_{0})
. Note that in particular
u^{\perp}=\psi_{R}u^{\perp}
, so that it follows that
\langle e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}v_{0},u^{\perp}\rangle=0
for all
v_{0}\in\mathrm{im}(P_{0})
as well. Thus

\displaystyle\left\|(P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}-P^{R})u\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|(P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}-I)\psi_{R}e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}-P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}\left((1-\psi_{R})e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}\right)-\psi_{R}e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}-P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}\left((1-\psi_{R})e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}\right)-\psi_{R}e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\left\|(1-\psi_{R})e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}\right\|\;.

It holds that

\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\int_{t=0}^{R}e^{-2t\lambda}\,dt\geq\int_{t=R}^{\infty}e^{-2t\lambda}\,dt

for all
\lambda\geq c
whenever
R
is sufficiently large, say
R\gtrsim\log(\varepsilon^{-2})c^{-1}
. Let
E_{\lambda}
denote the spectral measure of
\not{D}_{\Sigma}
(see Theorem
11.5
). Then,

\displaystyle\left\|(1-\psi_{R})e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{t=R}^{\infty}\left\|e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}\right\|^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{t=R}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\lambda=c}^{\infty}e^{-t\lambda}d\langle E_{\lambda}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle\right)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{\lambda=c}^{\infty}\left(\int_{t=R}^{\infty}e^{-t\lambda}\,dt\right)d\langle E_{\lambda}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\int_{\lambda=c}^{\infty}\left(\int_{t=0}^{R}e^{-t\lambda}\,dt\right)d\langle E_{\lambda}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\left\|\psi_{R}e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\|u\|^{2}\;.

The interchange of order of integration is justified by Tonelli’s theorem. Thus

\left\|(P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}-P^{R})u\right\|\leq\varepsilon\|u\|\;.

Overall, we conclude that
P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}
and
P_{R}
are projection whose difference has norm strictly less than
1
, so that they are homotopic (see for example
[
HR00
, Proposition 4.1.7]
).

Let
H_{R}=L^{2}([0,R]\times\Sigma)
, and denote by
V_{R}
inclusion map
H_{R}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma)
. Then
V_{R}
uniformly boundedly covers the inclusion
[0,R]\times\Sigma\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma
. Also consider
H_{\Sigma}=L^{2}(\Sigma)
. The isometry
V_{\Sigma}:\,H_{\Sigma}\to H_{R}
,
(V_{\Sigma}v)(t,x)=R^{-1/2}v(x)
uniformly covers the inclusion
\Sigma\hookrightarrow[0,R]\times\Sigma
. The conjugation maps corresponding to these isometries induce the isomorphisms

K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma)\right)\xlongrightarrow{\sim}K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}([0,R]\times\Sigma,\Sigma)\right)\xlongrightarrow{\sim}K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma,\Sigma\right)\;.

Now, observe that
P_{R}
is in the image of
\mathrm{Ad}(V_{R})
, namely it is the image of the projection onto the subspace of
H_{R}
containing all functions
u(t,\cdot)=e^{-t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}
,
u_{0}\in\mathrm{im}(P_{0})
,
t\in[0,R]
. Denote that projection by
P_{R}
as well. Now, there is a homotopy of projections
P_{R}^{s}
,
s\in[0,1]
, where
P_{R}^{s}
projects onto the subspace of
H_{R}
containing all functions
u(t,\cdot)=e^{-(1-s)t\not{D}_{\Sigma}}u_{0}
,
u_{0}\in\mathrm{im}(P_{0})
. Then,
P_{R}^{0}=P_{R}
, while
P_{R}^{1}=V_{\Sigma}P_{0}V^{*}_{\Sigma}
. Because
V_{\Sigma}
and
V_{R}
induce isomorphisms, we conclude that

[P_{\ker(\not{D}_{\max}^{+})}]=[P_{R}]=[P_{0}]\;\in\;K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma)\right)\cong K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\Sigma,\Sigma\right)\;.

Since we have already noted that the former projection represents
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\Sigma}(\not{D})
and the latter
\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma})
, this concludes the proof.
∎

We can derive a form of bordism invariance for the uniform higher
\rho
-invariant. Suppose that
\overline{\Omega}
is a spin manifold with boundary and bounded geometry that has uniformly positive scalar curvature and product structure near the boundary. Then Theorem
7.8
implies in particular that
\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\partial\Omega})
in in the image of the relative index map. Exactness of

K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}}K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\longrightarrow K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)

then implies the following.

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be an even-dimensional spin manifold with boundary and bounded geometry that has uniformly positive scalar curvature and product structure near the boundary. Then,

\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\partial\Omega})\in\ker\left(K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)\to K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)\right)\;.

Again, we interpret this statement as a form of bordism invariance: The uniform
\rho
-invariant of a upsc-null-bordant manifold vanishes upon being pushed forward to the bordism. It is admittedly unnatural that one first has to push forward to the bordism, though note that the uniform
\rho
-invariant itself can never vanish. The statement becomes more natural if one includes actions by some group
\Gamma
, and considers the push-forwards of the (uniform) higher
\rho
-invariants to
E\Gamma
. If one requires the upsc-null-bordism to extend the reference map of the boundary, then one obtains a genuine vanishing statement for the push-forward of the higher
\rho
-invariant of a upsc-null-bordant manifold. We refer to
[
PS14
]
for the non-uniform setting.

We move on to the case where we require
\overline{\Omega}
to have uniformly positive scalar curvature only on the boundary. In this case we can still consider the uniform higher
\rho
-invariant of the boundary. Due to the potential lack of upsc in the interior, we expect
\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(\not{D})
to have contributions from the interior. To get an idea of what to expect consider the following diagram

\textstyle{K^{u}_{*+1}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K^{u}_{*}(\partial\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ind}_{u}}
\textstyle{K_{*+1}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)\oplus K_{*+1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{*+1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)}
\textstyle{K_{*+1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)}

The upper-right square is the content of Theorem
3.37
, the horizontal row comes from the algebraic Mayer-Vietoris sequence of Theorem
3.30
, and the horizontal row from the exact sequence defining the relative uniform index map. The image of the class
[\not{D}_{\Omega}]\in K_{*+1}^{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
under the boundary map on uniform K-homology is the class
[\not{D}_{\partial\Omega}]\in K_{*}^{u}(\partial\Omega)
. Because
\partial\Omega
has upsc the uniform coarse index
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}([\not{D}_{\partial\Omega}])
vanishes. Thus

\delta\left(\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}([\not{D}_{\overline{\Omega}}])\right)=\mathrm{Ind}_{u}\left([\not{D}_{\partial\Omega}]\right)=0\;.

We conclude by exactness that the relative uniform index of
[\not{D}_{\overline{\Omega}}]
is the sum of an element of
K_{*+1}(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega))
and an element of
K_{*+1}(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega}))
. We have a good guess of what the former should be, namely the uniform
\rho
-invariant
\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\partial\Omega})
. The latter turns out to be a uniform analogue of the APS-index considered by Piazza and Schick
[
PS14
]
. It is defined as follows: Since we assume
\overline{\Omega}
to have product structure near the boundary, we may attach a cylinder (endowed with the product metric) to
\partial\Omega
to form the manifold
M=\overline{\Omega}\cup_{\partial\Omega}(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\times\partial\Omega)
of bounded geometry. If we assume upsc on
\partial\Omega
, then
M
has upsc outside the closed subspace
\overline{\Omega}\subseteq M
. By the localization principle the Dirac operator on
M
admits a localized index class

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{APS}(\not{D}_{\Omega}):=\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\overline{\Omega}}(\not{D}_{M})\;\in\;K_{n+1}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega}\subseteq M)\right)\cong K_{n+1}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)\;.

We call it the
uniform APS-index
. It provides the other component to the relative uniform index of
\not{D}_{\Omega}
.

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
\overline{\Omega}
be an even-dimensional spin manifold with boundary and bounded geometry. Assume that the metric on
\overline{\Omega}
has product structure near the boundary, and that the scalar curvature of
\partial\Omega
is uniformly positive. Then,

\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(\not{D}_{\Omega})=\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{APS}(\not{D}_{\Omega})+\rho(\not{D}_{\partial\Omega})\;\in\;K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\;.

Strictly speaking the right-hand side of the the index formula of Theorem
7.10
is given by the push-forwards of
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{APS}(\not{D}_{\Omega})
and
\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\partial\Omega})
under the respective inclusion maps

K_{*}\left(C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)\to K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\quad,\quad K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\partial\Omega)\right)\to K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\;.

To keep notation uncluttered we have kept these push-forward maps implicit, and we will do so in the following.

Proof.

Write
\Sigma:=\partial\Omega
, and
\Sigma_{\infty}:=\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\times\Sigma\subseteq M
. Let
\chi
be a normalizing function such that
1-\chi^{2}
is supported in
(-c,c)
, where
c>0
is such that
\not{D}_{\Sigma}^{2}\geq c^{2}
. Then,
\chi(\not{D}_{M})\in D^{*}_{u}(M)
is an involution modulo
C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega}\subseteq M)
. Let
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}
and
\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}
denote the characteristic functions of
\overline{\Omega}\subseteq M
and
\Sigma_{\infty}
respectively. Then,
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}
and
\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}
are mutually orthogonal projections summing to the identity.

We claim that
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}
are in
D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq M,\Sigma)
. We show this for the former, the latter is treated analogously. Let
\chi_{n}
be a sequence of normalizing functions with compactly supported Fourier transforms that converge to
\chi
. Then, each
\chi_{n}(\not{D}_{M})
has some finite propagation
R_{n}
, so that if
f\in C_{0}(\Sigma_{\infty})
and
g\in C_{0}(\overline{\Omega})
are each supported in distance
\geq R_{n}
from
\Sigma
, then
g\left(\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi_{n}(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\right)f=0
. Thus
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi_{n}(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}
is supported near
\Sigma
. Since
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}
and
\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}
are in
D^{*}_{u}(M)
, so is
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi_{n}(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. Thus it remains to prove that
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
over
M\setminus\Sigma
. To that end fix
L,R\geq 0
and
\delta>0
, and let
f\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(M)
have distance
\geq\delta
from
\Sigma
. Let
\chi^{\prime}
be a normalizing function so that
\chi^{\prime}(\not{D}_{M})
has propagation
<\delta
. Then,
\left(\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi^{\prime}(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\right)f=0
. On the other hand
\chi_{n}(\not{D}_{M})-\chi^{\prime}(\not{D}_{M})\in C^{*}_{u}(M)
.
84
84

84

By an approximation argument it suffices to consider the case
\chi_{n}-\chi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
. Then
(\chi_{n}(\not{D}_{M})-\chi^{\prime}(\not{D}_{M}))f\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
for
f\in C_{0}(M)
by Corollary
6.8
. Alternatively appeal to
[
Eng18a
, Section 7]
.
We conclude that

\left(\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi_{n}(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\right)f=\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\left(\chi_{n}(\not{D}_{M})-\chi^{\prime}(\not{D}_{M})\right)\left(\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}f\right)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0\;,

and
f\left(\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi_{n}(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\right)\sim_{\mathrm{ua}}0
is shown analogously. With the help of Lemma
4.20
we conclude that
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\in D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq M,\Sigma)
.

Thus, with respect to the decomposition into the images of
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}
and
\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}
we may write

\chi(\not{D}_{M})=\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}&0\\
0&\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\end{pmatrix}\;\mathrm{mod}\;D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq M,\Sigma)\;.

Since
\chi(\not{D}_{M})
is an involution modulo
C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega}\subseteq M)
, we deduce that
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\in D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})
is an involution modulo
D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\Sigma)
, and
\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\in D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma_{\infty})
one modulo
D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma_{\infty},\Sigma)
. Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{K_{1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})/D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\Sigma)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{\oplus}
\textstyle{K_{1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma_{\infty})/D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma_{\infty},\Sigma)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K_{1}\left(D^{*}_{u}(M)/D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega}\subseteq M,\Sigma)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\Sigma)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\oplus}
\textstyle{K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma_{\infty},\Sigma)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{0}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega}\subseteq M,\Sigma)\right)}

where the horizontal arrows are the sums of the respective inclusion maps. Note that the first vertical arrow is precisely the relative uniform index map. Inserting the above decomposition for
\chi(\not{D}_{M})
into this diagram lets us conclude that

\partial(\chi(\not{D}_{M}))=\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}\left(\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\right)+\partial\left(\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\right)\;,

Abusing notation we view
\chi(\not{D}_{M})
etc. as elements of the respective K-theory groups directly, whereas they should strictly speaking be identified with the corresponding unitary via Paschke duality first.

Now,
\partial(\chi(\not{D}_{M}))
coincides with the image of
\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{APS}(\not{D}_{\Omega})
under the inclusion of
C^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})
into
D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega}\subseteq M,\Sigma)
by definition of the uniform APS-index. Moreover,
\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}
gives rise to a uniform Fredholm module over
\Omega
that coincides up to a degenerate Fredholm module with the image of
\chi(\not{D}_{M})
under the restriction map
K^{u}_{0}(M)\to K^{u}_{0}(\Omega)
. Thus Proposition
6.34
yields
[\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}]=[\not{D}_{\Omega}]
in
K^{u}_{0}(\Omega)
. By excision this equality also holds in
K^{u}_{0}(\overline{\Omega},\Sigma)
, so that

\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}\left(\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\overline{\Omega}}\right)=\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(\not{D}_{\Omega})\;.

Analogously, it holds that
[\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}]=[\not{D}_{\Sigma_{\infty}}]\in K^{u}_{0}(\Sigma_{\infty},\Sigma)
. These two operators differ only by an operator in
D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma_{\infty},\Sigma)
, so that this equality actually holds in
K_{1}(D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma_{\infty})/D^{*}_{u}(\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma_{\infty},\Sigma))
. Thus we conclude from Theorem
7.8
that

\partial\left(\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\chi(\not{D}_{M})\psi_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\right)=\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{\Sigma}(\not{D}_{\Sigma_{\infty}})=-\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma})\;.

The sign comes from the fact that the attached cylinder
\Sigma_{\infty}=\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\times\Sigma
carries the opposite orientation of that considered in Theorem
7.8
. In combination we conclude that

\mathrm{Ind}_{u}^{APS}(\not{D}_{\Omega})=\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}(\not{D}_{\Omega})-\rho^{u}(\not{D}_{\Sigma})

in
K_{0}(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega}\subseteq M,\Sigma))
. Since this K-theory group is isomorphic to
K_{0}(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\Sigma))
by Proposition
3.27
, this concludes the proof.
∎

Again, we can appeal to exactness of

K^{u}_{*}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathrm{rel}}_{u}}K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega},\partial\Omega)\right)\longrightarrow K_{*}\left(D^{*}_{u}(\overline{\Omega})\right)
