is contained in the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
L^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R^{\prime}}(X)
.

We first give a sufficient condition for uniform resolvability in terms of the existence of certain open covers.

Lemma 13.2
.

Suppose there exists an open cover
\mathcal{U}
of
X\setminus Z
with the following properties:

(C1)

For all
R\geq 0
and
\delta>0
, there exists
M(R,\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all compact
K\subseteq X\setminus Z
with
\mathrm{diam}(K)\leq R
and
d(K,Z)\geq\delta
it holds that

\left|\left\{U\in\mathcal{U}\,|\,U\cap K\neq\emptyset\right\}\right|\leq M(R,\delta)\;.

(C2)

For all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
it holds that

\mathrm{diam}(U)\geq\varepsilon\;\Rightarrow\;d(U,Z)\geq\delta(\varepsilon)\;.

(C3)

For all
\delta>0
there exists
\lambda>0
such that for all
x\in X
,
d(x,Z)\geq\delta
there exists
U\in\mathcal{U}
with
B_{\lambda}(x)\subseteq U
.

Then
X
is uniformly resolvable near
Z
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i}
be an open cover of
X\setminus Z
satisfying (C1), (C2) and (C3). Define

\psi_{i}(x):=d(x,X\setminus U_{i})^{2}\bigg(\sum_{j}d(x,X\setminus U_{j})^{2}\bigg)^{-1}\;.

By construction
\psi_{i}^{-1}(0,1]\subseteq U_{i}
, so that points (2) and (3) of the definition of uniform resolvability follow from properties (C1) and (C2) respectively. Thus it remains to prove (1). To that end suppose that
d(U_{i},Z)\geq\delta
calculate that

|\sqrt{\psi_{i}}(x)-\sqrt{\psi_{i}}(y)|\leq L_{i}\cdot\left|d(x,X\setminus U_{i})-d(y,X\setminus U_{i})\right|\leq L_{i}\cdot d(x,y)

by the reverse triangle inequality, where
L_{i}:=\sup_{x\in U_{i}}\left(\sum_{j}d(x,X\setminus U_{j})^{2}\right)^{-1/2}
. By property (C3) there exists
\lambda>0
such that the
\lambda
-ball around any
x\in U_{i}
is contained in some
U_{j}
. It follows that
\sum_{j}d(x,X\setminus U_{j})^{2}\geq\lambda^{2}
, so that
L_{i}\leq\lambda^{-1}
.
∎

If
X
has jointly bounded geometry (Definition
2.13
) and
Z
is an arbitrary closed subspace, one can always produce such a cover by means of an iterative refinement process, starting with a cover from a quasi-lattice and successively refining ever closer to
Z
. This is done in the following proposition.

Proposition 13.3
.

Let
X
be a metric space of jointly bounded geometry, and
Z\subseteq X
an arbitrary closed subspace. Then there exists an open cover
\mathcal{U}
of
X\setminus Z
with properties (C1), (C2), (C3).

Consequently, a metric space of jointly bounded geometry is uniformly resolvable near any closed subspace.

Proof.

Fix a Borel decomposition
X=\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
and a compatible quasilattice
\Gamma\subseteq X
as in the definition of jointly bounded geometry. Let
c>0
be sufficiently large that the open
c
-balls around
\Gamma
cover
X
, and let
\mathcal{U}_{0}
be the open cover of
X\setminus Z
obtained by intersecting each
B_{c}(y)
,
y\in\Gamma
, with
X\setminus Z
. Because of jointly bounded geometry the cover
\mathcal{U}_{0}
has the property

(U)

For every
R\geq 0
there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that every compactum
K\subseteq X\setminus Z
,
\mathrm{diam}(K)\leq R
, intersects at most
N
elements of the open cover.

Fix a strictly decreasing null-sequence
(\delta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. We will construct a sequence of open covers
\mathcal{U}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, of
X\setminus Z
such that

•

\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
refines
\mathcal{U}_{n}
.

•

\mathcal{U}_{n}
satisfies (U).

•

If
U\in\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
satisfies
d(U,Z)\geq\delta_{n}
, then
U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
.

•

If
d(U,Z)<\delta_{n}
for
U\in\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
, then
\mathrm{diam}(U)\leq\delta_{n}
.

We have already constructed
\mathcal{U}_{0}
. Assume that the cover
\mathcal{U}_{n}
has already been constructed. Suppose
U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
satisfies
d(U,Z)<\delta_{n}
. Because
U_{n}
refines
\mathcal{U}_{0}
the set
U
is contained in a
c
-ball around an element of
\Gamma
. Thus there is a uniform bound on the number of
X_{i}
that
U
intersects. By assumption on
X_{i}
there exists a
\frac{\delta_{n+1}}{4}
-net
\Gamma_{n+1}^{i}
of uniformly bounded cardinality in each
X_{i}
. Thus there is a uniform bound on how many sets
B_{\delta_{n+1}}(y)\cap U
,
y\in\Gamma_{n+1}^{i}
,
i\in I
, are non-empty. Construct
\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
by replacing each
U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
with
d(U,Z)<\delta_{n}
by the collection of those
B_{\delta_{n+1}}(y)\cap U
,
y\in\Gamma_{n+1}^{i}
,
i\in I
, that are non-empty. Then
\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{U}_{n}
, and the third and fourth bullet point above hold by construction. Moreover, since
\mathcal{U}_{n}
satisfies (U) and every element of
\mathcal{U}_{n}
is replaced by a uniformly finite number of elements in
\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
the cover
\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
again satisfies (U).

Now define
\mathcal{U}
as the limit of
\mathcal{U}_{n}
: An open set
U\subseteq X\setminus Z
is in
\mathcal{U}
if and only if
d(U,Z)>0
and
U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
for one and hence any
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d(U,Z)\geq\delta_{n}
. To see that this is indeed an open cover of
X\setminus Z
, let
x
be a point with
d(x,Z)=\delta>0
and let
\delta_{n}
be less than
\delta
. There is an element
U\in\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
containing the point
x
. If
d(U,Z)\geq\delta_{n}
, then
U\in\mathcal{U}
by definition. If
d(U,Z)<\delta_{n}
, then
\mathrm{diam}(U)\leq\delta_{n}
. Since on the other hand
d(x,Z)>\delta_{n}
we conclude that
d(U,Z)\geq\delta_{m}>0
for some
m>n
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{m}
refines
\mathcal{U}_{n}
,
x
is contained in an element of
\mathcal{U}_{m}
, and that element has distance
\geq\delta_{m}
to
Z
. Thus it lies in
\mathcal{U}
. This concludes the argument that
\mathcal{U}
is an open cover of
X\setminus Z
.

We will prove that
\mathcal{U}
satisfies (C1), (C2) and (C3). For (C1) let
K
be a compactum in
X\setminus Z
with
\mathrm{diam}(K)\leq R
and
d(K,Z)\geq\delta
. Let
\delta_{n}
be strictly less than
\delta
. Because
\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
satisfies (U) the set
K
intersects at most
N_{n+1}(R)
elements of
\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
. Analogously to the argument above, there must be
m\geq n+1
such that each of these open sets has distance
\geq\delta_{m}
to
Z
. Additionally choose
m
such that
2\delta_{m}<\delta_{n}
. The compactum
K
intersects at most
N_{m}(R)
many elements of
\mathcal{U}_{m}
. Now, assume
U\in\mathcal{U}
intersects
K
. Either
d(U,Z)\geq\delta_{m}
, whence
U\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
, or
d(U,Z)<\delta_{m}
. In this case it must hold that
\mathrm{diam}(U)\leq\delta_{m}
. This would however yield

\delta_{n}\leq d(K,Z)\leq\mathrm{diam}(U)+d(U,Z)<2\delta_{m}\;,

a contradiction. Any element of
\mathcal{U}
intersecting
K
must therefore be contained in
\mathcal{U}_{m}
, and there can be at most
N_{m}(R)
such elements - a number depending only on
\mathcal{U}
,
R
and
\delta
, but not the specific
K
. This establishes (C1).

For (C2) suppose that
U\in\mathcal{U}
has diameter
\geq\varepsilon>0
. The set
U
lies in some
\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
, where we may take
n
such that
\delta_{n}\leq\varepsilon
. The properties of
\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
then yield that
d(U,Z)\geq\delta_{n}
.

Lastly, we establish (C3). Let
x\in X
have
d(x,Z)\geq\delta>0
. As a consequence of our argument for (C1) it holds that every
U\in\mathcal{U}
that contains
x
is an element of
\mathcal{U}_{m}
for a suitable
m
. Thus it suffices to prove that the covers
\mathcal{U}_{m}
has positive Lebesgue number. We will do this via induction. We defined
\mathcal{U}_{0}
as the collection of
c
-balls around the quasi-lattice
\Gamma
. After potentially increasing
c
we may assume that there is
0<c^{\prime}<c
such that the
c^{\prime}
-balls around
\Gamma
still cover
X
. Then, every
x
is in the
c^{\prime}
-ball of some
y\in\Gamma
, and the
\frac{c-c^{\prime}}{2}
-ball around
y
is contained in
B_{c}(y)\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
.

Now assume that
\mathcal{U}_{m}
has positive Lebesgue number. Recall that
\mathcal{U}_{m}
was constructed by replacing certain
U\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
with sets
B_{\delta_{m+1}}(y)\cap U
, where
y\in\Gamma^{i}_{m+1}
is a
\frac{\delta_{m+1}}{4}
-net in
X_{i}
. Take
x\in X_{i}
, and let
U\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
contain a
\lambda
-ball around
x
. If
U
is not changed upon passing to
\mathcal{U}_{m+1}
, there is nothing to show, so we suppose that it is. By construction
x
is within
\frac{\delta_{m+1}}{4}
of an element
y\in\Gamma_{m+1}^{i}
, so that the
\frac{\delta_{m+1}}{4}
-ball around
x
is contained in
B_{\delta_{m+1}/2}(y)
. Choosing
\lambda^{\prime}
smaller than both
\lambda
and
\frac{\delta_{m+1}}{4}
we conclude that
B_{\lambda^{\prime}}(x)\subseteq B_{\delta_{m+1}/2}(y)\cap U
. This proves (C3) and hence the proposition.
∎

Next we give a sufficient condition for transversal control in proper metric spaces in terms of the existence of
\varepsilon
-dense subsets of
Z
that are uniformly locally finite in
X
. The proof is an adaptation of the proof that surjections of unital function algebras admit (completely) positive splits found in
[
HR00
, Section 3.2]
.

Proposition 13.4
.

Let
X
be a proper metric space, and
Z\subseteq X
a closed subspace such that

(F)

For all
\varepsilon>0
there exists an
\varepsilon
-dense subset
90
90

90

Which is to say that
N_{\varepsilon}(\Gamma)=Z
.

\Gamma\subseteq Z
such that

\sup_{x\in X}\left|\left\{y\in\Gamma\,|\,d(x,y)<2\varepsilon\right\}\right|<\infty\;.

Then
X
is transversally controlled near
Z
.

Proof.

The cases
Z=\emptyset
and
Z=X
are trivial, we will therefore take
Z
to be a non-empty proper subset of
X
. We fix the following data: Let
\{\varepsilon_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a strictly decreasing null-sequence such that
2(\varepsilon_{n}+\varepsilon_{n+1})<2^{-n}
. Let
\Gamma_{n}=\{x^{(n)}_{i}\}_{i}
be an
\varepsilon_{n}
-dense subset of
Z
provided by (F). Consider the open cover
\mathcal{U}_{n}
of
X
given by the open
2\varepsilon_{n}
-ball in
X
centered on the elements of
\Gamma_{n}
, together with the complement of the closed
\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{2}
-neighborhood of
\Gamma_{n}
in
X
. The assumption (F) guarantees that every
\mathcal{U}_{n}
is a uniformly locally finite cover. Choose a subordinate partition of unity
\{\psi_{i}^{(n)}\}_{i}\cup\{\psi_{\infty}^{(n)}\}
such that each
\psi_{i}^{(n)}
is
l_{n}
-Lipschitz with uniform Lipschitz constant
l_{n}<\infty
. Here
\psi^{(n)}_{i}
is supported in
U_{2\varepsilon_{n}}(x^{(n)}_{i})
and
\psi_{\infty}^{(n)}
is supported in the complement of the
\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{2}
-neighborhood of
\Gamma_{n}
. Note that the restrictions of the
\{\psi^{(n)}_{i}\}
to
Z
form a partition of unity of
Z
. To see that the
\psi^{(n)}_{i}
can be chosen to be uniformly Lipschitz, it suffices to check that the cover
\mathcal{U}_{n}
has non-zero Lebesgue number, compare the proof of Lemma
13.2
above. To that end, let
x\in X
. Assume first that there is
y\in Z
with
d(x,y)<\frac{3\varepsilon_{n}}{4}
. Because
\Gamma_{n}
is
\varepsilon_{n}
-dense,
y
is contained in the
\varepsilon
-ball of some
y^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{n}
. In this case,
d(x,y^{\prime})<\frac{7\varepsilon_{n}}{4}
, so that the
\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{4}
-ball around
x
is contained in
U_{2\varepsilon_{n}}(y^{\prime})
. If on the other hand
d(x,Z)\geq\frac{3\varepsilon_{n}}{4}
, then the
\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{4}
-ball around
x
is contained in the complement of the closed
\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{2}
-neighborhood of
Z
. Hence
\mathcal{U}_{n}
has Lebesgue number at least
\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{4}
.

Define
\sigma_{n}^{\prime}:\,C_{0}^{+}(Z)\to C_{0}^{+}(X)
via
\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(\mathbf{1}_{Z}):=\mathbf{1}_{X}
and

\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g):=\sum_{i}g(x_{i}^{(n)})\cdot\psi^{(n)}_{i}

(36)

if
g\in C_{0}(Z)
. Note that
\sigma_{n}^{\prime}
is a positive and contractive map. If
g
has support of radius
R
, then
\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g)
has radius at most
R+4\cdot\varepsilon_{n}
. Moreover,
\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g)
is Lipschitz for any
g
:

\displaystyle\left|\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g)(x)-\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g)(y)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i}g(x_{n}^{i})\cdot\left(\psi^{(n)}_{i}(x)-\psi^{(n)}_{i}(y)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq||g||_{\infty}\cdot\sum_{i}\left|\psi^{(n)}_{i}(x)-\psi^{(n)}_{i}(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2l_{n}\cdot\mathrm{mult}(\mathcal{U}_{n})\cdot||g||_{\infty}\cdot d(x,y)\;.

In particular there exist
L_{n}^{\prime},R_{n}^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
\sigma^{\prime}_{n}
maps
L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(Z)
to
L^{\prime}_{n}\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R_{n}^{\prime}}(X)
, using also the
\psi^{(n)}_{i}
are compactly supported. We stress that this statement thus uses both the property (F) and the properness of
X
. Furthermore,
\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g)
converges to
g
on
Z
, and this convergence is uniform on
L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(Z)
for all
L,R
:

\displaystyle||\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g)|_{Z}-g||

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in Z}\left|\sum_{i}\left(g(x^{(n)}_{i})-g(x)\right)\cdot\psi^{(n)}_{i}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in Z}\sum_{i}L\cdot d(x,x^{(n)}_{i})\cdot\psi^{(n)}_{i}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 2L\cdot\varepsilon_{n}\cdot\sup_{x\in Z}\sum_{i}\psi^{(n)}_{i}(x)

\displaystyle=2L\cdot\varepsilon_{n}\xlongrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\;.

By construction
\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g)
is supported in the
2\varepsilon_{n}
-neighborhood of
Z
, so we cannot hope for convergence of
\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g)
to a continuous function outside of
Z
. To remedy that, pick Lipschitz functions
\varphi_{n}:\,X\to[0,1]
such that
\varphi_{n}=1
on
X\setminus N_{\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{2}}(Z)
and and
\varphi_{n}=0
on
N_{\frac{\varepsilon_{n+1}}{2}}(Z)
. Then, set
\sigma_{1}:=\sigma_{1}^{\prime}
and inductively define

\sigma_{n+1}:=(1-\varphi_{n+1})\cdot\sigma_{n+1}^{\prime}+\varphi_{n+1}\cdot\sigma_{n}\;.

(37)

Each
\sigma_{n}
is again positive and contractive, and
\sigma_{n}(\mathbf{1}_{Z})=\mathbf{1}_{X}
. Moreover, since
\varphi_{n+1}
is bounded and Lipschitz,
\sigma_{n}
maps
L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(Z)
to
\hat{L}_{n}\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{\hat{R}_{n}}(X)
for appropriate
\hat{L}_{n},\hat{R}_{n}\geq 0
. Since
\varphi_{n+1}=0
on
Z
it holds that
\sigma_{n}(g)|_{Z}=\sigma_{n+1}^{\prime}(g)|_{Z}
, whence
\sigma_{n}(g)|_{Z}\to g
and this convergence is uniform on each
L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(Z)
. We will now compute that
\sigma_{n}(g)
forms a Cauchy sequence whose rate of convergence is again uniform on each
L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(Z)
. Note first that
(1-\varphi_{n+1})\sigma_{n}=(1-\varphi_{n+1})\sigma_{n}^{\prime}
, following from the fact that
(1-\varphi_{n+1})\varphi_{n}=0
by construction. Then proceed as follows:

\displaystyle\left|\left|\sigma_{n+1}(g)-\sigma_{n}(g)\right|\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left|(1-\varphi_{n+1})\sigma_{n+1}^{\prime}(g)+\varphi_{n+1}\cdot\sigma_{n}(g)-\sigma_{n}(g)\right|\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left|(1-\varphi_{n+1})(\sigma_{n+1}^{\prime}(g)-\sigma_{n}(g))\right|\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left|(1-\varphi_{n+1})(\sigma_{n+1}^{\prime}(g)-\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g))\right|\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in N_{\frac{\varepsilon_{n+1}}{2}}(Z)}\left|\sigma_{n+1}^{\prime}(g)(x)-\sigma_{n}^{\prime}(g)(x)\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in N_{\frac{\varepsilon_{n+1}}{2}}(Z)}\left|\sum_{j}g(x^{(n+1)}_{j})\cdot\psi^{(n+1)}_{j}(x)-\sum_{i}g(x^{(n)}_{i})\cdot\psi^{(n)}_{i}(x)\right|

Now,
\{\psi^{(n)}_{i}\}_{i}
and
\{\psi^{n+1}_{j}\}_{j}
form a partition of unity on
N_{\frac{\varepsilon_{n+1}}{2}}(Z)
, as
\psi^{(n)}_{\infty}=0=\psi^{(n+1)}_{\infty}
there. Thus we compute further:

\displaystyle\left|\left|\sigma_{n+1}(g)-\sigma_{n}(g)\right|\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in N_{\frac{\varepsilon_{n+1}}{2}}(Z)}\left|\sum_{i,j}\left(g(x^{(n+1)}_{j})-g(x^{(n)}_{i})\right)\cdot\psi^{(n+1)}_{j}(x)\cdot\psi^{(n)}_{i}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in N_{\frac{\varepsilon_{n+1}}{2}}(Z)}\sum_{i,j}L\cdot\left(d(x^{(n+1)}_{j},x)+d(x,x^{(n)}_{i})\right)\cdot\psi^{(n+1)}_{j}(x)\cdot\psi^{(n)}_{i}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 2L(\varepsilon_{n+1}+\varepsilon_{n})\cdot\sup_{x\in N_{\varepsilon_{n+1}}(Z)}\sum_{i,j}\psi^{(n+1)}_{j}(x)\cdot\psi^{(n)}_{i}(x)

\displaystyle=2L(\varepsilon_{n+1}+\varepsilon_{n})<\frac{L}{2^{n}}\;.

It follows that
||\sigma_{n+m}(g)-\sigma_{n}(g)||\leq L\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}2^{-n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
. Since the union
\bigcup_{L,R}L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(Z)
is dense in the unit ball of
C_{0}(Z)
, and each
\sigma_{n}
is linear and contractive, it follows that
\sigma(g):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sigma_{n}(g)
exists for all
g\in C_{0}(Z)
. The resulting map
\sigma:\,C_{0}^{+}(Z)\to C_{0}^{+}(X)
is again positive and contractive. Since
\sigma_{n}(g)|_{Z}\to g
it holds that
\sigma(g)|_{Z}=g
. Lastly, for all
L,R\geq 0
and all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
||\sigma(g)-\sigma_{n}(g)||<\varepsilon
for all
g\in L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(Z)
, and by construction
\sigma_{n}
maps
L\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}_{R}(Z)
to
L^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{Lip}_{R^{\prime}}(X)
for appropriate
L^{\prime},R^{\prime}\geq 0
. This completes the construction.
∎

The next next lemma confirms that uniform resolvability and transversal control are indeed notions that depend only on the geometry near
Z
.

Lemma 13.5
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a pair, and
U\subseteq X
a neighborhood of
Z
such that
d(Z,X\setminus U)>\delta
. If
U
is uniformly resolvable resp. transversally controlled near
Z
, then so is
X
.

Proof.

Let
\delta:=d(Z,X\setminus U)
, and fix a Lipschitz map
\varphi:\,X\to[0,1]
such that
\sqrt{\varphi}
is also Lipschitz,
\varphi=1
on
N_{\delta/2}(Z)
and
\varphi=0
on
X\setminus U_{\delta}(Z)
. Let
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i}
be a partition of unity over
U\setminus Z
as in Definition
13.1
. Then
\{\psi_{i}\varphi\}_{i}\cup\{1-\varphi\}
is a partition of unity over
X\setminus Z
that also satisfies the requirements of Definition
13.1
. Moreover, suppose
\sigma:\,C_{0}^{+}(Z)\to C_{0}^{+}(U)
is an approximately filtered positive section. Then, the map
\sigma^{\prime}:\,C_{0}^{+}(Z)\to C_{0}^{+}(X)
defined by

\sigma^{\prime}(\lambda\mathbf{1}_{Z}+g):=\varphi\cdot\sigma(\lambda\mathbf{1}_{Z}+g)+\lambda\cdot(1-\varphi)

for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,
g\in C_{0}(Z)
, is also an approximately filtered positive section. This concludes the proof.
∎

We come to more concrete examples.

Lemma 13.6
.

Let
Z
be arbitrary, and consider
CZ=[0,1)\times Z
, equipped with the product metric. Then,
CZ
is transversally controlled near
Z\cong Z\times\{0\}
.

Proof.

Fix a Lipschitz function
\varphi:\,[0,1]\to[0,1]
with
\varphi(0)=1
and
\varphi(x)=0
for
x\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Then,
\sigma:\,C_{0}^{+}(Z)\to C_{0}^{+}(CZ)
defined by

\sigma(\mathbf{1}_{Z})=\mathbf{1}_{CZ}\;,\;\sigma(g)(t,x)=\varphi(t)\cdot g(x)

for
g\in C_{0}(Z)
has the desired properties.
∎

The argument of this lemma can be generalized. Instead of crossing with
[0,1)
one can instead cross with an arbitrary (non-empty) metric space
Y
. Embedding
Z
as
Z\cong\{y\}\times Z
for some
y\in Y
and taking
\varphi:\,Y\to[0,1]
a compactly supported Lipschitz function with
\varphi(y)=1
allows the same argument to prove that
Y\times Z
is transversally controlled near
Y\cong\{y\}\times Z
. We conclude that any metric space is transversally controlled near any subspace with uniformly thick tubular neighborhoods (interpreted suitably).

Corollary 13.7
.

Let
X
be any metric space, and
Z\subseteq X
a closed subspace. Suppose there exists an open neighborhood
U\subseteq X
of
Z
such that
d(Z,X\setminus U)>0
, and
U
is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to
Y\times Z
with product metric for some
Y
via a map that takes
Z\subseteq U
to
\{y\}\times Z\subseteq Y\times Z
for some
y\in Y
. Then
X
is transversally controlled near
Z
.

Let us conclude this appendix with the observation that every pair of interest in the body of this thesis has the properties of Definition
13.1
.

Corollary 13.8
.

Let
(X,Z)
be one of the following:

•

X
is a manifold of bounded geometry, and
Z
a bounded-geometry hypersurface.

•

X
is a simplicial complex of bounded geometry, and
Z
is a subcomplex.

•

Z
has jointly bounded geometry,
X=CZ=[0,1)\times Z
with the product metric, and
Z\cong Z\times\{0\}\subset CZ
.

Then
X
is uniformly resolvable and transversally controlled near
Z
.

Proof.

Manifolds of bounded geometry have jointly bounded geometry
[
Eng19
, Example 2.21]
, hence are uniformly resolvable near any closed subspace. Moreover, the metric on
X
is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to one that has product structure on a uniformly thick tubular neighborhood of
Z
(
[
Sch98a
, Prop. 7.3]
and
[
AGN19
, Theorem 2.10]
). By Lemma
6.20
(iv), this metric is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to the product metric on that tubular neighborhood. Hence, Corollary
13.7
implies that
X
is transversally controlled near
Z
.

Simplicial complexes of bounded geometry have jointly bounded geometry
[
Eng19
, Example 2.21]
, hence are uniformly resolvable near any closed subspace. Moreover, any subcomplex
Z
satisfies the property (F) of Proposition
13.4
. To see this, fix
\varepsilon>0
. Let
n
be the dimension of
X
. Inductively choose finite
\varepsilon
-dense subsets on the
k
-simplex,
k\leq n
, such that the intersection of this subset of the
k
-simplex with any face coincides with the chosen subset on the
(k-1)
-simplex. Then, define
\Gamma
to be the subset of
Z
that coincides with the chosen subset on any simplex in
Z
. Because
X
has bounded geometry, there is a uniform bound on how many elements of
\Gamma
can be within
2\varepsilon
of a given
x\in X
. Hence
Z\subseteq X
has the property (F). Since
X
is also proper, Proposition
13.4
gives the desired result.

Lastly, if
Z
has jointly bounded geometry, so does
CZ
. Indeed, if
\Gamma\subseteq Z
is a quasi-lattice in
Z
, it is also one in
CZ
. Moreover, if
\{X_{i}\}
is a countable Borel decomposition of
Z
as in Definition
2.13
, then
\{[0,1)\times X_{i}\}
is a decomposition of
CZ
with the analogous properties. Hence,
CZ
is uniformly resolvable near any closed subspace. It is transversally controlled near
Z
by Lemma
13.6
.
∎
